CAMRT Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (The CAMRT Code of Ethics, which sets the values guiding technologist conduct nationwide.): That code names honesty, respect for patient autonomy, and accountability as core principles for technologists across the country. The hospital act addresses facility operation and funding, not individual professional ethics. An employee handbook records local workplace rules that apply to one employer only. The Canada Health Act governs insured health services between governments and provinces, so it does not guide a technologist's personal conduct.
  2. The correct answer is D (The provincial regulatory college, which holds statutory power to regulate and discipline members.): Health professions are regulated provincially in Canada, so the college registers members, defines their scope, and handles complaints and discipline. The national office certifies competence through examination but has no statutory disciplinary power over practice. A radiology chief directs clinical work within one department without holding legal regulatory authority. A manufacturer sets equipment specifications, which have nothing to do with licensing or discipline.
  3. The correct answer is A (To demonstrate ongoing safe and current clinical practice to the regulator and the public.): Regulators require competency records so they can show the public that registered technologists keep their knowledge and skills up to date. Salary increases come from collective agreements, which are unrelated to regulatory quality assurance. Insurance premiums are set by actuarial risk and not by an individual's logged hours. Union membership is a labour matter, whereas the college program is a legal obligation tied to registration.
  4. The correct answer is C (Cultural safety, which centres the patient's experience and addresses power imbalances in care.): Cultural safety shifts the focus from the provider's knowledge to the patient's experience, and it asks providers to acknowledge historical and systemic power imbalances. Competency testing measures what staff know about a culture but does not judge whether patients feel respected. Standard precautions are infection control measures and have no link to discrimination. Diagnostic reasoning concerns interpreting clinical findings, not relationships with patients.
  5. The correct answer is D (Voluntary agreement by a capable patient, given freely and without any pressure.): Valid consent requires that the patient is capable, is informed, and agrees voluntarily without coercion or pressure. A family member cannot supply consent for a capable adult, so a witnessed family signature adds nothing. Approval from the referring physician confirms medical necessity but does not replace the patient's own agreement. A second technologist's confirmation is a workflow check and has no legal bearing on consent.
  6. The correct answer is B (Any identifiable data that links a patient to their health record or care.): Privacy legislation covers any information that identifies an individual and relates to their health, whatever its format or storage location. Limiting protection to paper charts ignores the electronic records that now dominate imaging departments. Images without a face still carry names, numbers, and dates that identify the patient. Billing codes are one small part of the record and are protected along with everything else.
  7. The correct answer is A (To support system-level analysis and prevent similar events from happening in the future.): Near-miss reports reveal weaknesses in processes, which lets the department fix them before a harmful event occurs. Using reports to assign blame discourages staff from reporting and weakens the safety culture. An annual review may mention reporting, but that is not the purpose of the report itself. Managing media attention is a communications task and does not drive the reporting system.
  8. The correct answer is C (Raising the safety concern on the patient's behalf with the supervising radiologist promptly.): Advocacy means speaking up for the patient's safety and wellbeing when the patient cannot or will not do so. Rushing through the study ignores a possible clinical problem. Deferring all decisions to the physician fails to pass on an observation that only the technologist has made at the bedside. Staying silent to preserve efficiency puts the schedule ahead of the person on the table.
  9. The correct answer is D (The range of activities the professional is legally authorized and competent to perform.): Scope of practice defines the procedures, duties, and decisions that the law and the regulator permit a profession to carry out. It is not tied to a hospital location, since technologists may work in many settings. It does not cap the number of patients seen in a shift, which is a workload matter. It also does not limit which employers can hire a technologist.
  10. The correct answer is A (Creating a reliable legal record that supports continuity of care between providers.): A complete record gives later providers the details of what was done and found, and it serves as the legal account of the care given. Calling it paperwork with no clinical value ignores how other clinicians rely on it for decisions. Productivity tracking is a secondary use at best. Written notes support verbal handover but cannot replace the conversation where questions are asked and answered.
  11. The correct answer is B (A partnership built on mutual respect and shared responsibility for patient outcomes.): Interprofessional collaboration treats each role as contributing distinct expertise toward the same patient goals. A strict hierarchy suppresses the technologist's observations, which are often critical to safety and image quality. An informal arrangement with no communication expectations invites errors and confusion. Restricting exchanges to written orders prevents the real-time conversation that complex procedures require.
  12. The correct answer is D (The national body provides certification, while the college regulates practice within its own province.): National certification confirms entry-level competence by examination, and provincial colleges hold the legal power to register, regulate, and discipline practising members. Reversing these roles misplaces who enforces provincial law. The two bodies do not duplicate each other, since certification and regulation are different functions. A college keeps its authority throughout a technologist's career in that province.
  13. The correct answer is C (To state the values and standards of conduct that every member is expected to uphold.): A code expresses the values, duties, and behaviours that define ethical practice, which guide judgment where no specific rule applies. Parking and dress rules are local employer policies. Wage rates belong to collective bargaining, not to an ethics document. A code complements provincial legislation and cannot replace the legal requirements that the law imposes.
  14. The correct answer is D (Pause and clarify her understanding of the study before continuing with the consent process.): Consent is valid only when the patient understands the procedure and agrees willingly, so confusion must be addressed first. A signed requisition shows a physician ordered the study but does not show the patient consents. Deferring questions until afterward removes the chance to decline. Recording hesitation and carrying on treats the concern as paperwork instead of resolving it.
  15. The correct answer is A (Address the concern directly with the colleague and report it if the behaviour continues.): Professional accountability includes speaking to a peer about unsafe practice and escalating if it persists, because patient identification protects every patient. Technologists share responsibility for correct identification, so it cannot be passed to physicians. Waiting for a harmful error defeats the purpose of preventing one. Gossip among coworkers does nothing to change the behaviour and damages trust.
  16. The correct answer is C (Decline the request and explain the scope of practice limitation to the physician.): Scope of practice is set by the regulator, so a physician's request cannot extend what a technologist is legally permitted to do. A colleague's sign-off has no power to change the limits of one's own authorization. Omitting the procedure from the record adds falsification to an unauthorized act. Declining politely and explaining the limit protects the patient and the technologist.
  17. The correct answer is B (Explain that image interpretation is outside the technologist's scope and the physician will discuss results.): Interpreting images and communicating diagnoses belong to the radiologist and the referring physician. Offering informal impressions risks false reassurance or alarm and exceeds the technologist's scope. Handing out images without authorization breaches privacy and the facility's release procedures. Removing the patient from the room disrespects the patient's rights over her own health information.
  18. The correct answer is A (Privately remind the coworker of the confidentiality duties for patient information.): A quiet, private reminder corrects the lapse promptly and respects the coworker while protecting patient confidentiality. Ignoring the discussion allows a privacy breach to continue. Contacting the patient is not the technologist's role and would be handled through the privacy officer if reporting is needed. Joining in would add to the disclosure of personal health information.
  19. The correct answer is C (Consult a supervisor and arrange more training or supervision before proceeding with the procedure.): Practising within one's competence is a core professional duty, and seeking supervision or training is how a new graduate extends it safely. Learning from mistakes on a live patient exposes that patient to avoidable risk. A porter has no qualifications to substitute for a technologist colleague. Hiding the uncertainty prevents anyone from supporting her and conceals a safety issue.
  20. The correct answer is B (Ask the patient privately which name and pronouns they prefer to be used.): Asking privately honours the patient's identity while still allowing the technologist to confirm identity with legal identifiers when required. Using only the legal name in public may out the patient and cause distress. Avoiding names altogether feels cold and does not solve the identification need. Copying what others used simply repeats a possible error without checking.
  21. The correct answer is A (The technologist documents the event and reports it according to facility policy.): Accountability means owning the error, recording the repeat accurately, and reporting it so dose tracking and learning can occur. Quietly redoing the image hides a dose event and removes a chance to improve practice. Waiting to see whether others notice shifts responsibility away from the technologist. Blaming equipment falsifies the record, which is a serious professional breach.
  22. The correct answer is D (Slow down, listen actively, and adjust the approach to build the patient's trust.): Cultural safety is judged by the patient's experience, so listening and adapting the pace directly address the concern she raised. Keeping the usual pace repeats the rushed experience she described. Saying everyone is treated the same dismisses her history and the power imbalance behind it. Passing the issue to administration avoids the immediate relationship with the patient.
  23. The correct answer is B (Decline until proper consent or legal authorization has been confirmed for the release.): Personal health information may be shared only with the patient's consent or under legal authority, whoever makes the request. A colleague's status does not create permission to disclose. Releasing only the images still discloses protected information. A waiver signed by the third party cannot stand in for the patient's own consent.
  24. The correct answer is C (Discuss the reasoning professionally with the radiologist before deciding whether to proceed.): Respectful, direct discussion lets both professionals share their reasoning, and ALARA means any extra dose needs a justification. Flat refusal damages collaboration and may ignore a valid clinical need. Silent compliance may expose the patient to unnecessary radiation. Passing the task along avoids the conversation and the responsibility for the dose.
  25. The correct answer is A (Explain that documented consent is still required before the procedure can go ahead.): Trust does not replace consent, which must be informed, voluntary, and documented according to policy. Proceeding without it exposes the patient to a procedure that has not been properly agreed. Signing for a capable patient is falsification. A witness signature without the patient's own agreement does not establish consent at all.
  26. The correct answer is D (Ask clarifying questions and check the record to learn the patient's status.): Continuity of care depends on an accurate picture of what was completed, what remains, and any exposures already given. Assuming completion could leave a needed study undone. Restarting without checking may repeat radiation exposure that was unnecessary. Relying on an anxious patient to describe the technical details is unreliable and shifts the responsibility onto the patient.
  27. The correct answer is B (A possible breach of the supervision standards set for students in training.): Students are permitted to perform clinical tasks only under the direct or indirect supervision defined by their program and the regulator. Feeling competent does not remove that requirement. Whether a complaint follows is irrelevant to whether a standard was broken. The issue concerns patient safety and regulation, not just equipment damage.
  28. The correct answer is C (Arrange for a qualified interpreter rather than relying on gestures or guesses alone.): Qualified interpreters give accurate, confidential communication, which is essential for safety and informed consent. Family members may omit or alter information, or may be unsuitable to hear private details. Simple English alone leaves too much to chance when instructions affect image quality and safety. Postponing indefinitely denies the patient timely care.
  29. The correct answer is B (Raise the concern with a supervisor to protect patient safety and support the colleague.): A fatigued worker can harm patients, and reporting to a supervisor lets the department adjust assignments before an error occurs. Fatigue is a recognized safety issue that managers must address. Covering silently hides the problem and may lead to burnout for both people. A public confrontation embarrasses the colleague and disrupts patient care.
  30. The correct answer is D (Refuse, because falsifying a record breaks documentation standards and honesty.): Records must reflect what actually happened, and altering a time to suit an administrative deadline is falsification. Billing needs never outrank truthful documentation. Adding a side note does not make a false entry true. Having another person make the change shares in the falsification and does not remove the technologist's responsibility.
  31. The correct answer is B (Give accurate, clear dose information within the technologist's scope and invite questions.): Patients have a right to clear information about their care, and technologists can share dose facts they know while referring complex risk questions to the radiologist. Refusing to discuss dose leaves the anxious patient uninformed. A manufacturer's help line cannot address a patient's personal concern. Dismissal ignores the worry and may damage trust in screening.
  32. The correct answer is C (Decline to sign, since an honest assessment requires direct observation of the work.): A competency sign-off is a statement that the signer has verified the skill, so signing without observation is dishonest. Personal trust is not evidence of competence. A private disclaimer does not change what the signed document claims. A secretary has no clinical basis to assess competence and cannot hold the responsibility.
  33. The correct answer is A (That appropriate patient consent or legal authorization has been obtained for the release.): Sharing is allowed only within the patient's circle of care or with consent or legal authority, so authorization must be confirmed. Training equivalence to a radiologist is not a condition for access. A verbal request is less verifiable than a written one. Providers cannot share freely, because privacy law applies to every disclosure.
  34. The correct answer is D (Contact the provincial college for official clarification of the new regulation.): The college wrote the regulation and is the authoritative source on how it applies to registrants. A coworker's opinion may be mistaken and does not protect her if it is wrong. Waiting for a patient complaint risks practising outside the rule in the meantime. Forums are unreliable and may reflect other jurisdictions.
  35. The correct answer is C (Listen supportively and connect the patient with appropriate resources or designated staff.): A supportive, private response builds trust and links the patient to social work or other trained supports while keeping her safe. Changing the subject tells the patient her disclosure does not matter. Confronting the partner could raise the risk of harm. A personal log breaches confidentiality and does nothing to help.
  36. The correct answer is B (Amend the record following the facility policy for documented corrections and note the reason.): Corrections must be traceable, which means the original entry stays visible and the amendment is attributed to the person making it. Deleting an entry erases the audit trail. Leaving a known error may mislead later clinicians reviewing the study. Using someone else's login falsifies who made the change.
  37. The correct answer is A (Decline and explain the regulatory limits that apply to the technologist's scope of practice.): Scope is defined by law and the regulator, so an employer cannot expand it, and the technologist is personally accountable. Obedience to a manager does not shield her from regulatory consequences. Acting in secret conceals a violation. A student has even less authorization and would be placed at greater risk.
  38. The correct answer is D (Explain that relevant information must be shared with the care team for safety.): Information needed for safe care is shared within the circle of care, and the technologist can reassure the patient about confidentiality while explaining why it matters. Promising secrecy could lead to a preventable reaction. Sharing with colleagues only still withholds it from the person making decisions. Refusing the study is a punitive step and ignores the chance to address her concern.
  39. The correct answer is B (Report the falsification through the facility's incident reporting process without delay.): A falsified quality control record hides equipment problems that can affect image quality and dose, so it must be reported through the formal process. Assuming no one will check is no excuse for silence. Quietly fixing the entry leaves the colleague's behaviour and the failed result unaddressed. Informal chat does not protect patients or trigger an investigation.
  40. The correct answer is C (Delay the examination until valid consent is obtained from a lawful decision-maker.): Without valid consent from the minor or a substitute decision-maker, the procedure would be an unauthorized intervention. A physician's referral confirms need but cannot supply consent. Security staff have no legal role as decision-makers. Documenting a lack of consent afterward does not undo the harm of acting without it.
  41. The correct answer is C (Respect for patient-centred communication, including introductions and clear explanations of care.): Introducing oneself and explaining the procedure are basic parts of patient-centred care and support informed participation. Dose limits concern radiation safety rather than communication. Maintenance scheduling is an equipment management matter. Resolution standards relate to image quality, which the coworker's lack of introductions does not directly affect.
  42. The correct answer is B (An objective account of the events and the factors that contributed to them.): Incident reports aim to record facts and contributing factors so the department can learn and prevent repeats. Opinions about fault are subjective and discourage honest reporting. Writing to limit liability distorts the account. A one-line note gives reviewers too little information to identify causes.
  43. The correct answer is A (Decline to release information without the patient's documented consent for the disclosure.): Health information belongs to the patient and may be released only with consent or legal authority, regardless of who pays. Paying for a service does not create a right of access. Redirecting to a supervisor without confirming consent still risks an improper release. Sharing the diagnosis alone discloses protected information.
  44. The correct answer is C (Raise the discrepancy with management and follow the current college standard meanwhile.): College standards carry regulatory authority, so practice must conform to the current version, and management must be told so the protocol can be updated. Tradition does not override a regulator. Majority preference makes patient care inconsistent and depends on opinion. Switching by radiologist produces unpredictable practice and does not resolve the conflict.
  45. The correct answer is D (Calmly restate the observation with evidence and ask that it be recorded.): Accountability means communicating a patient-related concern clearly and professionally until it is heard. Dropping it for harmony leaves a possible error unaddressed. Anonymous outside reporting bypasses the team that could fix it. Telling a friend outside the department breaches confidentiality and achieves nothing.
  46. The correct answer is B (Explore options with her that respect the belief and still meet diagnostic needs.): Collaboration respects the patient's autonomy and often finds a workable option, such as a different garment or private changing. Rigid insistence disregards cultural and religious rights. Cancelling without exploring delays necessary care. Proceeding secretly is deceptive and violates informed consent.
  47. The correct answer is D (Compile the evidence and escalate the pattern to leadership for a structural review.): System-level problems need system-level solutions, and leadership holds the authority to change staffing. Separate reports leave the connections hidden. Coaching individuals treats a structural issue as a personal failing. Waiting for improvement postpones action while patients remain at risk.
  48. The correct answer is B (Decline to co-sign and explain the specific concerns to the manager clearly.): A signature attests to verified competence, and signing against one's own judgment endangers patients and misleads the regulator. Staffing pressure does not change that. A promise of future training does not make a present sign-off honest. Passing the task to someone else avoids both the duty to be honest and the opportunity to explain concerns.
  49. The correct answer is C (Respect the request to stop and explain the clinical implications to the decision-maker.): Consent can be withdrawn at any time, and continuing against that wish would be an unauthorized act. The technologist's role is to stop safely and explain the consequences so the decision remains informed. Continuing because of image value puts the study ahead of the patient's rights. Security has no role in clinical consent decisions.
  50. The correct answer is A (Verify directly with the involved parties before proceeding with the examination.): Direct verification with the people involved prevents imaging the wrong area or missing needed views in a high-risk patient. Guessing between sources invites error. Resolving discrepancies afterward could mean unnecessary repeat radiation. A trauma patient may be in pain or confused and is not a reliable arbiter of conflicting records.
  51. The correct answer is B (Encourage her to tell the surgeon and relay the concern to the right team member.): The technologist acts as a patient advocate by encouraging open communication with the care team and by relaying the concern through proper channels. Quietly noting the symptom without telling anyone leaves the clinicians who plan her care unaware of relevant information. Dismissing the concern because the form is silent ignores the duty to advocate, and refusing to scan until she phones takes unilateral action that delays care and exceeds the technologist's role.
  52. The correct answer is D (Explain that interpretation is outside technologist scope and direct the nurse to the reporting radiologist.): Offering a diagnostic opinion falls outside the regulated scope of practice of a medical radiation technologist, however much experience the technologist has. Referring the nurse to the reporting radiologist keeps professional boundaries clear and protects patient safety. An informal impression or a comparison-based guess risks miscommunication and could undermine the formal report, while a bare refusal with no direction leaves the nurse without a path to the result.
  53. The correct answer is A (Speak privately with the colleague about the concern and encourage adherence to the quality control protocol.): A direct and collegial conversation is the proportionate first step, because it lets the colleague correct the behaviour before escalation is needed. Reporting to the college with no prior discussion skips this step. Rewriting the log hides a falsified record, and ignoring it wrongly assumes collimation errors do not matter, when poor collimation affects dose and image quality.
  54. The correct answer is C (Discuss the dose concern with the physician, seek clarification, and escalate per department policy if unresolved.): Open discussion respects the physician's clinical judgment while meeting the technologist's own responsibility for radiation protection under ALARA. Complying without question could give the patient unnecessary dose with no justification. Secretly altering factors is dishonest and undermines trust, and cancelling without any conversation denies the physician the chance to explain a valid reason.
  55. The correct answer is B (Explain that the radiologist interprets images and results will reach the patient through the proper channels.): Diagnostic interpretation belongs to the radiologist, even when a finding looks obvious on screen. Explaining this and describing how results will be delivered respects scope of practice while still showing care. Describing the mass or guessing it is harmless can distress the patient or give false reassurance before a formal report, and letting the patient draw conclusions from the image has the same effect.
  56. The correct answer is A (Missing repeat reasons will undermine efforts to correct recurring positioning and exposure errors.): A repeat analysis depends on recorded reasons, so missing entries prevent the program from spotting trends such as positioning or exposure faults and from targeting education or equipment fixes. Imaging systems record that an image was rejected but cannot supply the human reason in every case. Billing and result delivery are separate processes that this documentation gap does not directly affect.
  57. The correct answer is D (Decline and escalate the staffing gap to management, since practising outside competence is unsafe.): Practising beyond one's competence exposes the patient to avoidable risk, whatever the staffing pressure, so the technologist should decline and raise the gap with management. General radiography training does not cover fluoroscopic technique and radiation management. Telephone permission from a physician does not create competence, and a short video cannot replace supervised training and assessed skill.
  58. The correct answer is C (Decline, citing confidentiality obligations that apply regardless of the requester's profession or relationship.): Confidentiality duties apply to anyone not involved in the patient's care or authorized by the patient, whatever their clinical background. A verbal summary discloses protected information just as the images would. Asking the physician for permission also misses the point, because the authority to release information rests with the patient's consent or a legitimate care need, not a clinician's casual approval.
  59. The correct answer is B (Consult current evidence-based guidelines and departmental policy, and seek clarification from a senior colleague.): Continuing competency means actively seeking current evidence and local policy, and a senior colleague can supply practical context. Relying on habit assumes tradition equals best practice. Changing the protocol on personal judgment bypasses evidence and approval, and waiting months for a review leaves the uncertainty affecting patients in the meantime.
  60. The correct answer is D (Signing without direct verification misrepresents the record and violates professional accountability for accurate documentation.): A signature certifies what the signer actually witnessed or confirmed, so signing unverified work misstates the record and breaches professional accountability. Quality control logs support patient safety and equipment reliability, so they are not merely administrative. The ethical breach exists when the signature is made, regardless of whether harm follows, and a colleague's good reputation does not replace direct verification.
  61. The correct answer is A (Accommodate the request where feasible, keeping the healer outside the primary beam during exposure.): Culturally safe care respects a patient's spiritual and cultural practices whenever they can reasonably be accommodated without compromising the examination or radiation safety. Denying the request outright, or forcing a choice between culture and care, creates a barrier and disrespects the patient. Postponing the discussion until after imaging ignores the request when it matters most.
  62. The correct answer is B (Discuss the clinical rationale with the radiologist before deciding whether to proceed.): A respectful conversation lets the technologist learn the radiologist's reasoning, which may involve subtle findings the technologist did not notice, while also voicing the dose concern. Repeating silently forfeits that learning and any chance to avoid an unneeded exposure. Refusing without discussion and handing the task to someone else both avoid the collaboration that good professional practice requires.
  63. The correct answer is C (Listen to her concerns and relay the relevant information to the appropriate care team member.): Advocacy within scope means listening and making sure the concern reaches the right member of the care team. Telling her the team is right dismisses her experience, and diagnosing from the images oversteps the technologist's scope. Avoiding the topic is also wrong, because attentive listening and communication are part of professional patient care.
  64. The correct answer is A (Verify the discrepancy with the ordering physician or appropriate staff before proceeding with any exposure.): Confirming the information before any exposure is a basic safety check that prevents an inappropriate or unnecessary examination. Questioning a suspected discrepancy is part of the technologist's role in patient safety. Cancelling silently leaves the error uncorrected, and substituting an examination on personal judgment goes beyond the authority of the technologist to change an order.
  65. The correct answer is D (Ensure the patient's safety first, then document and report the incident per facility policy.): Patient safety always comes first, followed promptly by formal documentation and reporting through the established incident process. Delaying the report to the next shift or relying on informal talk fails the facility's reporting requirements. Skipping documentation because no harm is visible is also wrong, since delayed injuries can appear and reports help the facility prevent similar events.
  66. The correct answer is B (Raise the pattern with department leadership to establish a standardized process for complete requisitions.): A recurring system problem is best solved at department level, where leadership can create a lasting process for complete requisitions. Performing examinations without history can affect protocol choice and the radiologist's interpretation. Refusing each requisition one at a time never fixes the pattern, and inventing history introduces unverified information into the patient's record.
  67. The correct answer is C (Delay the examination until a qualified interpreter is arranged, in keeping with informed consent standards.): Valid informed consent requires real understanding of the procedure, risks and alternatives, which gestures or simple English cannot reliably provide. A family member is not trained to interpret medical content accurately and may filter or alter it. Asking for a signature before an explanation defeats the purpose of consent entirely.
  68. The correct answer is A (Address the comment directly with the colleague and remind them of confidentiality duties.): Speaking directly to the colleague addresses a breach of both respect and confidentiality, since naming a patient in a public space exposes private information. Ignoring the remark allows unprofessional behaviour to continue, and laughing along signals approval. Anonymous reporting without a direct conversation skips a simple first step that often resolves the issue.
  69. The correct answer is D (Notify a supervisor of the staffing shortfall before proceeding, as required by facility safety policy.): Staffing policies for mobile units protect patients and staff, so deviations must be reported to a supervisor who can find a solution. A porter lacks the qualifications to fill a regulated role. Proceeding as if the policy did not exist ignores a safety rule, and cancelling everything without discussion harms patient care and skips the communication needed to fix the problem.
  70. The correct answer is B (Raise the concern through appropriate channels, since practising unregistered is a legal issue.): Practising without current registration is a serious professional and legal matter affecting patient safety and public trust, so the concern should go to a supervisor or the regulatory college. Registration is not a private matter. A public confrontation is unprofessional and unfair if the posted certificate is merely old, and doing nothing leaves a possible breach unchecked.
  71. The correct answer is A (Full name together with date of birth, checked against the requisition and wristband.): Two independent identifiers such as full name and date of birth are the recognized minimum for confirming identity. Room numbers and diagnoses change or are shared between patients, so they cannot confirm who is on the table. A referring physician's name does not identify the patient at all, and a first name with gender is far too common to distinguish patients.
  72. The correct answer is D (Before an aseptic task, performed immediately prior to accessing the sterile vascular site.): Hand hygiene must be performed immediately before an aseptic task such as catheter insertion, to avoid introducing organisms into a sterile site. The other moments occur after contact with the patient, the surroundings or body fluids, and protect the technologist and the environment instead. They do not address the specific need to protect the vascular access site before the procedure begins.
  73. The correct answer is B (To give the technologist a secure handhold that improves control and reduces fall risk during transfer.): A gait belt gives the technologist a stable grip around the patient's trunk, which improves control and lowers the risk of a fall during the transfer. It is a safety handling aid, not an immobilization device, so it does not restrict movement during imaging or fix the arms to the table. It is also not a measuring tool for choosing equipment sizes.
  74. The correct answer is C (Hives, itching, and a sensation of throat tightness developing within minutes.): Hives, itching and throat tightness are classic early signs of an allergic or anaphylactic reaction to contrast and demand prompt recognition and response. Bradycardia with warm dry skin and slow deep breathing are not typical early anaphylaxis findings, since anaphylaxis tends to cause a fast heart rate and laboured breathing. Fatigue over hours does not match the acute onset expected within minutes.
  75. The correct answer is A (Call for help and begin the facility's emergency response protocol immediately, starting basic life support.): An unresponsive patient who is not breathing is a medical emergency, so the technologist should call for help and activate the emergency response protocol while starting basic life support as trained. Continuing the exam or waiting for a physician delays critical intervention. Moving the patient before assessment wastes time and could worsen injury.
  76. The correct answer is D (Patients of childbearing potential, who are asked before abdominal or pelvic examinations.): Protocol requires asking patients of childbearing potential, because radiation to an early, unrecognized pregnancy carries potential risk. Relying on patients to volunteer the information misses early pregnancies they may not know about. Limiting the question to obstetric referrals leaves out most of the patients at risk, and screening every patient regardless of potential adds no protection.
  77. The correct answer is B (Airborne precautions, using a fit-tested N95 respirator and a negative-pressure room where available.): Tuberculosis spreads through tiny airborne particles that stay suspended in air, so airborne precautions with a fit-tested N95 respirator are required. Contact precautions alone do not protect the respiratory tract. A surgical mask and droplet precautions are designed for larger particles and do not filter the fine aerosols that carry the organism.
  78. The correct answer is C (Use a commercial immobilization device such as a Pigg-O-Stat to minimize the need for holding.): Commercial devices reduce motion while avoiding unnecessary radiation exposure to staff or family who would otherwise hold the child in the beam. An unprotected parent in the primary beam is never acceptable. Holding the child with bare hands exposes the technologist, and routine sedation carries medical risk that is not justified for a short, simple examination.
  79. The correct answer is A (Orthostatic hypotension or vasovagal syncope, usually relieved by sitting or lying down again.): Lightheadedness and sweating on standing after lying down are classic features of orthostatic hypotension or a vasovagal response, which respond to position change and monitoring. Sitting or lying the patient down again and watching symptoms is the usual first response. A myocardial infarction would usually bring chest pain, hyperglycemia would bring thirst and frequent urination, and anaphylaxis would bring hives or wheezing, none of which are present.
  80. The correct answer is C (Keep the mobility aid within reach and assist with transfers as needed.): Keeping the walker within reach and helping with transfers directly lowers fall risk for a patient who cannot move safely alone. Leaving the patient unattended or telling them to walk unaided raises the chance of a fall. Taking away the walker removes the very support the patient needs to move and makes a fall more likely.
  81. The correct answer is B (Baseline practices applied to every patient regardless of diagnosis, since infection status is often unknown.): Standard precautions are the baseline infection control practices, including hand hygiene and personal protective equipment as indicated, applied consistently to every patient because infectious status is often unknown. Restricting them to confirmed cases or to surgical settings leaves routine encounters unprotected, and gloves never replace hand hygiene since hands can be contaminated during glove removal. This baseline approach protects both patients and staff.
  82. The correct answer is C (Substitute latex-free supplies and inform the healthcare team of the documented latex allergy.): Using latex-free supplies and telling the team prevents accidental exposure and supports coordinated, safe care. Waiting indefinitely denies the patient care that is easily available through substitution, and giving a medication without an order is outside the technologist's scope.

Withholding information increases uncertainty, which is a main driver of anxiety.
  160. The correct answer is B (Support the injured limb throughout and coordinate the move with enough staff.): A fractured limb needs continuous support, and a coordinated team move keeps the spine and limb aligned. Moving fast with an unsupported limb allows the fracture ends to shift and increases pain. Weight bearing on a suspected fracture can displace it. A solo transfer of an injured patient raises the risk to both the patient and the technologist.
  161. The correct answer is C (Notify the radiologist and requesting physician of the allergy before any contrast is given.): A prior severe reaction is a major risk factor for another one, so the radiologist and requesting physician must decide whether to proceed, premedicate or choose another study before any contrast is given. Slowing the injection does not prevent an allergic-type reaction. A different brand is still iodinated contrast and the choice is not the technologist's alone. The facility where it happened does not make the history less credible.
  162. The correct answer is D (Moving the patient before clearance could turn an unstable injury into permanent neurological damage.): An unstable cervical injury can cause permanent cord damage if the patient is moved before clearance, and a slightly imperfect image can be tolerated or supplemented. Image quality never outranks preventing irreversible harm. Radiolucency of a board says nothing about whether movement is safe. Spinal precautions begin at the scene and continue until clearance, not after a bedside examination.
  163. The correct answer is A (Hold the examination and consult the radiologist, since new pain may signal a complication.): New abdominal pain during bowel preparation could indicate obstruction, perforation risk or another complication, and a retained stool burden also degrades a barium study. The radiologist must weigh the risk before any contrast enters the colon. Proceeding assumes the pain is harmless. Repeating instructions and starting ignores a possible emergency, and cancelling silently leaves the referring physician unaware of a possible complication.
  164. The correct answer is B (Stop the examination, alert nursing or medical staff immediately, and stay with the patient.): A continuous alarm with a falling saturation signals possible deterioration, so the patient's safety comes before the image. The technologist should stop, summon qualified staff and stay present. Continuing for workflow reasons or finishing the exposure first delays help for a possibly deteriorating patient. Silencing the alarm removes the only warning the monitor is providing.
  165. The correct answer is D (Pause, use calm redirection, and consider inviting the daughter in to reduce her distress.): Least restrictive measures come first: pausing, calm redirection and a familiar person often settle an agitated patient with dementia. Full restraints without trying alternatives violate the principle of least restraint. Rushing ignores the cause of the distress and raises fall risk. Having a relative hold the patient still in the beam exposes that person to radiation and does not address the agitation.
  166. The correct answer is C (Lower the table, keep side rails engaged, and do not leave the patient unattended.): A drowsy, high-risk patient needs a low surface, engaged rails or supports, and continuous observation. Verbal instructions cannot be followed reliably by a sedated person, and a high table raises the fall height. Leaving the patient alone assumes the sedation has worn off when it has not. Sitting upright does not clear a drug and may cause dizziness or a fall.
  167. The correct answer is A (Tell the radiologist the symptoms immediately and be ready to stop the study.): Chest tightness with breathlessness can signal a contrast reaction, aspiration or a cardiac event, so the radiologist must be told at once and the study paused if needed. Chest tightness is not an expected routine finding. Waiting for the sequence to end delays recognition of a possible emergency. Coaching breathing while withholding the symptoms hides information the physician needs.
  168. The correct answer is D (Use a cross-table lateral that leaves the injured limb undisturbed, because patient safety comes first.): With a possible hip fracture, forced rotation can displace the fracture, so the technologist adapts the projection to avoid moving the limb. A cross-table lateral gives the needed orthogonal view without manipulation. Pushing through pain risks serious harm. Cancelling the study denies a needed diagnosis when a safe alternative exists, and asking the patient to rotate the limb does not remove the risk or the technologist's responsibility.
  169. The correct answer is B (It may signal a wristband, requisition or registration error, so identity needs a second check.): A name mismatch may mean a registration error, a wrong chart or a different patient, so the technologist must stop and verify identity from an additional source before any exposure. Treating it as clerical risks imaging the wrong patient. Trusting the registration system or the spoken name alone ignores that either can be wrong, and neither settles the question without a second check.
  170. The correct answer is C (Latex contact can trigger a systemic allergic reaction, so confirming latex-free equipment prevents an avoidable event.): Latex can provoke systemic reactions, including anaphylaxis, so latex-containing items must be identified and replaced before contact. Cuffs, straps, tourniquets and gloves can all contain latex, so equipment is not automatically safe. The allergy is not limited to skin irritation, and it is not limited to injections.
  171. The correct answer is A (Document the findings factually and report through the child protection process without confronting the family.): Health professionals have a duty to report suspected child abuse, and the technologist should record objective observations and use the facility's reporting pathway. Silence on scope grounds fails that duty. Confronting a parent can endanger the child and compromise an investigation. Waiting for a later question from the referring physician allows continued risk to the infant.
  172. The correct answer is D (Confirm the rated capacity exceeds the weight, or arrange bariatric-rated equipment first.): Equipment must be rated above the patient's weight with a safety margin, and bariatric-rated devices should be arranged if it is not. A capacity that is merely close risks mechanical failure and a patient fall. Manual lifting puts staff at risk of injury. Delaying with no plan neglects the patient's need for the examination.
  173. The correct answer is B (Listen with empathy, avoid speculating about the diagnosis, and connect her with appropriate support resources.): Technologists support patients by listening, acknowledging feelings and directing them to the right people, while leaving diagnosis to the physician. Personal opinions on results exceed the scope and may be wrong. False reassurance can be inaccurate and dismissive. Emotional support is part of patient-centred care, so ending the conversation abruptly neglects her needs.
  174. The correct answer is C (Mask the patient if tolerated, notify the receiving department, and take the most direct route.): Droplet precautions continue during transport, so the patient should be masked if tolerated, the receiving area should prepare, and the direct route limits contacts. Precautions do not end at the unit door. Lifting isolation temporarily exposes others to the pathogen. Postponing until precautions end ignores clinical need when safe transport is possible.
  175. The correct answer is D (Hold movement-based positioning, promptly notify nursing or medical staff, and avoid massaging the limb.): Possible deep vein thrombosis needs prompt medical assessment, and manipulating or massaging the limb could dislodge a clot that then travels to the lungs. Dismissing the pain as routine postoperative discomfort misses the warning. Massage and walking both risk clot movement. Notifying staff and avoiding stressful positioning is the safe response.
  176. The correct answer is A (Confirm terminal cleaning is complete first, since ending isolation does not remove environmental contamination.): Pathogens can persist on surfaces after isolation ends, so the room must have a terminal clean before another patient uses it. Discontinuing an order says nothing about the state of the environment. A patient's sense of wellness is not a measure of contamination. Daily cleaning is less thorough than the terminal process required after an outbreak.
  177. The correct answer is B (She can continue, using distance and shielding while keeping fetal dose within occupational limits.): A declared pregnant worker may keep working provided the dose to the fetus stays within regulatory limits, using distance, shielding and ALARA practice. A blanket refusal is not required for low dose work. Scatter at close range to a portable exposure is not negligible. Waiting for paperwork before applying protection leaves her exposed in the meantime.
  178. The correct answer is C (Worsening confusion may signal neurological deterioration, so pause and alert the treating team.): Worsening confusion after head trauma can mean expanding intracranial bleeding, which is time-sensitive. The team needs to know right away so the patient can be reassessed. Treating the change as expected misses a possible emergency. Silent restraint hides a clinical change from the people who must act, and documenting after discharge comes too late for intervention.
  179. The correct answer is D (Clear hazards, cushion the head, call for medical help, and time the episode.): During a seizure the priority is preventing injury: clear hazards, cushion the head, summon help and time the event. Restraining the limbs can cause fractures or strains. Objects in the mouth can break teeth or obstruct the airway. Leaving to fetch paperwork abandons a patient who may be injured or stop breathing.
  180. The correct answer is A (Secure all tubing before moving, since dislodging a drain or line causes harm besides falls.): Lines and drains can be pulled out during a move, causing bleeding, loss of access or injury, so each must be secured and tracked before movement. Falls are not the only hazard. Removing an intravenous line is outside the technologist's role and costs the patient access. Patients cannot be relied on to manage tubing during a transfer.
  181. The correct answer is B (Impaired mobility, a cane and a drug causing dizziness on standing together compound fall risk.): Fall risk is multifactorial. Impaired mobility, a mobility aid and a drug that lowers blood pressure on standing each add risk, and together they place the patient in a high-risk group. Medication effects are part of the assessment. A single factor rarely decides the category, and age alone is a poor predictor compared with the combination.
  182. The correct answer is D (Use pillows or table adjustments for a modified position that spares the incision yet stays diagnostic.): Positioning can be adapted with supports and small adjustments that protect the surgical site while still meeting the diagnostic requirement. Rigidly demanding the flat position ignores patient safety and comfort. Cancelling because of pain is unnecessary when adaptation is possible. Technologists do not administer pain medication, which is outside their scope.
  183. The correct answer is C (Reconnect the oxygen at once, confirm flow is restored, and monitor the patient.): Restoring the patient's oxygen supply is an immediate safety action that any trained staff member can perform, followed by checking that flow is present and watching the saturation recover. The disconnection will not fix itself. Silencing the alarm hides a real problem, and waiting for rounds leaves a hypoxic patient unsupported.
  184. The correct answer is A (Follow facility policy to involve a substitute decision-maker or legally authorized representative in the consent process.): Valid consent requires capacity, so when capacity is in doubt the facility's process for a substitute decision-maker must be followed. A fleeting yes from a person who cannot retain information is not informed consent. Skipping the discussion removes the patient's rights, and a signature from someone whose authority is unverified has no legal standing.
  185. The correct answer is D (Review the documented triggers, explain the procedure in calm, concrete language, and consider a support person.): Past reactions are a useful guide, so the technologist should plan around the known triggers, explain what to expect and offer supports such as a companion. Ignoring the history invites the same episode. Hiding the enclosed nature of the scanner removes the patient's chance to prepare and erodes trust. Sedation without consultation bypasses consent and carries its own risks.
  186. The correct answer is B (Locate a sling rated for the patient's actual weight before proceeding with the lift transfer.): using a sling rated below the patient's actual weight risks equipment failure during the lift, which could cause a serious fall, so obtaining correctly rated equipment first is the safest course. Using the mismatched sling anyway, switching to an unassessed manual transfer, or estimating weight informally all introduce avoidable risk.
  187. The correct answer is C (Stop positioning, call staff trained in tracheostomy care, and watch the airway closely.): Airway distress in a tracheostomy patient may mean a blocked tube and needs trained help at once, while the technologist stays and watches. Finishing the examination first delays rescue. Untrained suctioning can injure the airway. Reassurance alone does not clear an obstruction, and waiting can allow oxygen levels to drop dangerously.
  188. The correct answer is A (Reporting near misses lets the facility identify contributing factors and fix processes before a real injury.): Near-miss reports reveal weaknesses in equipment, staffing or technique so the facility can fix them before someone is hurt. Waiting for an injury wastes the free warning. A just reporting culture focuses on systems rather than blame. Verbal-only discussion leaves no record for trend analysis or follow-up.
  189. The correct answer is B (Autonomic dysreflexia, so remove the trigger if possible and alert medical staff urgently.): Headache, flushing and sudden severe hypertension in a patient with a high spinal cord injury suggest autonomic dysreflexia, a medical emergency that can cause stroke. The trigger, such as a full bladder or tight positioning, should be removed and medical staff called urgently. Anxiety, positional effects and dehydration do not explain the pattern and none of them warrants waiting.
  190. The correct answer is D (Close the anteroom door at once, since an open door defeats the room's negative pressure.): Negative pressure rooms work only when doors stay closed, so an open anteroom door lets contaminated air escape into the corridor. It should be closed at once. The door clearly affects containment. Propping it further worsens the breach, and waiting until after the examination leaves other people exposed in the meantime.
  191. The correct answer is C (Consult guidelines for the specific agent, since breastfeeding advice varies with its half-life.): Radiopharmaceuticals pass into breast milk to different degrees, so advice on interruption depends on the agent and its half-life and should come from established guidelines and the nuclear medicine physician. Breastfeeding status does matter. Permanent cessation is rarely needed, and one fixed interval for each agent is incorrect.
  192. The correct answer is A (Raise the rails and stay near, since a sedated patient could fall at any moment.): A sedated patient cannot protect himself, so raising the rails and staying close prevents an imminent fall. Raising rails on a stretcher for safety is standard and is not a restraint. Assuming someone else will act leaves the patient unattended. Waiting for sedation to wear off leaves a long window of risk.
  193. The correct answer is D (The nurse can monitor status and lines continuously, spotting deterioration beyond the technologist's scope.): An unstable postoperative patient needs ongoing clinical assessment and line management that fall outside the technologist's scope, so the nurse should stay and watch for deterioration. Technologist training does not include independent monitoring of unstable patients. The nurse's role is clinical, not clerical, and removing the nurse removes the only trained observer.
  194. The correct answer is B (Position him supine or semi-reclined and watch for early signs like pallor.): A lying or semi-reclined position keeps blood flow to the brain and lowers the chance of fainting, and early signs such as pallor or sweating give time to react. Standing makes fainting more likely and more dangerous. A past episode is a good predictor, so ignoring it is unwise. Indefinite delay does not help the patient.
  195. The correct answer is C (Identify and use an alternative antiseptic that does not contain chlorhexidine before any skin preparation.): A documented allergen must be avoided completely, so a different antiseptic should be chosen according to facility policy. Chlorhexidine can cause serious reactions, including anaphylaxis in rare cases. A smaller amount still exposes the patient and a reaction can be dose independent. Having the patient apply it changes nothing about the risk.
  196. The correct answer is A (Keep the system below chest level so fluid and air cannot return to the pleural space.): The drainage unit must stay below the level of the chest so gravity keeps fluid and air moving out of the pleural space and not back in. Height does affect function. Clamping can trap air and cause a tension pneumothorax. Disconnecting breaks the closed system and risks air entering the pleural space.
  197. The correct answer is D (Use the son only for urgent needs, and request an interpreter for clinical content or consent.): A family member can help in a pinch with simple, urgent instructions, but clinical information and consent need a professional interpreter to ensure accuracy and privacy. Relying entirely on the son risks errors and omissions. Gestures alone cannot convey a complex sequence. Indefinite postponement delays care when an interim solution exists.
  198. The correct answer is B (Tell the requesting physician, since these signs may indicate an undocumented coagulation risk.): Jaundice with easy bruising can reflect liver disease and impaired clotting, which matters before any invasive injection. The physician needs this information beforehand to decide whether to proceed. Technologist observations are valuable clinical data. Documenting afterward is too late, and assuming others already know is how important findings get missed.
  199. The correct answer is C (Raising the head of the table reduces venous return and eases his breathing.): Lying flat increases venous return to a failing heart and fluid shifts into the lungs, worsening orthopnea. Raising the head of the table or sitting him up relieves this and the examination can often be done upright. Ignoring the symptoms is unsafe. Prone positioning would worsen breathing for most heart failure patients.
  200. The correct answer is A (It lets the team review events, find system gaps, and support staff wellbeing.): Structured debriefing is a learning and support process: the team reviews what happened, finds communication or system problems and checks on the emotional impact on staff. Its purpose is improvement, not blame. It does give measurable benefits for later responses. Delaying until details fade removes the very information that makes the review useful.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Verify identity with a second identifier such as date of birth and reconcile the records.): A mismatch between the wristband and the requisition must be resolved with a second identifier such as date of birth, and the source of the error corrected through proper channels. Answering to a called name is not a reliable identifier. A relative is not a substitute for system checks, and editing the requisition to fit the patient's statement hides the discrepancy.
  2. The correct answer is D (Perform the examination with a portable unit at the bedside using appropriate personal protective equipment.): An unstable patient should not be moved, and a portable bedside examination with contact precautions, gown and gloves, followed by soap-and-water hand hygiene and cleaning of the equipment, meets both clinical and infection control needs. Moving the patient under standard precautions spreads spores and risks his stability. Cancelling ignores clinical urgency.
  3. The correct answer is A (Use a slide board or mechanical lift with enough staff and locked brakes.): A patient who cannot bear weight and may have a hip fracture must be moved with equipment and enough hands so the injured limb is never loaded. A slide board or lift keeps the whole body supported, and braked surfaces stop the stretcher or table from drifting during the move. Standing and pivoting risks a fall and fracture displacement. A trapeze pull or gravity slide gives no control over the injured leg.
  4. The correct answer is C (Stop the injection, call the emergency team, and begin assessing the patient's airway and breathing.): Hives, wheezing and unresponsiveness after contrast indicate anaphylaxis, which can progress to airway closure and cardiac arrest within minutes. Stopping the injection removes further allergen, and activating the emergency team brings drugs and airway skills quickly. Finishing the scan or waiting to observe delays treatment when every minute counts. Documentation comes after the patient is stabilized.
  5. The correct answer is B (Follow the department pregnancy screening protocol, such as a questionnaire or radiologist consultation.): When menstrual history is unreliable, the department's pregnancy screening protocol decides the next step, often a questionnaire and a discussion with the radiologist about risk and benefit. The technologist does not diagnose pregnancy, so waiting for a blood test or guessing is not required. Refusing outright is unnecessary, and reducing factors only degrades the image without addressing fetal exposure.
  6. The correct answer is A (Give simple age-appropriate explanations, let the parent stay if possible, and use distraction techniques.): Young children cooperate best when they hear short, honest explanations and keep a trusted caregiver close. Distraction with toys or conversation lowers fear and motion blur, which also prevents repeat exposures. Separating the child from the parent usually raises distress. Rushing without explanation frightens the child further, and postponing delays a needed diagnosis.
  7. The correct answer is D (Perform hand hygiene right after removing the gloves, even though gloves looked intact.): Gloves can have tiny unseen defects, and hands are contaminated while the gloves are peeled off. Hand hygiene is therefore required immediately after glove removal and before touching the next surface or patient. Waiting for visible soiling misses invisible organisms. Putting on new gloves over unclean hands only transfers contamination inside the glove.
  8. The correct answer is C (Use the least restrictive method that works, tell the parent why, and stay with the child.): Immobilization should be only as firm as needed for a diagnostic image, with the parent informed and involved. A child who is restrained must be watched at all times because of injury and fall risk. Excess restraint adds fear and potential harm. Repeating exposures without controlling motion adds dose without benefit and breaks the ALARA principle.
  9. The correct answer is B (Call for extra staff or obtain a mechanical lift before attempting the transfer.): A patient who cannot bear weight needs more support than one person can safely provide. Extra staff or a lift protects the patient from a fall and the technologist from a back injury, which good body mechanics alone cannot prevent. Asking the patient to assist is unreliable, and a single-person drawsheet slide still loads the technologist heavily.
  10. The correct answer is D (Stop the examination, check the vital signs, and activate the emergency response system according to protocol.): Chest pain with sweating can signal a heart attack, so the examination must stop and the patient must be assessed at once. Taking vital signs and activating the emergency system gets trained help to the room quickly. Continuing the study or waiting delays care. Extended questioning alone wastes time, and giving water adds an aspiration risk.
  11. The correct answer is A (A surgical mask, adding eye protection when splash or spray is possible during close contact.): Droplet precautions call for a surgical mask within close range of the patient, with eye protection when spray or splash is likely. N95 respirators are reserved for airborne pathogens such as tuberculosis. Gloves alone do not stop droplets reaching the nose and mouth, and standard precautions alone are insufficient for a known droplet infection.
  12. The correct answer is C (Confirm two identifiers, such as full name and birth date, against the requisition each time.): Shared surnames raise the chance of mixing up patients, so two independent identifiers must be matched to the requisition for each person. Arrival order, a surname alone or appearance can all be wrong. A wrong-patient exposure delivers dose to someone who does not need the study and delays the right diagnosis.
  13. The correct answer is D (Let the patient sit up slowly, pause, and assist during the move to standing.): Orthostatic hypotension means blood pressure drops when posture changes quickly, causing dizziness or fainting. Moving through sitting to standing in stages, with a pause and hands-on support, gives circulation time to adjust. Rapid standing or a fast table tilt invites a fall, and being alert does not prevent a sudden blood pressure drop.
  14. The correct answer is B (Acknowledge her feelings, listen with empathy, and direct questions about findings to the physician.): Patients value being heard, so the technologist responds with empathy and active listening. Interpreting images and discussing diagnosis belong to the radiologist or referring physician, so those questions are redirected. False reassurance may prove untrue, and reviewing old findings oversteps scope. Ignoring her distress leaves her feeling dismissed.
  15. The correct answer is C (Consult the radiologist or ordering physician about deferring the non-urgent study.): For a non-urgent study, a newly known pregnancy should prompt a conversation with the radiologist or ordering physician about delaying until after delivery. They weigh benefit against fetal risk, and the decision is documented. Proceeding without discussion or ignoring fetal dose breaks ALARA, and cancelling silently leaves the physician unaware of why the study did not happen.
  16. The correct answer is A (Use bariatric-rated equipment and enough trained staff to perform a safe transfer.): Equipment has a rated weight limit, and exceeding it risks collapse, table damage and injury. Bariatric-rated tables, lifts and enough trained staff keep the patient and staff safe during the transfer. Holding up an overloaded table is unsafe, and unassisted transfer risks a fall. Checking ratings only after imaging begins invites the very hazard being avoided.
  17. The correct answer is B (Protect without restraining, turn the patient to one side, and call for help.): During a seizure the goals are to prevent injury, keep the airway clear and get help. Clearing hazards and turning the patient onto one side lets saliva drain, and nothing should force the limbs still. Objects in the mouth can break teeth or block the airway, and leaving a seizing patient alone risks a fall from the table.
  18. The correct answer is D (Disinfect all touched surfaces and equipment per the facility infection control protocol.): MRSA spreads by contact with contaminated surfaces, so everything the patient or technologist touched, including the table, detector, positioning aids and handles, must be disinfected. A dry wipe does not kill organisms, airing the room does nothing to surfaces, and limiting cleaning to visible contact misses touched areas. Following protocol protects the next patient.
  19. The correct answer is C (Cross-check identity with the wristband and chart, not the confused patient's statement.): A confused patient's verbal answers cannot be trusted as the only identifier. The technologist verifies using objective sources such as the wristband, chart and requisition, and resolves any mismatch before exposure. Starting first risks a wrong-patient study, and coaching the patient into matching the paperwork defeats the purpose of identification.
  20. The correct answer is A (Accommodate the request by arranging a female technologist when it is reasonably possible.): Respecting cultural and religious preferences is part of culturally safe, patient-centred care, and a same-gender provider should be arranged when staffing allows. A rigid refusal or pressure to change the preference damages trust and may cause the patient to avoid care. Here a suitable technologist is available, so there is no barrier.
  21. The correct answer is D (Sievert, the SI unit of effective dose that includes radiation and tissue weighting factors.): The sievert is the SI unit of effective and equivalent dose because it applies weighting factors for radiation type and tissue sensitivity. The gray measures absorbed energy per mass with no weighting. Coulomb per kilogram is the SI unit of air exposure, and the roentgen is an older non-SI unit of exposure, so neither expresses effective dose.
  22. The correct answer is B (Keep exposure as low as reasonably achievable while still obtaining images that answer the clinical question.): ALARA means dose is reduced as far as is reasonable, balancing economic and social factors, while the image remains diagnostic. Eliminating exposure is impossible when imaging is needed, and nondiagnostic images force repeats that raise dose. A single fixed limit ignores the different doses that different examinations legitimately require.
  23. The correct answer is A (50 millisieverts in one calendar year, with a separate five-year limit of 100.): Canadian regulations set the effective dose limit for a nuclear energy worker at 50 millisieverts in a calendar year and 100 millisieverts over a five-year dosimetry period. The 500 millisievert figure is the skin and extremity limit. A single-year limit of 100 would defeat the five-year ceiling, and 5 millisieverts is far lower than the regulated annual limit.
  24. The correct answer is C (At collar level outside the lead apron, near the unprotected thyroid region.): A single dosimeter worn at the collar outside the apron records the dose to unprotected areas such as the head, neck and thyroid, which is the conservative estimate of exposure. Under the apron it would under-report dose to unshielded tissue. An ankle badge misrepresents body dose, and a badge on the unit measures the equipment, not the worker.
  25. The correct answer is D (It falls to one quarter of the original intensity because distance is squared.): The inverse square law states that intensity is inversely proportional to the square of the distance. Doubling distance gives two squared, or four, so intensity drops to one quarter. A one half change would apply to a simple inverse relationship, and one eighth would need a cubic one. Distance is one of the most effective protection tools.
  26. The correct answer is C (0.5 millimetre of lead equivalent, giving substantial scatter attenuation while remaining wearable all day.): A minimum of 0.5 millimetre lead equivalent is the standard for aprons in diagnostic radiography because it attenuates much of the scatter at those energies while remaining practical. Thinner aprons provide too little protection, and thicker ones are heavy and unnecessary for most diagnostic work, leading to fatigue and poor use.
  27. The correct answer is B (Annually, or on the schedule set by the facility's quality assurance program.): kVp accuracy is normally verified annually or per the quality assurance program, which catches slow drift in generator calibration. Testing only at installation would miss gradual change, and testing before every patient is impractical and unnecessary. A five-year interval is too long to detect problems that alter image quality and patient dose.
  28. The correct answer is A (4 millisieverts for the remainder of the pregnancy once the pregnancy has been formally declared.): After a formal declaration, Canadian regulations limit the dose to the embryo or fetus to 4 millisieverts for the remainder of the pregnancy, much lower than the ordinary worker limit. The 50 millisievert figure is the ordinary annual limit and would not protect the fetus. A monthly allowance with no total would permit a higher cumulative dose.
  29. The correct answer is D (Probability of occurrence rises with dose, no threshold exists, and severity does not depend on dose.): Stochastic effects, such as cancer and hereditary changes, are modelled with no threshold: any dose carries some chance, and the chance increases with dose while severity does not. Threshold-dependent effects with dose-related severity are deterministic. Stochastic effects are not reversible, and they can appear in germ cells passed to offspring.
  30. The correct answer is B (It reduces the volume of tissue irradiated and lowers scatter reaching the patient and staff.): Tight collimation limits the beam to the needed area, so less tissue receives dose and less scatter is produced. Less scatter also improves image contrast and lowers staff exposure. Collimation does not replace personal monitoring, and it does not determine kVp, which is chosen from part thickness and the examination.
  31. The correct answer is C (A threshold dose must be exceeded, and severity then increases as the dose becomes greater.): Deterministic effects, such as erythema or cataract, appear only above a threshold dose, and once that threshold is passed the severity climbs with dose. Stochastic effects have no threshold and constant severity. Deterministic effects are not defined by reversibility, and they are directly related to the dose received.
  32. The correct answer is A (Pleural effusion, fluid collecting in the pleural space with a curved meniscus-shaped upper border.): A homogeneous density with a meniscus-shaped border at the lower chest is the classic appearance of pleural effusion, fluid layering in the pleural space. A pneumothorax would be lucent with absent lung markings, not dense. A rib fracture shows a cortical line break, and a foreign body appears as a discrete, sharply defined opaque object.
  33. The correct answer is D (2.5 millimetres of aluminum equivalent, combining the inherent and added filtration.): For diagnostic units operating above 70 kVp, a minimum of 2.5 millimetres of aluminum equivalent total filtration is required. This strips out low-energy photons that add skin dose but do not reach the detector. The smaller values are below the standard and would leave a beam with too many low-energy photons.
  34. The correct answer is A (It gives a cumulative measurement of the worker's dose over the monitoring period.): A thermoluminescent dosimeter stores energy from radiation in a crystal, and when processed later it reports the cumulative dose for the period. It gives no real-time warning, so it cannot sound an alarm. It records the wearer's dose, not a patient's, and it has no connection to the x-ray unit that could shut it down.
  35. The correct answer is B (Position it accurately over the gonads without hiding the anatomy that the examination needs to show.): A gonadal shield only helps when it sits over the gonads and clear of the area to be diagnosed. If it covers the anatomy of interest, the image is nondiagnostic and must be repeated, increasing dose. Loose or unchecked placement risks shifting off the gonads, so the technologist verifies it against the light field before exposure.
  36. The correct answer is C (1 millisievert per year, well below the limit applied to nuclear energy workers.): The limit for members of the public from artificial sources is 1 millisievert per year. It is far below the occupational limit because the public includes children and pregnant women who have not agreed to occupational risk and are not trained in protection. The higher values apply to monitored radiation workers, not to people in a waiting area.
  37. The correct answer is D (A distal humeral fracture, located near the elbow end of the bone with displacement.): A sharp break in the cortex with slight displacement at the elbow end of the humerus, with an intact proximal humerus and shaft, is a distal humeral fracture. A proximal fracture would sit near the shoulder. A normal epiphysis is smooth and symmetrical, and a foreign body is a separate dense object, not a discontinuity in the cortex.
  38. The correct answer is A (500 millisieverts in one calendar year, reflecting the lower radiosensitivity of skin and extremities.): The equivalent dose limit for the skin and extremities is 500 millisieverts in a calendar year because these tissues are less radiosensitive than internal organs. The 50 millisievert value is the whole-body effective dose limit, and the 150 and 20 millisievert figures do not apply to the hands. Hand doses still must be kept as low as reasonably achievable.
  39. The correct answer is C (To identify patterns of overexposure or underexposure and prompt corrective changes to technique.): Digital systems rescale the image, so overexposure can go unnoticed because the picture still looks acceptable. Trending exposure indicator values reveals drift toward higher dose or noisy underexposure so technique can be corrected. The indicator does not set kVp, it is not an exact patient dose, and it does not replace physicist calibration.
  40.
