The correct answer is B (It removes low-energy photons that the patient would absorb without adding useful information to the image.): Low-energy photons are absorbed in the superficial tissues and never reach the detector, so they add patient skin dose with no image value. Aluminum filtration removes them and makes the beam more penetrating. It reduces, not increases, entrance dose, it does not define the field, and it does not change the kVp that is selected.
  41. The correct answer is D (Move as far from the source as practical, ideally two metres, or use shielding.): Distance and shielding are the primary tools for mobile work, and exposure falls with the square of the distance, so stepping back to two metres or more greatly reduces dose. An apron alone leaves unprotected areas exposed. Turning away does not reduce dose, and standing beside the patient or leaving bystanders nearby maximizes scatter exposure.
  42. The correct answer is A (Gray, the SI unit of absorbed dose, equal to one joule per kilogram of tissue.): The gray is the SI unit of absorbed dose, defined as one joule of energy deposited per kilogram of tissue. The sievert applies weighting factors to give equivalent or effective dose. The becquerel and the curie both describe how many nuclei decay each second, so they measure activity of a source, not energy absorbed by tissue.
  43. The correct answer is B (Minimize total fluoroscopy time, use pulsed fluoroscopy, and apply last image hold.): Patient skin dose rises with beam-on time and dose rate. Pulsed fluoroscopy lowers the dose per second, last image hold lets the operator review without further exposure, and keeping time short cuts total dose. High-dose continuous mode and wider fields add dose. Removing last image hold forces continued live exposure.
  44. The correct answer is C (Reposition the patient to correct the rotation and repeat the exposure.): Asymmetric clavicle distances from the spinous processes on a PA chest radiograph indicate patient rotation, which distorts the appearance of the mediastinum and cardiac silhouette and can obscure or mimic pathology. Correct marker placement does not compensate for positioning error, and adjusting kVp does not correct rotation. The patient should be repositioned and the exposure repeated to obtain a properly aligned, diagnostic image.
  45. The correct answer is D (Remove the unit from clinical service and notify service personnel so it can be recalibrated.): A kVp error beyond the tolerance of about 5 percent means the generator is outside its calibration, which alters contrast, penetration and dose. The unit should be removed from service and recalibrated. Changing technique charts or mAs only masks the fault, and waiting a week continues patient exposures on a unit known to be out of specification.
  46. The correct answer is B (Review her duties and monitoring and adjust to meet the lower declared-pregnancy limit.): A declared pregnancy brings a lower dose limit for the fetus, so the employer reviews her duties, work locations and monitoring so the limit is met. This may include adjusting tasks, not forced removal from the department. The ordinary limits are not sufficient, and removing her dosimeter would eliminate the very monitoring that shows compliance.
  47. The correct answer is A (Warn those nearby, then increase distance or use shielding to protect them.): Before a portable exposure, nearby people should be told, kept out of the primary beam and moved back or shielded. Portable units produce real scatter, so assuming minimal radiation is wrong. Evacuating the whole building is unreasonable and impractical. Speed alone does not lower the dose to bystanders, whose protection depends on distance and shielding.
  48. The correct answer is C (Grid cutoff from misalignment or wrong source-image distance, so the alignment and distance must be corrected.): Uniform loss of density with visible grid lines is typical of grid cutoff, where the grid is tilted, off-centre or used outside its focal distance so the primary beam is absorbed. Correcting the alignment and distance fixes it. Motion produces blur, collimator misalignment affects field edges, and an expired receptor would not cause grid lines.
  49. The correct answer is D (Review technique selection and AEC calibration, then retrain staff to correct it.): Digital detectors tolerate overexposure, so images still look good while patients receive more dose than necessary. A rising exposure indicator trend should be investigated promptly, including technique habits and automatic exposure control calibration, with retraining as needed. Accepting it, moving the target upward or waiting a year allows needless dose.
  50. The correct answer is B (Address the cause of the motion, such as using immobilization or clearer breath-hold instructions.): Repeating an image with the same setup invites another blurred image and a second dose. The technologist should find why the patient moved and fix it with immobilization, clearer instructions or comfort measures. Raising factors substantially increases dose, and simply asking the patient to try harder rarely changes the cause of motion.
  51. The correct answer is A (20 millisievert per year, averaged over five years, with no single year above 50 millisievert.): Canadian occupational effective dose limits allow this five year averaging structure, permitting some flexibility across years while capping any single year at 50 millisievert. This balances variable clinical workload against long term cumulative risk. The other figures either misstate the annual cap or omit the averaging provision entirely.
  52. The correct answer is C (Attached to the collar outside the apron at the thyroid or chest level.): Placing the dosimeter at the collar outside the apron estimates the dose to the unshielded head, neck, and thyroid, which typically receive the highest exposure during fluoroscopy. This location gives a more representative estimate of effective dose than placement beneath the apron alone. Placing the badge away from the body or under clothing would not reflect true occupational exposure.
  53. The correct answer is B (Exposure decreases to one quarter of the original value.): The inverse square law states that intensity is inversely proportional to the square of the distance from the source. Doubling the distance therefore reduces exposure to one quarter, not one half, of the original value. This principle underlies the emphasis on maximizing distance as a core radiation protection strategy.
  54. The correct answer is D (An equivalent dose limit of 4 millisieverts for the remainder of the declared pregnancy.): On formal declaration, Canadian regulations apply a dose limit of 4 millisieverts to the fetus for the rest of the pregnancy, well below the ordinary worker limit. Saying no separate limit exists, or reusing a worker limit, leaves the fetus unprotected. A prorated 50 millisievert figure would still allow a much higher dose than the regulation permits.
  55. The correct answer is A (0.5 millimetre of lead equivalent across the front panel of the apron.): A minimum of 0.5 millimetre lead equivalent is the standard for aprons in fluoroscopic and other higher-scatter work because it attenuates a large fraction of scatter. Thinner aprons give inadequate protection. A centimetre of lead would be far too heavy to wear, and a thyroid collar protects only one area, so it cannot replace the apron.
  56. The correct answer is D (The As Low As Reasonably Achievable principle, balancing image quality against patient dose.): The As Low As Reasonably Achievable principle guides technologists to select the lowest exposure factors consistent with acceptable diagnostic image quality. It balances the competing goals of minimizing patient dose and preserving diagnostic value. This principle underlies technique selection decisions throughout diagnostic imaging practice.
  57. The correct answer is B (Annually, as part of the scheduled preventive maintenance and QC program.): Automatic exposure control systems require periodic verification of accuracy and reproducibility to ensure consistent, appropriate exposure across a range of patient sizes. Annual testing is the standard interval within a comprehensive quality assurance program. Testing only after component failure or at much longer intervals would allow drift to go undetected.
  58. The correct answer is C (Monthly, matching the standard interval used by most dosimetry services.): Monthly exchange is the common interval for diagnostic staff because it balances timely dose tracking against the cost of processing. Weekly exchange is unnecessarily costly for low-dose work, and a five-year interval would leave unnoticed exposures for years. Reading only on request would break the regulatory duty to monitor workers.
  59. The correct answer is D (Release the fluoroscopy pedal promptly and reactivate it only when imaging actually resumes.): Fluoroscopic dose accumulates for as long as the beam is on, so releasing the pedal whenever no image is being assessed cuts both patient and staff exposure directly, which is ALARA applied in real time. Greater source to image distance does not help while the beam stays on, because the patient is still irradiated. A higher kVp alters beam quality but does not remove idle beam time. Courtesy toward a colleague is not a radiation protection justification.
  60. The correct answer is B (Pneumothorax, requiring immediate notification of the ordering physician.): Absent lung markings with a visible pleural line indicate air within the pleural space, consistent with pneumothorax. This finding is urgent and requires prompt communication to the ordering physician for clinical correlation and management. The described appearance is not consistent with normal aeration, effusion, or an external artifact.
  61. The correct answer is A (Deterministic effects, which have a dose threshold and grow more severe as dose increases.): Skin erythema and hair loss are deterministic (tissue reaction) effects: they have a threshold below which they do not occur and their severity increases with dose above it. Stochastic effects such as cancer have no threshold, and their probability, not severity, rises with dose. Genetic effects describe heritable change rather than a dose-severity pattern, and latent effects describe timing rather than a threshold relationship.
  62. The correct answer is C (Grid cutoff caused by tube angulation or off level grid alignment during this exposure.): Grid cutoff produces a band of reduced density where the grid lines absorb useful primary photons because the beam and grid are misaligned, typically from tube angulation or an off level grid, while the periphery stays normal. Patient motion causes blur rather than a localized density band. Processing chemistry problems affect the whole image uniformly, and low milliamperage would lighten the whole image rather than only a central strip.
  63. The correct answer is D (Reducing dose to radiosensitive reproductive organs near the collimated field.): Gonadal shielding placed over radiosensitive reproductive organs near the primary beam reduces the equivalent dose to these tissues without obscuring the anatomy of clinical interest. This remains a relevant consideration when the gonads lie close to the collimated field. Shielding in this context serves a protective rather than an image quality purpose.
  64. The correct answer is B (500 millisievert annual equivalent dose limit for the extremities.): The occupational equivalent dose limit for the extremities, including the hands, is substantially higher than the whole body effective dose limit because localized tissue tolerates more dose before harmful effects occur. This higher limit acknowledges the close proximity of interventional staff hands to the primary beam. It remains distinct from the whole body, eye, and fetal dose limits.
  65. The correct answer is A (Gradually raising anode temperature to reduce the risk of thermal damage and inconsistent output.): A warm up sequence raises the anode temperature gradually, which limits thermal shock that can crack a cold rotating anode and helps keep tube output consistent. Worklist synchronization is an information systems task unrelated to the tube. Collimator light and field agreement is checked during periodic quality control with a test tool, and lead apron attenuation is verified by inspection and imaging, not by tube exposures.
  66. The correct answer is C (Hang it flat on a proper rack and have it imaged to check for cracks.): A crease near the shoulder seam can hide a crack in the lead lining that lets radiation through, and only radiographic or fluoroscopic imaging can confirm the lining is intact. Garments should be stored flat or on a rounded rack to prevent this damage. Continued use, disinfecting alone, or leaving it folded over the hook all keep an unverified apron in service while the folding damage continues.
  67. The correct answer is D (Tight collimation of the primary beam to the region of clinical interest.): Tight collimation limits the primary beam to only the anatomy of clinical interest, directly reducing the volume of tissue irradiated and lowering patient dose. This is one of the most direct and immediate dose reduction techniques available to a technologist. Increasing distance or milliampere seconds does not achieve the same targeted reduction in irradiated volume.
  68. The correct answer is B (Grid cutoff from a misaligned or off-level grid, producing a band of reduced density.): A grid that is misaligned with the beam absorbs primary photons across part of the field, leaving a band of reduced density while the rest of the image stays normal. Rotation distorts anatomy rather than density, overexposure darkens the whole image, and processing problems affect the whole film evenly.
  69. The correct answer is D (The relative biological effectiveness of different radiation types in the same tissue.): The radiation weighting factor scales absorbed dose by the relative biological effectiveness of each radiation type, so alpha particles receive a much larger factor than x-rays or gamma rays. Travel distance, beam kilovoltage and the tissue irradiated are not what this factor describes. Tissue differences are handled separately by the tissue weighting factor when effective dose is calculated.
  70. The correct answer is C (20 millisievert per year, aligned with the current whole body effective dose limit.): Current ICRP based recommendations lowered the occupational lens of the eye equivalent dose limit to 20 millisievert per year, aligning it with the whole body effective dose limit. The older 150 millisievert value has been superseded. Four millisievert is the limit for the remainder of a declared pregnancy, and 500 millisievert is the limit for the hands and skin, not the lens.
  71. The correct answer is D (Maximizing distance from the tube and patient while wearing a properly fitted lead apron.): When a technologist cannot leave the room during a portable exposure, maximizing distance from the tube and patient while wearing a properly fitted lead apron combines two core protection strategies. Distance reduces exposure according to the inverse square law, while the apron attenuates scatter that does reach the technologist. Removing the apron or eliminating distance would substantially increase occupational exposure.
  72. The correct answer is A (Whether there is any possibility the patient could currently be pregnant.): Confirming pregnancy status before an examination that includes the pelvis in the primary beam field is essential, since a fetus is more radiosensitive than an adult and unnecessary exposure could pose risk. This question should be asked routinely before any examination with the pelvis in or near the primary field for patients of childbearing potential. Other listed questions are not directly related to radiation protection for this examination.
  73. The correct answer is B (Accept the image because the diagnostic information the physician requested is clearly visible.): When rotation is mild and the requested anatomy remains clearly diagnostic, accepting the image avoids an unnecessary repeat and follows ALARA, since a repeat adds dose without adding information. Immediate repeats, higher technique factors, or recalling the patient another day all increase radiation exposure for no diagnostic gain. Repeats are justified only when the image cannot answer the clinical question.
  74. The correct answer is C (Annually, along with the broader suite of acceptance and constancy testing.): Kilovoltage peak accuracy directly affects both image contrast and patient dose, making periodic verification essential. Annual testing, alongside broader acceptance and constancy testing, is the standard interval for this quality control check. Testing only at installation or only after a complaint would allow gradual drift to go undetected for extended periods.
  75. The correct answer is D (A substantially higher dose rate than standard fluoroscopy, which requires judicious and limited use.): High level control trades dose for image quality, delivering a substantially higher entrance dose rate than standard fluoroscopy, and guidance requires it to be activated deliberately and used sparingly. It does not lower dose to the level of radiography, and it cannot eliminate scatter. Source to skin distance is set by the equipment geometry and minimum distance rules rather than changed by this control.
  76. The correct answer is A (Investigate the circumstances of that monitoring period before drawing conclusions.): An elevated dosimetry reading compared to established investigation levels warrants investigation into the circumstances of that monitoring period before conclusions are drawn, since factors such as badge mishandling can produce misleading readings. Discarding the reading without investigation or taking drastic permanent action would be premature. This investigative step is standard radiation safety program practice.
  77. The correct answer is C (Primary barrier, typically needing more shielding than a secondary barrier wall.): A wall that regularly intercepts the primary beam is classified as a primary barrier and typically requires greater shielding thickness than a secondary barrier wall, which receives only scatter and leakage radiation. This distinction guides appropriate shielding design during facility planning. Treating all walls identically would either under protect primary barrier areas or add unnecessary cost elsewhere.
  78. The correct answer is B (Its shielding lowers the technologist's occupational exposure to scatter radiation.): The patient scatters x-rays during every exposure, so the technologist's occupational dose is reduced by combining distance with the lead equivalent shielding of the booth. Communication, switch protection and convenient technique selection are side benefits at most. They are not the reason the booth is built into the room or required by radiation protection guidance.
  79. The correct answer is D (It adjusts tube current continuously to reduce dose in thinner body regions.): Automatic exposure control with tube current modulation continuously adjusts output based on tissue attenuation along the scan length, reducing dose in thinner body regions while maintaining adequate image quality in thicker regions. This represents a meaningful CT dose optimization strategy compared to a fixed tube current throughout the entire scan. It does not eliminate the need for proper patient centring.
  80. The correct answer is A (Portable and fluoroscopic work often keeps staff closer to the source than is ideal.): The radiation is the same kind of ionizing radiation in every setting; what changes is whether staff can use distance and a barrier. Portable and fluoroscopic work often requires staff to stay near the patient, so lead aprons, thyroid shields, minimized beam time and the greatest available distance replace the booth. Barriers in fixed rooms are engineered shielding and are not decorative.
  81. The correct answer is B (Justification, which requires that an exposure provides a benefit that outweighs its radiation risk.): Justification requires that any exposure give the patient a net benefit greater than its risk, and an examination done only for research with no clinical purpose is not justified under that principle unless it belongs to an approved research protocol. The inverse square law and half value layer concern physical dose and beam quality, and the extremity limit governs workers, not patient examinations. Applying the principle before the exposure protects the patient from a dose that offers no benefit.
  82. The correct answer is D (The radiation safety officer, who oversees the facility's protection program.): The radiation safety officer is designated to run the protection program, which includes dosimetry oversight, investigation of unusual readings and regulatory compliance. Scheduling, image archive administration and infection control are important department functions, but none is accountable for radiation protection or dose monitoring. Other roles may support the program, but accountability sits with the officer.
  83. The correct answer is C (For the life of the individual, well beyond the end of their employment.): Occupational dose records are required to be retained for the life of the individual, since the long-term nature of potential stochastic radiation risk means a much shorter retention period tied only to active employment would not support long-term epidemiological or regulatory review. This requirement applies specifically to individual occupational dose records, not general administrative files.
  84. The correct answer is A (Announce clearly before each exposure and ask others to step back or leave.): A clear verbal announcement before every portable exposure lets staff and visitors move away or leave, which uses distance to cut their exposure to scatter and keeps the technologist in control of who is near the beam. A sign alone cannot warn people who arrive after it was read, silence leaves bystanders unprotected, and a patient cannot reliably manage others. Warning people before every exposure is a simple habit that scales to any ward setting.
  85. The correct answer is B (Verifying the image quality, contrast and exposure consistency of the mammography unit over time.): Imaging a standard phantom verifies that image quality, contrast and exposure remain consistent with the baseline, so drift or malfunction is found before patients are examined. It does not confirm schedules, replace compression force testing, which has its own separate schedule, or calibrate personal dosimeters, which are processed by an external monitoring service. Regular phantom results also give a documented trend that supports corrective action when limits are approached.
  86. The correct answer is D (Shielding can obscure anatomy and disturb automatic exposure control without meaningful dose benefit.): Better collimation and lower doses mean gonadal shielding offers little real dose reduction, while a shield can cover anatomy of interest and, if placed in the automatic exposure control field, cause a repeat exposure that raises dose. Equipment still produces scatter, shielding is not universally mandatory, and the change was driven by dose evidence rather than patient requests. The shift reflects current practice and does not remove the need to follow local policy and regulator guidance.
  87. The correct answer is C (Dose creep, where technologists gradually use higher exposures than needed for adequate images.): Because overexposed digital images still look acceptable, nobody sees the extra dose unless the exposure indicator is checked, and technique factors can drift upward over time, which is called dose creep. Underexposure is the visible problem that gets repeated, so it is self correcting. Processing delays are an efficiency issue, and software correction never removes the need for indicator monitoring.
  88. The correct answer is A (An estimate of the dose reaching the receptor compared with the expected target value.): The exposure index is a vendor standardized estimate of the signal, and so the dose, that reached the digital receptor, shown relative to a target so that over or underexposure can be recognized even when post processing makes the image look fine. It does not report the selected technique factors, the patient's examination history or the tube's remaining life. Tracking this value regularly is how a department detects dose creep before it becomes routine.
  89. The correct answer is D (Linear energy transfer, describing the density of energy deposited along the radiation track.): Linear energy transfer describes how densely a type of radiation deposits energy along its path through tissue, with densely ionizing radiation such as α particles depositing energy over a much shorter path than diagnostic x-rays. This concept underlies differences in biological effectiveness between radiation types. It is distinct from half value layer, exposure index, and radiation weighting factor, though related to the weighting factor concept.
  90. The correct answer is B (Reassignment away from high exposure fluoroscopic duties to lower dose imaging areas.): Reassigning a pregnant technologist away from high exposure fluoroscopic duties toward lower dose imaging areas is a common accommodation used to help ensure the declared fetal dose limit is respected for the remainder of the pregnancy. This reassignment is a practical, collaborative response between the technologist and radiation safety officer. Termination of employment or ignoring the declared pregnancy would not be appropriate or consistent with occupational protection practice.
  91. The correct answer is C (Occupational workers are trained, monitored, and voluntarily accept a higher limit under regulated conditions.): Occupational workers are trained in radiation safety, continuously monitored through personal dosimetry, and voluntarily accept a higher regulated dose limit in exchange for the clinical benefit their work provides. Members of the general public have not accepted this occupational role and are afforded a lower dose limit as a result. This distinction is a foundational concept in radiation protection philosophy.
  92. The correct answer is A (Half value layer, the aluminum thickness that reduces beam intensity by half.): The half value layer is the thickness of a specified material, usually aluminum, that cuts beam intensity to half, and a larger value indicates a more penetrating, higher energy beam. Exposure index concerns receptor dose, linear energy transfer describes energy deposition in tissue, and the radiation weighting factor converts absorbed dose to equivalent dose, so none of them measures penetration. Knowing this term matters because filtration and kilovoltage both change how penetrating the beam is.
  93. The correct answer is D (Misalignment can irradiate tissue outside the intended field or clip needed anatomy.): The light field is what the technologist uses to position and collimate, so if it does not match the true x-ray field, tissue outside the intended area may be irradiated or required anatomy may be clipped and need a repeat. The test therefore protects the patient as well as the image. It does not assess exposure control detectors, and bulb function is only incidental to it.
  94. The correct answer is A (Children are generally more radiosensitive and have longer remaining lifespans for stochastic effects.): Children are more radiosensitive than adults and have many more years in which stochastic effects such as cancer could appear, so each unit of dose carries a greater lifetime risk. Tighter collimation and adjusted technique reduce that dose. Equal radiosensitivity, workflow speed and tube heat conservation do not explain pediatric protocols.
  95. The correct answer is C (Investigate the badge handling history and compare workload records before finalizing the dose.): Before finalizing an unusually elevated dose assignment, the radiation safety officer should investigate the circumstances of that monitoring period, including how and where the badge was stored, to distinguish a true occupational exposure from an artifact such as accidental laundering. Cross referencing workload records helps corroborate or refute the elevated reading. Accepting the reading uncritically or taking drastic permanent action without investigation would be inappropriate.
  96. The correct answer is A (Raise kilovoltage peak moderately while limiting any rise in milliampere seconds.): A moderate kilovoltage increase improves penetration of thick tissue and lowers the milliampere seconds needed, which limits patient entrance dose while keeping adequate signal. Maximizing milliampere seconds raises dose directly. Leaving technique unchanged risks a repeat, which doubles exposure, and removing the grid for a thick body part increases scatter and degrades contrast.
  97. The correct answer is D (Reassess her duties, considering reassignment or added shielding, using projected dose.): After a pregnancy is declared, the dose to the fetus must be kept below the specific limit for the remainder of the pregnancy, so the officer reviews projected dose and decides on reassignment, additional shielding or closer monitoring. Unchanged duty ignores the limit, and automatic long term removal is not required if dose can be kept below the limit. The declaration clearly changes how dose is managed.
  98. The correct answer is B (Correct the positioning error causing rotation and use technique factors appropriate to the corrected projection.): Correcting the positioning error that caused the rotation and selecting technique factors appropriate to the corrected projection addresses the actual cause of the rejected image while avoiding unnecessary additional dose from a poorly planned repeat. Repeating with identical technique factors without correcting the underlying positioning error risks another rejected image and further repeat dose. Performing additional exposures at different angles instead of correcting the original error would compound rather than resolve the problem.
  99. The correct answer is C (Stop using automatic exposure control in the room and arrange repair or recalibration first.): An automatic exposure control that fails accuracy or reproducibility will deliver unpredictable doses and repeat exposures, so it should not be used clinically until it is repaired and passes retesting. Continuing to use it knowingly, treating the test as optional, or inflating manual factors without validation puts patients at risk of overexposure or non diagnostic images. Documenting the failure and the repair also keeps the quality assurance record complete.
  100. The correct answer is A (Maximize distance, use available shielding, and limit unnecessary time in the room.): Time, distance and shielding are the three basic protection measures, and combining them gives the greatest reduction in staff dose because scatter intensity falls rapidly with distance and is attenuated by barriers and aprons. Staying close, relying on aprons alone, or rotating staff randomly neglects at least one of these measures and does not systematically lower exposure. Planning these measures before the procedure starts is more effective than reacting during it.
  101. The correct answer is C (Immobilize the limb with sandbags or a wrap and shorten the exposure time.): Motion, not geometry or exposure factors, produced the central blur, so the fix must address the child's movement rather than the technical exposure settings. Immobilizing the limb and shortening the exposure time reduces the chance of motion during the shorter interval. Changing kVp or SID would not correct blur caused by patient motion and could waste a second exposure. Repeating without addressing the cause risks a third avoidable exposure, which violates the ALARA principle.
  102. The correct answer is B (The occupational limit is roughly twenty times higher than the public limit.): The CNSC sets the occupational effective dose limit at 50 millisievert in a single year, averaged to 20 millisievert per year over a five year period, while the public dose limit is 1 millisievert per year. This makes the occupational allowance roughly twenty times higher, reflecting that workers are trained, monitored, and volunteer for controlled exposure. The public limit is not restricted to pregnant individuals, and the two limits are deliberately unequal rather than identical.
  103. The correct answer is D (Interview the technologist about recent procedures and badge wearing practices.): An unexplained spike in a monthly dosimetry reading warrants investigation into its cause before assuming it reflects true occupational exposure, since badges can record artifact readings from being left in a radiation field, exposed to a source, or worn incorrectly. Interviewing the technologist about recent workload and badge handling is the logical first step to distinguish a true dose from an artifact. Permanent reassignment or a department wide technical change is premature without first identifying the cause.
  104. The correct answer is B (It reads the dose approaching the thyroid and eyes, above the apron's protection.): A collar badge worn outside the apron estimates the dose to the unprotected head, neck, and eyes, which is the appropriate placement for a person who regularly wears a protective apron during fluoroscopy. This is not an error, since a single waist level badge worn under the apron would significantly underestimate the dose to unshielded areas above the collar. Some departments use two badges, one under and one over the apron, to separately estimate whole body and unprotected tissue dose.
  105. The correct answer is B (Their dose is reduced to approximately one quarter of the original amount.): The inverse square law states that radiation intensity varies inversely with the square of the distance from the source, so doubling the distance reduces the dose to one over two squared, which is one quarter of the original value. Distance is one of the three core protection principles along with time and shielding, and it applies to scatter as well as primary beam radiation. Reducing to one half or one eighth would require a different distance ratio, not doubling.
  106. The correct answer is C (4 millisievert for the balance of the pregnancy, counted from the date of declaration.): Canadian regulations set the dose limit for the balance of a declared pregnancy at 4 millisievert, applied from the date the worker declares it in writing. The 20 millisievert figure is a whole body annual limit used internationally, 50 millisievert is the annual limit for other workers in Canada, and 1 millisievert is the public limit, so none protects the fetus adequately. The declaration must be in writing, because the limit applies only after the employer has been notified.
  107. The correct answer is B (Wear a lead apron, maximize achievable distance, and minimize exposure time.): When the ideal distance cannot be reached, ALARA is applied by combining all three protection tools: wearing a lead apron, standing as far from the beam as the situation allows, and keeping exposure time minimal. Skipping the apron removes a barrier, an annual audit only reviews dose after the fact, and a family member holding the receptor would receive avoidable exposure. Standing behind a mobile barrier or a wall, if one is available, further reduces the dose.
  108. The correct answer is C (Warm up of the x-ray tube and a processor or receptor cleanliness check.): Warming up the tube and checking processor or receptor cleanliness are quick checks done each day before patients are imaged. Half value layer measurement is an annual physicist test, detailed automatic exposure control accuracy testing is done periodically with phantoms, and collimator alignment is checked at longer intervals using a template or test tool. Daily checks catch problems early, while the longer interval tests catch slower drift in performance.
  109. The correct answer is D (The 0.5 mm apron attenuates more scatter radiation than the 0.25 mm apron.): A higher lead equivalency means more attenuating material, so the 0.5 mm apron absorbs a greater percentage of scatter photons than the 0.25 mm apron, at the cost of added weight. Thinner does not mean more protective, lead equivalency applies to every diagnostic energy setting, and the rating clearly changes attenuation. Apron weight and comfort still matter, but protection per unit of dose is set by lead equivalence.
  110. The correct answer is C (It blocks scatter reaching the pelvis but not the primary beam at the chest.): A pelvic apron during a chest examination only attenuates scatter that would otherwise reach the pelvis, and the primary beam is aimed at the chest, so the patient still receives the dose from the examination itself. The apron is therefore genuinely protective but not complete. Breath holding is relevant to motion blur, not shielding performance.
  111. The correct answer is B (The lens is highly radiosensitive and cumulative exposure can lead to cataract formation.): The lens of the eye is composed of long lived, actively dividing epithelial cells with limited repair capacity, making it particularly sensitive to radiation induced opacities that can progress to cataracts over a career of cumulative exposure. This biological sensitivity, not an absence of blood supply or skeletal shielding, is why regulators set a separate, more restrictive equivalent dose limit for the lens. The limit reflects genuine radiobiological risk and is incorporated into Canadian regulation, not simply borrowed to match another jurisdiction.
  112. The correct answer is C (It lets unusual or high readings be identified and investigated promptly.): Frequent exchange shortens the time between exposure and reading, so an unusually high dose, a lost badge or a faulty practice is detected and investigated while it can still be corrected. A badge does not protect the wearer and its own dose is irrelevant, warranty terms are not the reason, and officers must still review every report. Short intervals also limit the time any lost or damaged badge leaves a gap in the dose record.
  113. The correct answer is D (Undifferentiated, rapidly dividing cells with a long mitotic future are the most radiosensitive.): The Law of Bergonie and Tribondeau states that cellular radiosensitivity increases with mitotic activity and decreases with the degree of cellular differentiation, meaning undifferentiated cells with a long dividing future, such as stem cells and gonadal cells, are most vulnerable. Highly differentiated, slowly dividing cells such as muscle and nerve cells are comparatively radioresistant. This principle explains why rapidly proliferating tissues, not just bone marrow, are consistently more sensitive to radiation induced damage across the body.
  114. The correct answer is A (Deterministic effects have a threshold dose and become more severe as the dose increases.): Deterministic effects, such as skin erythema or cataract formation, appear only above a threshold dose and become more severe with increasing dose. Stochastic effects, such as cancer induction, have no threshold, and their probability rather than severity rises with dose. Neither category is routinely fatal at its threshold, and stochastic risk applies to patients and workers alike.
  115. The correct answer is A (Review the case with the radiologist to confirm the clinical justification for that exposure time.): An unusually long fluoroscopy time should prompt a review with the supervising radiologist to confirm the exposure was clinically justified and to document the reason, since fluoroscopy time is directly tied to patient and staff dose. Dismissing the reading without review ignores a potential opportunity to identify equipment issues or procedural inefficiencies contributing to unnecessary dose. Adjusting kVp on an unrelated future case does not address the specific reading in question, and assuming a malfunction without verification is not a safe response.
  116. The correct answer is B (A grid requires a higher entrance dose but improves contrast by removing scatter.): A grid absorbs scatter before it reaches the receptor, which improves contrast, but it also absorbs some useful photons, so technique and patient entrance dose must be increased to keep receptor exposure adequate. It does not lower dose, it clearly affects dose, and its role is image quality at the receptor, not staff protection. This is why grids are chosen by part thickness and kilovoltage, not used automatically on every examination.
  117. The correct answer is A (The beam is insufficiently filtered, allowing more low energy photons to reach the patient.): Half value layer measures beam quality, and a value below the minimum means the beam contains too many low energy photons that do not contribute to the image but add to patient skin dose, which points to inadequate filtration. Excess filtration would raise the value. Focal spot size and exposure control calibration are assessed with other tests.
  118. The correct answer is A (Approximately weeks 2 through 15 of gestation, from organogenesis into early brain development.): The fetus is most sensitive to malformation during organogenesis in roughly weeks 2 through 8 and to central nervous system effects, including growth and intellectual effects, through about week 15. The first two weeks are an all-or-none period, the final trimester is far less sensitive, and sensitivity is not the same across the pregnancy.
  119. The correct answer is A (Effective dose reflects tissue sensitivity and risk, while air kerma describes beam output.): Effective dose in sievert weights absorbed dose by radiation type and tissue sensitivity, so it expresses the risk to a worker, whereas air kerma describes the energy transferred in air by the beam and says nothing about tissue. They are different quantities. Sievert applies to all radiations including x-rays, and air kerma has largely replaced the older roentgen based exposure unit.
  120. The correct answer is B (Patient dose is expected to decrease compared to using a higher ratio grid.): A lower ratio grid absorbs less of the primary beam than a higher ratio grid, so less compensatory increase in technique factors is required to achieve adequate image receptor exposure, resulting in a lower patient dose when appropriately matched to a thinner body part with less scatter production. Grid ratio is directly tied to the technique factor adjustments needed, so it is not independent of dose. Collimation also affects scatter and dose, but it does not eliminate the separate contribution of grid ratio to overall technique.
  121. The correct answer is C (It reduces the technologist's cumulative occupational exposure to scatter radiation across many repeated examinations.): The patient scatters radiation in all directions during every exposure, and the lead lined barrier attenuates it, so the technologist's cumulative occupational dose over thousands of examinations is much lower. This is a radiation protection measure, not just policy. It does not hide the technologist from the patient or shield the console from electrical interference.
  122. The correct answer is B (The five year average allows flexible workloads while still capping any single year's dose.): Expressing the limit as both a single year cap of 50 millisievert and a five year average of 20 millisievert per year allows some flexibility for workload variation between years, such as an unusually busy year followed by a lighter one, while still preventing any single year from becoming excessively high and capping cumulative exposure over time. Both figures remain actively enforced together, not as an outdated versus current pairing, and they apply to all radiation workers, not exclusively minors, since occupational exposure below the age of eighteen is generally restricted further.
  123. The correct answer is A (Remove it from clinical use and report it for repair or replacement.): A crack in the lead lining is a break in attenuating material that lets radiation reach the wearer, so the apron can no longer be trusted and must be taken out of service and reported for repair or replacement. A small crack near a fold line can still leak, hiding it by refolding changes nothing, and lowering kVp does not repair a defective apron. The decision should be documented so that the apron's inspection history stays accurate.
  124. The correct answer is A (The fraction of time that the adjacent area is typically occupied by people.): The occupancy factor is the fraction of the working time that a person is present in the area being protected, so a rarely used storage room needs less shielding than a staff office. Daily exposure counts belong to workload, lead thickness is the result of the calculation, and kilovoltage range affects the transmission data used. A low occupancy factor therefore allows a thinner barrier than a continuously occupied area would need.
  125. The correct answer is A (Follow up imaging should be justified against the clinical need, applying ALARA for repeat studies.): When a finding such as this one is identified, any subsequent follow up radiographs must still be clinically justified against the benefit of confirming resolution or change, applying the ALARA principle to avoid unnecessary repeated exposure even when a significant finding is present. Maximizing technique factors on every repeat is not appropriate, since factors should remain optimized for diagnostic quality without excess dose. Shielding decisions are made based on anatomy and clinical urgency on a case by case basis, not eliminated categorically for an entire exam type.
  126. The correct answer is B (The extremities contain less actively dividing, radiosensitive tissue than the trunk does.): Hands and forearms contain little red marrow, no gonads and few organs with rapidly dividing cells, so they are less radiosensitive than the trunk and the regulations allow a higher equivalent dose there. Skin is not impermeable, extremity dose is routinely measured with ring badges, and regeneration speed is not the stated rationale. This is why the limits differ by tissue, reflecting the risk each body region carries.
  127. The correct answer is D (It helps give consistent receptor exposure across different patient thicknesses, limiting repeat examinations.): A working automatic exposure control ends the exposure when the receptor has received the intended amount, giving consistent image receptor exposure over varying patient thicknesses and reducing repeats from over or underexposure. It cannot reduce dose to zero, technologists still choose kVp, field and chamber selection, and it is used in general radiography as well as other modalities. Quality control is needed because a drifting chamber would produce consistent but wrong exposures.
  128. The correct answer is C (The adequacy of existing structural shielding for the adjacent occupied areas.): An increase in workload, meaning the total amount of radiation output generated in a room over a given period, directly affects the shielding calculation for that space, so adding equipment or increasing procedure volume should prompt a reassessment of whether existing structural shielding remains adequate to protect adjacent occupied areas. Cosmetic or administrative factors such as warning light colour, apron brand, or scheduling software have no bearing on the physics based shielding requirement. This reassessment is a core responsibility of the radiation safety program when clinical capacity changes.
  129. The correct answer is D (Advise the colleague to use the holding device and avoid placing hands in the primary beam.): Placing a hand directly in the primary beam delivers a far higher localized dose than scatter exposure and should be avoided whenever an alternative holding device or immobilization method is available, so advising the colleague to use the device directly addresses the unsafe practice before further exposure accumulates. Waiting for a dosimetry badge to reflect elevated exposure means the harmful practice would have already occurred repeatedly before any correction. Gloves provide no meaningful attenuation against the primary x ray beam and are not a substitute for keeping hands out of the field.
  130. The correct answer is B (A shorter beam on time directly reduces the cumulative dose received during that interval.): Dose accumulates in proportion to the time a person is exposed in a given radiation field, so cutting beam on time with intermittent fluoroscopy and short pulses lowers staff dose as well as patient dose. The inverse square relationship belongs to distance, not time, and time applies to every modality that uses ionizing radiation. Time is also the easiest of the three measures to control directly during a procedure.
  131. The correct answer is A (It lowers the risk of deterministic skin injury from prolonged exposure.): A larger minimum source to skin distance lowers the beam intensity at the skin entrance point, so the entrance dose rate is reduced and the chance of deterministic injury such as erythema or necrosis during long procedures falls. It therefore directly affects patient dose, it is not about image sharpness or clearance, and it protects the patient more than the technologist. This is also why the beam should be moved when a procedure is long, to spread the entrance dose.
  132. The correct answer is D (The x ray generator is producing inconsistent output for identical technique settings.): Reproducibility testing evaluates whether an x ray unit produces consistent radiation output when the identical technique factors are selected repeatedly, and poor reproducibility indicates the generator itself is not delivering a stable, predictable exposure for a given set of technique settings. This finding relates specifically to generator performance rather than collimator alignment, which is assessed through a separate light field to radiation field congruence test, and it is not primarily an indicator of image receptor aging or apron condition. Inconsistent output can compromise both image quality and predictable patient dosing.
  133. The correct answer is D (Consider a lighter or wraparound style apron that distributes the weight better.): Musculoskeletal discomfort from prolonged apron wear is a recognized occupational concern, and the appropriate solution is to consider an alternative apron design, such as a lighter composite material or a wraparound style that distributes weight more evenly across the hips and shoulders, while maintaining equivalent attenuation. Discontinuing apron use entirely sacrifices necessary shielding and is not an appropriate tradeoff, and wearing it only intermittently defeats its protective purpose during unpredictable exposures. Reducing lead equivalency to save weight also reduces attenuation and is not the recommended approach when comfort focused alternatives exist.
  134. The correct answer is D (Somatic effects appear in the exposed individual, while genetic effects may appear in offspring.): Somatic effects, such as skin burns or cancer, occur in the irradiated person, whereas genetic effects arise from damage to reproductive cells and may be seen in the offspring. They are different categories. Somatic effects can be either deterministic or stochastic, and genetic effects are stochastic.
  135. The correct answer is C (Issue a dosimeter promptly, since occupational monitoring applies to any person working with radiation.): Occupational monitoring applies to anyone who works with or near ionizing radiation, regardless of how many shifts they work, so the dosimeter should be issued promptly and the gap in the dose record documented. Waiting delays monitoring, and sharing another person's badge falsifies both workers' dose records. The incident should be reviewed so that any dose received in the meantime can be estimated and recorded.
  136. The correct answer is A (Absorbed dose is energy per unit mass, while effective dose adjusts for tissue risk.): Absorbed dose in gray is the energy deposited per unit mass of tissue, while effective dose in sievert weights that energy by radiation type and by the sensitivity of the tissues irradiated, so it estimates stochastic risk. They are not interchangeable. Effective dose applies to x-rays, and absorbed dose does not include any biological weighting.
  137. The correct answer is C (It checks that the visible light field shows where the beam will strike.): Technologists collimate by the light field, so if it does not match the true x-ray field, tissue outside the intended area can be irradiated or anatomy can be clipped and need repeating. The congruence test checks this match. Tube output, exposure control timing and processor temperature are evaluated by other tests.
  138. The correct answer is A (Confirm with the ordering provider that more imaging is clinically indicated first.): Justification applies to every additional exposure, so before more imaging is done the technologist should confirm with the ordering provider that it is clinically indicated and which examination answers the question. A new, well defined density change on a femur may be a real finding that needs correlation rather than another exposure. Skipping correlation, doubling technique or shielding a completed image all ignore dose optimization.
  139. The correct answer is B (It is now considered more practical to directly screen for pregnancy before the exam.): The ten day rule has been largely replaced in current practice by directly screening patients of reproductive age for the possibility of pregnancy before an elective exam, using a documented last menstrual period, a pregnancy questionnaire, or a pregnancy test when indicated, which is considered a more direct and reliable safeguard than restricting scheduling to a narrow window of the menstrual cycle. The original rule did have a radiobiological rationale, related to minimizing the chance of imaging during an undetected early pregnancy, but direct screening is now viewed as more effective. Modern equipment reduces dose but does not eliminate the possibility of fetal exposure, and the original guideline applied broadly to abdominal and pelvic imaging, not to computed tomography alone.
  140. The correct answer is A (Obtain two projections at right angles during the first attempt.): A suspected fracture needs two projections at right angles to show displacement and angulation, and obtaining both at the first attempt avoids a recall for a second examination. Single views can miss displacement, repeating the same view adds dose without new information, and widening the field exposes extra tissue and contradicts collimation. Collimating tightly to the injured region keeps both projections within ALARA.
  141. The correct answer is A (Filament of the cathode, heated to release electrons by thermionic emission.): The filament of the cathode is heated to produce thermionic emission, releasing electrons that form the electron cloud available for acceleration toward the anode, and the mA selected by the technologist directly controls the filament current and therefore the number of electrons produced. The rotor and stator assembly spins the anode disc rather than controlling electron quantity, the anode heel angle affects beam intensity distribution across the field, and the tube envelope maintains the vacuum required for tube operation.
  142. The correct answer is D (A laser reader that stimulates the photostimulable phosphor plate.): Computed radiography uses a photostimulable phosphor imaging plate that stores a latent image after exposure, and a dedicated laser reader scans the plate, stimulating the phosphor to release light proportional to the stored energy, which is then converted into a digital image. Wet chemical processing was used for older film based systems, not computed radiography. A thin film transistor array is a feature of direct radiography flat panel detectors, not computed radiography cassettes, and a charge coupled device is not the primary image formation component in either standard CR or DR systems described here.
  143. The correct answer is D (To remove low energy photons that add patient dose without helping the image.): Added aluminum filtration preferentially absorbs low energy photons that cannot penetrate the patient and would only be absorbed in the skin, raising the average beam energy and reducing patient dose. It reduces rather than increases photon number, field size is set by the collimator, and focal spot size is a property of the tube's filament and target, not of filtration. A thicker filter lowers patient dose further but also reduces output, so the amount is regulated.
  144. The correct answer is A (It defines a common format for exchanging images and patient data between imaging equipment and systems.): DICOM stands for Digital Imaging and Communications in Medicine and gives equipment from different manufacturers one shared file and communication format. Occupational dose limits come from radiation protection regulation, so the standard has no part in setting them. Exposure index targets are vendor and exam specific quality benchmarks that DICOM does not define. A radiology information system manages scheduling and reporting workflow and keeps operating alongside DICOM image exchange.
  145. The correct answer is C (A vendor-specific estimate of the radiation exposure that actually reached the image receptor.): The exposure indicator is a numeric value calculated by the system to estimate how much radiation reached the receptor during that exposure. It lets the technologist judge under or overexposure even though digital processing can make the displayed brightness look normal. Cumulative lifetime dose is tracked elsewhere and is not calculated from one image. The kVp and source to image distance are technique settings recorded separately, not measurements made after exposure.
  146. The correct answer is C (Raising mAs increases the number of photons produced, so receptor exposure rises as the setting increases.): Milliampere seconds combines tube current and exposure time, so it directly sets how many photons the tube produces. More photons delivered to the receptor means greater receptor exposure at the same kVp. Penetration of tissue is governed mainly by kVp, not mAs. The relationship between mAs and exposure is predictable and roughly proportional, which is why technique charts are built around it.
  147. The correct answer is B (It makes intensity vary across the field, with lower intensity toward the anode side.): Photons headed toward the anode side travel through more target material and are absorbed in greater proportion. The result is higher intensity on the cathode side and lower intensity toward the anode side of the field. This applies to conventional radiography as well as other tube based imaging. Focal spot size is a separate geometric property and is not what the heel effect changes.
  148. The correct answer is A (It absorbs scatter radiation before it reaches the image receptor, improving contrast.): A radiographic grid consists of thin strips of lead alternating with a radiolucent interspace material, positioned between the patient and the image receptor to absorb obliquely traveling scatter radiation before it reaches the receptor, thereby improving image contrast on thicker body parts where scatter production is greater. Using a grid typically requires an increase, not a decrease, in exposure time or other technique factors to compensate for the absorbed primary photons. A grid does not replace the need for collimation, which independently limits the field size and reduces scatter production at its source, and a grid does not alter the beam's energy spectrum in the way filtration does.
  149. The correct answer is D (A direct radiography flat panel detector with built-in thin film transistor readout.): A flat panel detector has the thin film transistor array built into its housing, so absorbed x-ray energy becomes an electronic signal read out immediately. No plate has to be removed and scanned by a separate reader. Screen film depends on chemical processing instead of electronic readout. Computed radiography plates and other photostimulable phosphor cassettes are both scanned in a reader unit to release the stored image.
  150. The correct answer is C (To adjust the displayed contrast and brightness range without altering the underlying pixel data.): Window width and window level adjustment on a picture archiving and communication system workstation changes how the underlying pixel data is displayed, altering the visible contrast and brightness range shown to the viewer, without modifying the original acquired pixel data or the patient's actual received radiation dose, which was already fixed at the moment of exposure. This post processing tool cannot correct actual positioning errors present in the acquired image, since those reflect the physical patient and beam geometry at exposure time, and the exposure indicator reflects the original acquisition, not subsequent display adjustments.
  151. The correct answer is D (A field of view containing all the anatomy of interest within the image.): Truncation happens when anatomy extends beyond the edges of the captured field, so the entire anatomy of interest must lie inside the field of view. Choosing the right receptor size and centering helps meet that requirement. Source to image distance and object to receptor distance are geometric factors that affect magnification and sharpness, not cropping. The focal spot to anode angle relationship affects heat loading and field coverage rather than whether anatomy is included.
  152. The correct answer is C (To terminate the exposure if the AEC fails to end it within a safe time limit.): The automatic exposure control back up timer is a safety feature that terminates the exposure after a preset maximum time if the AEC system fails to end the exposure normally, preventing an excessively long exposure and protecting both the patient from unnecessary dose and the x ray tube from potential thermal damage. This backup timer does not select kVp, which remains a technologist chosen parameter, nor does it actively increase exposure time in response to underexposure, since its role is specifically to impose a maximum limit rather than to optimize the exposure. Effective dose calculation is a separate process not performed by this timer function.
  153. The correct answer is C (The ability of the imaging system to display fine anatomical detail as separate structures.): Spatial resolution describes how well a system displays small, closely spaced structures as distinct objects. It is influenced by focal spot size, detector pixel size, and motion. Overall brightness relates to exposure level and display settings rather than sharpness. Scatter fog and patient dose are separate quality and safety concerns that do not measure fine detail.
  154. The correct answer is D (The relationship between source to image distance and object to image receptor distance.): Geometric magnification is primarily determined by the relationship between the source to image distance and the object to image receptor distance, since increasing the distance between the object and the receptor while keeping source to image distance constant increases magnification, while increasing source to image distance while keeping the object close to the receptor reduces magnification. kVp and mA are exposure factors that affect beam penetration and photon quantity respectively, not the geometric magnification of the image. Grid ratio affects scatter absorption and contrast but does not directly determine the magnification of the projected anatomy.
  155. The correct answer is A (The patient likely received a higher radiation dose than necessary for this exposure.): An exposure indicator well above the target range indicates the image receptor received more radiation than intended, and because digital post processing can normalize the displayed brightness regardless of the actual exposure level, the image can appear visually acceptable even though the patient likely received a higher radiation dose than necessary. The exposure indicator is specifically designed to reflect actual receptor exposure, so it does have a meaningful relationship to delivered dose. Image processing adjusts how the acquired data is displayed but cannot retroactively change the radiation dose that was physically delivered to the patient during the exposure itself.
  156. The correct answer is C (A reproducibility test showing inconsistent output at the same technique settings.): Unexplained density variation between exposures made at identical technique factors and similar patient size points toward inconsistent generator output, which is precisely what a reproducibility test is designed to detect by comparing several exposures made under identical conditions. Collimator misalignment would primarily affect field coverage rather than overall density, and both HVL within expected range and acceptable focal spot size are findings that would rule out those specific components as the cause rather than explain the unexpected density change described. Reproducibility failure directly explains inconsistent output between otherwise identical exposures.
  157. The correct answer is C (Lower subject contrast because more scatter is produced at the higher kVp setting.): Higher kVp raises average photon energy and penetration, but it also increases the proportion of Compton scatter. Added scatter lowers subject contrast by adding a uniform fog across the image. Contrast is clearly influenced by kVp, so expecting no change is incorrect. Scatter is not removed at any kVp threshold, and higher kVp does not reduce scatter production.
  158. The correct answer is A (Faster image availability at the workstation because no separate cassette reader step is needed.): A wireless flat panel detector reads out electronically right after exposure, so no cassette has to be carried to a reader. That removes a step and usually makes images appear at the workstation faster. Chemical processing belongs to film and is not part of either digital system. Technique factors are not routinely raised, and digital systems still display an exposure indicator.
  159. The correct answer is C (Request or locate an extension cord or reposition equipment to achieve adequate distance.): When the available exposure switch cord length does not allow the technologist to achieve the recommended minimum distance from the patient during exposure, the appropriate action is to obtain an extension cord or reposition the equipment within the room to create adequate working distance, applying the distance principle of radiation protection. Cancelling the exam is an excessive response when a reasonable workaround exists, since mobile radiography is routinely and safely performed in isolation rooms with proper technique. Having the patient trigger their own exposure switch is not an appropriate substitute for maintaining the technologist's own protective distance and workflow.
  160. The correct answer is C (A hardware related artifact that is intrinsic to that specific detector panel.): A pattern that repeats across every patient and examination points to the detector, such as a damaged element row or a calibration problem. Variable causes such as motion or positioning would change from one exam to the next. Overexposure from high mAs affects one technique choice and does not create a fixed line pattern. Consistent artifacts like this usually need a gain or offset calibration or service evaluation.
  161. The correct answer is A (The displayed exposure indicator may not fully reflect true noise, warranting a technical review.): When the exposure indicator falls within an acceptable range but the image still shows visible mottle or graininess, it suggests the displayed indicator alone may not fully capture all factors contributing to perceived image noise, such as detector calibration drift, post processing settings, or a discrepancy between the region used to calculate the indicator and the diagnostically relevant anatomy, warranting a technical review of the acquisition and processing chain. It would be inappropriate to assume the software calculated the value incorrectly without investigation, and body habitus alone does not fully explain mottle when the indicator suggests adequate exposure was achieved. Graininess is more classically associated with underexposure and increased quantum mottle, not overexposure, making that option factually reversed.
  162. The correct answer is B (It holds the last acquired frame on screen for review without keeping the beam on.): Last image hold keeps the most recent fluoroscopic frame on the monitor so findings can be discussed without further beam time, which lowers patient and staff dose. It does not improve resolution, it does not change kVp, and it clearly does have a radiation safety benefit.
  163. The correct answer is C (Check the specific cassette and imaging plate for sensitivity calibration or erasure issues.): Because the density discrepancy is consistently tied to one specific cassette rather than appearing across all cassettes used in that room, the most logical first troubleshooting step is to check that particular cassette and its imaging plate for a sensitivity calibration issue or incomplete erasure of the prior latent image, both of which are recognized causes of cassette specific density variation in computed radiography. Replacing the entire reader unit is a costly and disproportionate response before isolating the problem to a single cassette, and increasing kVp for all exams in the room would inappropriately affect every other properly functioning cassette. Dismissing a consistent, reproducible discrepancy as normal variation would allow the underlying equipment issue to persist unaddressed.
  164. The correct answer is D (A chamber poorly covered by the anatomy of interest can end the exposure improperly.): The system ends the exposure based on the radiation detected by the selected chamber. If anatomy does not adequately cover that chamber, it can detect more radiation than expected and terminate early, causing underexposure. Chamber choice therefore matters, and the system cannot correct for poor placement. This pattern points to technologist technique rather than a generator fault, and chambers should match the anatomy.
  165. The correct answer is C (The inverse square law, since beam intensity decreases as distance from the source increases.): The inverse square law describes how radiation intensity decreases proportionally to the square of the distance from the source, so increasing source to image distance significantly reduces the intensity reaching the image receptor, requiring an increase in mAs to compensate and maintain adequate receptor exposure for a diagnostic image, as is done for a standard upright chest technique. The anode heel effect relates to intensity variation across the field due to target angle, not to the overall distance based mAs compensation needed. Grid ratio selection is based on tissue thickness and scatter production from the patient, not directly dictated by source to image distance, and scatter production is primarily a function of patient thickness and kVp, not the distance between the tube and the receptor.
  166. The correct answer is D (A smaller focal spot size reduces the amount of penumbra, improving spatial resolution.): A smaller focal spot size reduces the geometric unsharpness, or penumbra, at the edges of anatomical structures, resulting in improved spatial resolution and a sharper appearing image, which is why the small focal spot is often selected for fine detail extremity work when the tube's heat capacity allows it. Focal spot size selection does not independently dictate mAs requirements, since mAs is chosen based on desired receptor exposure and tube loading considerations rather than solely by focal spot size. The clear, well established effect of focal spot size on resolution contradicts the claim that it has no measurable effect, and the large focal spot is generally reserved for situations requiring higher heat capacity rather than being universally preferred for fine extremity detail.
  167. The correct answer is D (Lower kVp raises subject contrast because photoelectric absorption depends on atomic number.): At lower kVp, more of the beam is absorbed by photoelectric interactions, which depend strongly on density and atomic number. That increases the attenuation difference between a foreign body and soft tissue. Higher kVp makes attenuation more uniform and reduces subject contrast. The kVp setting plainly affects contrast, while mAs mainly controls the quantity of exposure.
  168. The correct answer is D (Increase mAs as thickness increases, because more tissue attenuates a greater share of the beam.): With kVp held constant, thicker tissue attenuates more of the primary beam before it reaches the receptor. The mAs must rise to deliver enough photons to keep receptor exposure adequate. Lowering mAs would worsen underexposure on thick patients. Changing only the distance is not the standard way to build a technique chart, and unchanged mAs would leave large patients underexposed.
  169. The correct answer is C (The beam falls outside the grid's focal range, so lead strips absorb peripheral primary photons.): A focused grid has lead strips angled to match the diverging beam at a specific distance range. When the tube angle or distance falls outside that range, the strips absorb primary photons, especially toward the periphery, and density drops. A low grid ratio causes more scatter reaching the receptor, not cutoff. Receptor distance from the patient and a low kVp do not create this focusing error.
  170. The correct answer is A (Increase mAs to provide a greater quantity of photons forming the image.): Quantum mottle results from an insufficient quantity of photons reaching the image receptor to form a statistically adequate image, producing a grainy or noisy appearance, so increasing mAs directly addresses this by providing a greater number of photons to form the image, improving the signal available at each pixel. Increasing kVp alone without adjusting mAs affects beam penetration and contrast but does not directly increase the photon quantity in the way mAs does, so it is not the most direct fix for a quantity related noise problem. Decreasing SID would change magnification and would need to be compensated by other factors, and thin body parts such as the hand or wrist typically do not require grid use at all, making grid ratio an unlikely and disproportionate solution for this scenario.
  171. The correct answer is A (The mobile unit's generator output differs from the fixed unit, so it needs its own chart.): Mobile units often have different generator characteristics, power limits, and output calibration than a fixed room. Using one technique chart for both can therefore leave mobile images underexposed. Each unit should have a chart calibrated to its own output. Mobile equipment can produce diagnostic images when properly calibrated, and the same receptors are commonly used in both settings.
  172. The correct answer is D (Post processing can enhance real detail but may also introduce artifacts requiring careful interpretation.): Edge enhancement and other post processing algorithms can genuinely improve the conspicuity of fine linear structures such as a hairline fracture by increasing the apparent contrast at sharp transitions, but the same enhancement process can also exaggerate or create false appearing line artifacts from noise, detector irregularities, or normal anatomical structures, so images processed in this way require careful and experienced interpretation. It is therefore inaccurate to claim post processing always improves accuracy without any tradeoff, since this dual effect is a recognized limitation. Post processing does not alter the actual underlying anatomy that was captured at the moment of exposure, only how the existing pixel data is subsequently displayed and enhanced.
  173. The correct answer is B (It allows for a significantly higher heat capacity and permits higher technique factors.): A rotating anode continuously spreads the heat generated during an exposure over a much larger surface area of the anode disc compared to a stationary fixed anode target, allowing for a significantly higher heat capacity and enabling the use of higher technique factors, such as those needed for a larger patient's abdominal radiograph, without risking anode damage. Filtration requirements are determined by regulatory standards for beam quality and are independent of whether the anode is fixed or rotating. Neither anode design produces a truly monoenergetic beam, since bremsstrahlung interactions inherently produce a spectrum of photon energies, and the anode heel effect remains present with a rotating anode design as well, since it results from the angled target geometry rather than from anode rotation itself.
  174. The correct answer is B (To keep the image brightness at the monitor adequate despite the greater patient attenuation.): Automatic brightness control adjusts kVp and mA as patient thickness and attenuation change. That keeps the monitor brightness consistent even when more tissue absorbs the beam. It does not shorten the procedure or affect table positioning. Magnification is controlled separately by selecting a smaller field of view, so it is not the purpose of this adjustment.
  175. The correct answer is B (A foreign object or debris present on or within that specific cassette or detector.): An artifact that is sharply marginated, geometrically shaped, and consistently appears in the identical location across multiple otherwise unrelated exams performed with the same cassette strongly points toward a foreign object, debris, or damage on or within that specific cassette or detector, rather than something related to the individual patients themselves. Patient motion would be expected to vary between individual patients rather than appear identically positioned across multiple unrelated exams. It would be an extraordinary coincidence for multiple unrelated patients to share an identical anatomical variant in the exact same image location, making incorrect mAs selection for each separate exam and a shared anatomical variant both far less plausible explanations than a fixed equipment related cause.
  176. The correct answer is D (Chamber positions may miss the region of greatest interest in an unusually shaped body part.): Automatic exposure control ends the exposure from the fixed location of its chambers. In a body part with uneven thickness, those chambers may not sit over the area of greatest interest. The exposure can then end based on the wrong tissue. This is why manual technique remains a valid alternative, and the limitation does not rule out use on lateral projections generally.
  177.
