The correct answer is B (The specific post processing algorithm and parameters applied to the raw image data.): Beyond the raw quantity of radiation reaching the receptor, the specific post processing algorithm and its parameter settings can independently influence how noisy or smooth the final displayed image appears, since different processing approaches can either suppress or inadvertently amplify underlying noise present in the raw pixel data. The patient's recorded age has no direct technical bearing on image noise characteristics, nor does the colour of the lead apron used for shielding, which is a cosmetic feature unrelated to image formation. Similarly, the brand name printed on the receptor housing is not itself a technical factor affecting noise, though different manufacturers' underlying detector technology and processing algorithms certainly can differ in performance.
  178. The correct answer is B (It reduces the amount of scatter radiation reaching the image receptor, improving contrast.): Beyond its important role in reducing patient dose by limiting the volume of tissue irradiated, appropriate collimation to the anatomy of interest reduces the amount of tissue available to generate scatter radiation, which in turn reduces the scatter reaching the image receptor and improves overall image contrast. This means collimation does have a genuine and direct image quality benefit, contrary to the claim that its effect is limited to dose reduction alone. Collimation does not directly increase the intrinsic spatial resolution capability of the receptor itself, and it does not eliminate the separate clinical need for a grid on sufficiently thick body parts, though it can somewhat reduce the total scatter burden the grid must manage.
  179. The correct answer is A (Spatial resolution generally improves with a smaller detector element pixel pitch.): A smaller detector element pixel pitch means the individual sampling elements of the receptor are more finely spaced, allowing the system to more precisely capture and display fine anatomical detail, which generally improves spatial resolution when other factors such as focal spot size and motion are held constant. This makes the claim that resolution worsens with smaller pixel pitch factually reversed, and pixel pitch is a well established and significant contributor to a digital receptor's maximum achievable spatial resolution, not an irrelevant factor. Spatial resolution is influenced by multiple factors including focal spot size and detector characteristics, but it is not determined exclusively by kVp, which primarily governs beam penetration and contrast rather than image sharpness.
  180. The correct answer is B (Preventive maintenance services components to prevent failure, while quality control verifies performance.): Preventive maintenance involves scheduled servicing, cleaning, and inspection to lower the chance of unexpected failure. Quality control testing measures whether performance, such as output consistency or alignment, remains within tolerance. The two work together and neither replaces the other. Servicing done only after failure is corrective maintenance, which is a different activity.
  181. The correct answer is A (Pediatric patients generally need lower mAs and often higher kVp with a shorter exposure time.): Children are smaller and have less tissue to penetrate, so less radiation is needed to reach the receptor. Technique usually uses lower mAs, often with higher kVp and the shortest possible exposure time to limit motion and dose. Adult factors would overexpose a child. Thickness is lower, not greater, in pediatric patients, and kVp is commonly adjusted as well.
  182. The correct answer is B (Different detector technologies, algorithms, and examination types each require their own established reference ranges.): Manufacturers use different detector technologies and proprietary algorithms to calculate their indicator values. Different examinations also have different appropriate exposure needs. Each combination of equipment and exam therefore needs its own reference range. The ranges are clinically meaningful and are not set by personal preference, and no single formula is shared by all vendors.
  183. The correct answer is D (It reduces the volume of tissue producing scatter, which improves contrast on the resulting image.): A smaller field exposes less tissue, so less scatter is generated and reaches the receptor. Less scatter fog means better image contrast in addition to lower patient dose. Collimation does not change magnification, which depends on distances. Anatomical side markers are still required on every image, and image quality clearly responds to field size.
  184. The correct answer is A (Background radiation exposure has accumulated on the unused plate over the storage period.): Photostimulable phosphor imaging plates used in computed radiography can accumulate a small amount of latent signal from ambient background radiation over an extended storage period if not erased or used regularly, which can manifest as a subtle fogging effect and reduced image contrast when the plate is finally exposed and processed, making periodic erasure of infrequently used plates a recommended practice. The plate's physical dimensions do not change due to storage duration, and the laser reader itself remains fully capable of processing a plate regardless of how long it sat unused, ruling out a reader limitation as the cause. This finding does not require establishing an entirely separate technique chart for that plate, since the underlying issue relates to accumulated background signal rather than an inherent difference in the plate's exposure requirements.
  185. The correct answer is B (The higher kVp lower mAs technique generally reduces patient dose but also reduces contrast.): A higher kVp with correspondingly lower mAs technique generally reduces overall patient dose, since fewer photons are needed at the higher, more penetrating energy to achieve similar receptor exposure, but this approach also tends to reduce subject contrast because the higher energy beam produces relatively more uniform Compton scatter and less differential photoelectric absorption between tissues. This tradeoff means it is inaccurate to claim the higher kVp lower mAs approach increases both dose and contrast simultaneously, since it typically reduces both when compared with an equivalent density lower kVp higher mAs technique. Both kVp and mAs meaningfully influence patient dose, since mAs directly scales photon quantity and kVp affects both photon energy and the efficiency of dose deposition in tissue.
  186. The correct answer is A (It lets staff spot trends in equipment performance before they begin to affect image quality.): A log of readings over time shows gradual drift in equipment performance. Spotting a trend lets staff correct a problem before it degrades diagnostic images. This applies to digital readers and processors as well as film processors. The log is about equipment performance, not staffing, and it keeps its value after film is retired.
  187. The correct answer is C (Beam hardening and scatter effects caused by the high atomic number metallic object.): A metallic orthopedic implant has a much higher atomic number and density than surrounding soft tissue and bone, causing pronounced beam hardening as lower energy photons are preferentially absorbed and producing characteristic bright signal with radiating streak artifacts extending outward from the metal, a well recognized physical phenomenon related directly to the object's composition rather than to equipment malfunction. This artifact pattern is a physical interaction effect, not a software problem occurring independent of the implant's actual physical presence. Underexposure of surrounding tissue would produce a generally darker or noisier appearance rather than this specific bright streaking pattern centred on the metal, and while AEC chamber selection can affect overall exposure, it does not directly explain this metal specific streak pattern, which occurs even with appropriately selected technique.
  188. The correct answer is D (It reduces the number of x-ray pulses delivered per unit of time, which lowers dose.): Pulsed fluoroscopy delivers short bursts of x-rays, and a lower pulse rate means fewer bursts each second. Fewer pulses over the same fluoroscopy time reduces cumulative patient dose. The tradeoff is reduced temporal resolution, not an improvement. Automatic brightness control still operates in pulsed mode, and pulse rate does change radiation delivered.
  189. The correct answer is D (Investigate the AEC calibration and chamber sensitivity specific to that room's equipment.): Because reproducibility testing already confirmed the generator's output is consistent within tolerance, and the same technologists working with similar patients show no comparable problem in a second room, the pattern points toward a room specific technical issue rather than a staffing or generator output problem, making an investigation of the AEC calibration and chamber sensitivity in the affected room a logical and targeted next step, since a drifted or miscalibrated AEC could cause premature exposure termination leading to systematic underexposure. Jumping directly to staff retraining is not supported when the same staff perform normally in the comparable room, and dismissing a statistically significant, room specific increase as coincidental would leave a real technical problem unaddressed. Replacing equipment in the unaffected room does nothing to address the actual problem occurring in the affected room.
  190. The correct answer is B (A DICOM configuration or software setting difference specific to the affected workstations.): Because the monitor hardware is identical across all workstations, yet the display orientation problem appears only at certain workstations on the network while displaying correctly at others, the issue most likely lies in a DICOM configuration or software setting specific to the affected workstations, such as an orientation tag interpretation difference or a workstation specific display protocol setting, rather than in the underlying image data or hardware itself. Since identical hardware is confirmed across all workstations, a hardware failure specific to only some of them is a less likely explanation than a software configuration difference. It would be inconsistent for the modality itself to be incapable of correct orientation if the same images display correctly at other workstations, and individual exam positioning variation would not explain a pattern isolated to specific workstations rather than specific exams.
  191. The correct answer is C (The maximum mA and kVp output capability of that specific generator.): Output consistency and beam quality are already confirmed, yet only large patients are underexposed at recommended factors. That pattern suggests the generator may not deliver its rated maximum output when asked for high technique. Checking maximum available mA and kVp is the logical next step. Light field alignment and monitor color calibration do not affect receptor exposure, and scheduling software is unrelated.
  192. The correct answer is B (Reviewing and reinforcing technologist positioning technique and training for that exam type.): Because equipment testing across all relevant rooms shows normal performance, and the repeats are specifically characterized as positioning related rather than exposure related, the appropriate focus is on reviewing and reinforcing technologist positioning technique and training specific to that exam type, which directly addresses the identified root cause rather than an unrelated equipment factor. Replacing generators that are already confirmed to be performing normally would not resolve a positioning related problem and represents an unnecessary and costly response. Increasing technique factors addresses exposure related issues, not positioning related repeats, and would not correct the actual identified cause. Discontinuing a clinically necessary exam type is a disproportionate response to a training and technique issue that can reasonably be addressed through education and quality improvement.
  193. The correct answer is B (A slow calibration drift in the detector or generator needing scheduled recalibration.): A gradual, slow drifting trend in the exposure indicator values required to achieve acceptable image appearance over several weeks, occurring despite consistent technique factor selection for similar patients, is characteristic of a slow developing calibration drift in the detector or generator system rather than a sudden acute failure, which would typically present as an abrupt rather than gradual change, warranting scheduled recalibration to correct the drift before it progresses further. This gradual, consistent trend across many exams over time is not consistent with simple random patient to patient variation, which would be expected to fluctuate without a clear directional trend. Since the technologist's selected technique factors have remained consistent throughout the period in question, an error in technique factor selection is not a logical explanation for this specific trend.
  194. The correct answer is A (Misalignment can develop gradually and quietly degrade image quality or raise dose if undetected.): Misalignment from wear, mounting shift, or mechanical stress can build up slowly. A longer interval lets such a problem go unnoticed for longer, with subtle effects on image quality and repeat rates or dose. Grids do not realign themselves. Efficiency gains from fewer tests do not outweigh the risk of undetected degradation, and alignment testing has clear clinical relevance.
  195. The correct answer is D (Verifying the dose measurement or reporting system for a possible calibration or logging error.): An unexpected and unexplained drop in recorded cumulative air kerma values compared to historical data for similar procedures, without any corresponding change in clinical technique, should prompt priority verification of the dose measurement or reporting system itself for a possible calibration drift or logging error, since an inaccurate low reading could mask a true dose that has not actually decreased, creating a false sense of reassurance. It would be premature to simply assume improved staff efficiency accounts for the discrepancy without first ruling out a measurement or reporting problem. Concluding that lower recorded values are automatically favourable without verifying their accuracy risks overlooking a genuine equipment or system error, and making further clinical protocol changes such as reducing pulse rate based on unverified data could introduce risk without addressing the actual underlying question.
  196. The correct answer is D (The portable generator's power output capability may be more limited than a fixed room's dedicated generator.): Portable units often have limited generator power because of size, battery capacity, or available electrical supply. That can restrict the technique factors reachable, including short exposure times that limit motion. Lateral decubitus views can be done with portable equipment, and portable units can give diagnostic images. Differences in staff skill are not a unique feature of portable equipment.
  197. The correct answer is C (Gradual x ray tube performance changes or output degradation over its accumulated service life.): Since detector calibration has already been verified as within specification and the standard scanning protocols have not changed, yet image noise has gradually increased over several months, an additional factor worth investigating is gradual x ray tube performance changes or output degradation accumulated over the tube's service life, which can subtly reduce the effective photon output reaching the detector over time even without triggering an outright equipment failure alarm. The patient's reported symptoms prior to the scan have no technical bearing on the physical image noise characteristics produced by the scanner. The colour display scheme used for viewing reconstructed images affects visual presentation only, not the underlying noise present in the acquired data, and appointment scheduling software has no technical relationship to image quality or tube performance whatsoever.
  198. The correct answer is D (The workflow efficiency gain and expected patient volume that would benefit from faster direct radiography readout.): For a low volume clinic, the value of faster readout depends on how many patients would benefit from the saved time. That must be weighed against purchase and service costs. Both systems can already produce diagnostic images, so capability is not the deciding point. Housing color and brand recognition do not affect throughput, dose, or total cost of ownership.
  199. The correct answer is C (Establish a reliable way to track, store, and later reconcile images with the patient record.): During an unplanned PACS outage, the most important priority is establishing a reliable downtime method to track which images belong to which patient, temporarily store those images in a way that preserves their diagnostic quality and associated data, and ensure they are later properly reconciled and uploaded to the permanent patient record once the system is restored, protecting both patient safety and the continuity and completeness of the medical record. Continuing every scheduled exam exactly as normal without any downtime workflow adjustment risks images becoming lost, misattributed, or unreadable once normal systems are restored. Cancelling every scheduled examination, including urgent or emergent cases, is an excessive response when a functional downtime procedure can safely continue to serve at least urgent clinical needs. Discarding images acquired during the outage would represent a serious loss of clinically important information and is never an appropriate response to a temporary system outage.
  200. The correct answer is B (An alignment or technique issue specific to how the mobile grid cassette is used.): The artifact appears only with the mobile grid cassette, while the fixed grid in the same room performs normally. That isolates the problem to how the mobile cassette is aligned or positioned, such as tube angle or centering. A generator fault would affect every examination in the room. Network problems cannot create a grid cutoff pattern in the acquired image itself.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Patient dose increases because the system lengthens mAs to offset primary photons the grid absorbs.): A higher ratio grid absorbs more scatter and also more primary photons. The automatic exposure control keeps going until the receptor reaches its preset exposure, so it lengthens the exposure to compensate. That raises the mAs and the patient dose, even though contrast improves. Density is maintained because the system adjusts, and higher ratio grids clean up more scatter rather than transmitting more.
  2. The correct answer is A (92 kVp with 20 mAs, a 15 percent kVp rise with half the mAs.): A 15 percent rise in kVp roughly doubles receptor exposure, so mAs can be cut in half to keep density the same. Ninety two kVp is about 15 percent above 80 kVp, and 20 mAs is half of 40. This lowers patient dose. Halving mAs alone would underexpose, lowering kVp with the same mAs would underexpose, and raising kVp without cutting mAs would overexpose.
  3. The correct answer is C (The plate was overexposed, so the patient received more dose than necessary despite an acceptable appearance.): A higher indicator value reflects more radiation reaching the plate. Post processing rescales the displayed brightness, so an overexposed plate can still look acceptable. The patient nevertheless received more dose than needed, which conflicts with ALARA. The indicator is a valid feedback tool and should not be ignored. Automatic rescaling hides the error but does not remove the extra dose.
  4. The correct answer is D (To direct the more intense part of the beam toward the thicker inferior thoracic region.): The anode heel effect makes the beam more intense on the cathode side and weaker toward the anode side. Placing the cathode over the thicker inferior thoracic region sends more photons where more attenuation occurs, which evens out receptor exposure. It does not shorten exposure time or align the focal spot with grid lines. Off-focus radiation is not controlled by tube orientation.
  5. The correct answer is B (Focal spot blooming, in which the actual focal spot enlarges at very high tube current.): At very high tube current, electron repulsion in the beam enlarges the actual focal spot beyond its rated size, which is called blooming. A larger effective focal spot increases penumbra and reduces recorded detail. Scatter from a low ratio grid affects contrast rather than sharpness. A receptor fault would show on every exam, and a longer distance reduces magnification, not sharpness.
  6. The correct answer is C (Even density loss across the entire image, since the angle error is constant.): A tilt away from perpendicular to the central ray makes the lead strips absorb primary photons in the same way everywhere on the grid. The result is a uniform loss of density across the image. Edge only loss is typical of lateral decentering and not an off-level tilt. Central increase is not a grid cutoff pattern, and cutoff clearly removes primary photons and so reduces density.
  7. The correct answer is A (The algorithm may misjudge the anatomy, so the image looks too dark or too light.): Histogram analysis looks at the range of pixel values to find the anatomy and set the display. Direct exposure outside the patient adds many very high values and can mislead the algorithm. The displayed image may then look too dark or too light, and the indicator can also be skewed. Tight collimation avoids this. Processing is affected by collimation, so the image will not always look correct.
  8. The correct answer is D (Bearing failure can stop the anode from rotating, causing focal track melting and possible tube failure.): The anode must spin quickly so heat spreads over a large focal track. A grinding sound at spin-up often indicates bearing wear or failure. If the anode stops rotating, heat concentrates in one spot, which can melt the track and destroy the tube. Filament heating is quiet, and bearing problems do not simply change contrast or kVp accuracy.
  9. The correct answer is C (Fluctuation in mA output caused by changes in filament temperature that affect the space charge cloud.): Electrons boiled off the filament form a space charge cloud that limits further emission, and this changes with kVp. Space charge compensation adjusts filament current so the mA output stays consistent. A failure can make output vary and produce mottle. Timer drift, line voltage loss, and grid alignment are separate matters that this circuit does not address.
  10. The correct answer is B (It permanently discards image data, which can degrade fine detail needed to see subtle findings.): Lossy compression reduces file size by permanently removing data judged less visually important. Aggressive settings can blur fine detail needed to recognize subtle findings, and the lost data cannot be recovered. Lossless compression keeps all pixel information. Compression does not improve resolution, and an archived image may be reviewed again later, for example as a comparison study.
  11. The correct answer is D (An increase in mAs, because filtration removes low energy photons and lowers beam intensity.): Added filtration absorbs the lowest energy photons, which mostly add skin dose without reaching the receptor. Removing them lowers overall beam intensity and the exposure reaching the receptor, so mAs must go up. Lower mAs would worsen the loss. Filtration does affect receptor exposure, and distance changes are not the standard compensation.
  12. The correct answer is A (The beam's effective penetrating quality and the adequacy of its total filtration.): A half-value layer is the aluminum thickness that reduces intensity by half, which reflects average beam penetrating quality. It is tied to total filtration, since too little filtration leaves many low energy photons and a low value. Timer precision, receptor resolution, and light field alignment are checked by other tests and do not use this measurement.
  13. The correct answer is B (The number may be falsely reassuring, because unattenuated background exposure can skew the calculated value.): Exposure indicators are calculated from pixel values across a defined region. Large areas of direct exposure outside the patient can distort that calculation and make a poorly centered image look acceptable. The technologist should still judge the image itself, including centering and collimation. The indicator guarantees nothing about positioning, and centering does affect the calculation.
  14. The correct answer is D (Quantum mottle from too few photons reaching the image receptor.): Quantum mottle is a grainy, speckled pattern that results from statistical fluctuation when too few photons form the image. Low mAs together with a high ratio grid, which absorbs many primary photons, leaves few to reach the receptor. Motion produces blur rather than speckle. An upside down grid causes severe cutoff, and scatter fog lowers contrast rather than adding grain.
  15. The correct answer is C (Document and report the cluster for engineering evaluation before relying on affected images.): Defective pixel clusters can hide or mimic pathology depending on where they fall, so daily quality control tracks them and their growth. The finding should be documented and reported so engineering can evaluate and recalibrate or service the panel. Correction software has limits and cannot fix every cluster. Raising mAs adds dose without helping, and exposure control calibration is unrelated to pixels.
  16. The correct answer is A (Receptor exposure falls substantially, so mAs must rise using the density maintenance formula.): The inverse square law says intensity falls with the square of distance. Moving from 100 cm to 180 cm cuts intensity to roughly 31 percent, a large drop. The mAs must be increased in proportion to the square of the distance ratio to keep receptor exposure constant. Exposure does not rise with distance, distance does change intensity, and the drop is too large to ignore.
  17. The correct answer is D (The exposure may end based on tissue outside the area of interest, giving poor fracture density.): The exposure ends once the selected cell receives a preset amount of radiation, which assumes that cell lies under the key anatomy. If the center cell sits under different tissue, the exposure may end too early or late for the fracture region. The cells must be selected manually beforehand, and the selected cell determines termination, not kVp.
  18. The correct answer is B (It reduces the field the tube can cover, limiting use for large field of view exposures.): The line focus principle projects a larger actual focal spot as a smaller effective spot. A steeper (smaller) anode angle makes the effective spot smaller, but the beam is cut off by the target, so it covers a smaller field. The heel effect also becomes more pronounced at steeper angles. Field coverage clearly changes with angle, and the effective focal spot gets smaller rather than larger.
  19. The correct answer is A (The tube rating chart, to confirm technique and repetition rate stay within safe heat limits.): Tube rating charts list safe combinations of kVp, mA, and time for a given tube, and anode cooling curves show how fast heat dissipates between exposures. Using them prevents anode damage during rapid series. A grid table adjusts technique for grid ratio. Backup timers cap single exposures only, and collimator logs concern field size, not heat loading.
  20. The correct answer is C (Images could be reviewed and acted on under the wrong patient's chart, a serious error.): An HL7 interface passes demographic and order data between the radiology information system, modality, and PACS so images attach to the right patient. When that fails, a radiologist could report on another patient's study, leading to wrong diagnosis or treatment. The problem must be reported and corrected. It is not just a billing issue, it does not self correct, and the system does not catch every mismatch.
  21. The correct answer is C (The diaphragm, the dome shaped muscle separating the thoracic and abdominal cavities.): The diaphragm is a dome shaped muscle that separates the thoracic cavity above from the abdominal cavity below and contracts to drive inspiration. It attaches to the lower ribs, sternum, and lumbar vertebrae, and its position shifts with the breathing phase, which matters when timing chest exposures. The mediastinum, xiphoid process, and costal cartilage are structures within or around the thorax but do not form its floor.
  22. The correct answer is D (Visualization of about ten posterior ribs above the diaphragm, indicating a good inspiratory effort.): Adequate inspiration on a PA chest image is judged by counting posterior ribs seen above the diaphragm, with about ten indicating a good inspiratory effort. Poor inspiration crowds lung markings and can hide or mimic pathology, so the exposure should be repeated when needed. The clavicles are normally seen, scapular position relates to arm placement, and seeing only a few ribs shows poor inspiration.
  23. The correct answer is A (Complete fracture, in which the break passes entirely through the bone and separates the fragments): A complete fracture runs through the full thickness of the bone, so the fragments lose all continuity. A greenstick fracture is incomplete and mostly seen in children whose bones bend before breaking. A stress fracture comes from repeated microtrauma rather than one acute injury and may be radiographically occult at first. A torus fracture is a cortical buckle with no through-and-through break.
  24. The correct answer is B (Greenstick fracture, an incomplete break where one cortex cracks and the opposite side bends): Children have flexible bone with a thick periosteum, so a greenstick fracture cracks one cortex while the other side only bends. Comminuted fractures produce several fragments, which does not match a partial crack. A compound fracture is defined by a skin wound over the break, not by an intact periosteum. A pathologic fracture arises in diseased bone and does not describe a traumatic bend in healthy pediatric bone.
  25. The correct answer is D (At the peak of a full deep inspiration, when the lungs are maximally expanded): Full inspiration expands the lungs, lowers the diaphragm, and separates overlapping lung markings so the fields are well shown. Exposing on expiration crowds the markings and makes the heart look larger and the lungs look hazy. A shallow breath leaves the lungs only partly aerated and the diaphragm high. Exposing right after a cough is not a controlled breath hold and gives inconsistent inflation.
  26. The correct answer is A (Osmolality, the number of dissolved particles per kilogram of solvent in the injected agent): Osmolality measures particle concentration relative to plasma, and high-osmolality agents are linked to more adverse effects, which is why low-osmolality agents are preferred. Viscosity describes thickness and affects injection force, not particle concentration. Ionicity describes whether a molecule dissociates in solution and only influences osmolality indirectly. Radiodensity describes how strongly the agent attenuates x-rays, which relates to image brightness rather than reaction risk.
  27. The correct answer is C (Mild reaction, with limited symptoms that are self-limited and need only observation): Mild reactions involve a few hives, limited nausea, or brief flushing, and they resolve without drug treatment while the patient is watched. A moderate reaction involves symptoms that persist or spread and require medication. A severe reaction threatens the airway or circulation and needs emergency care, which does not fit stable vital signs. An anaphylactoid reaction involves systemic compromise such as bronchospasm or hypotension, which this patient does not show.
  28. The correct answer is B (A soft tissue mass or foreign body superimposed on the upper left region of the image): A discrete density separate from the bone outlines is most consistent with a soft tissue mass or a foreign body projected over that region, and it needs correlation with the history. Normal trabecular pattern lies within the bone and would not appear as a separate soft tissue density. The distal fibula is a known bone landmark with a predictable position and does not match an unexplained density. A marker is a small lettered object placed at the edge, so it would not mimic a mass.
  29. The correct answer is A (Corticosteroids, usually combined with an antihistamine, given in doses before the injection): Premedication protocols use a corticosteroid, often with an antihistamine, given hours before contrast to blunt an allergic-type response. Beta blockers act on heart rate and do not prevent hypersensitivity, and they can make treating a reaction harder. Anticoagulants address clotting and have no effect on allergy. Diuretics remove fluid and can worsen dehydration, which raises kidney risk rather than lowering allergic risk.
  30. The correct answer is C (Serum creatinine or eGFR, which shows how well the kidneys filter the blood): Contrast is cleared by the kidneys, so creatinine or estimated glomerular filtration rate shows whether renal function is adequate before the injection. Hemoglobin A1c describes long-term glucose control and says nothing about kidney clearance. Platelet count relates to bleeding and clotting, not to nephropathy risk. Serum sodium reflects fluid balance and does not measure the filtering capacity that determines contrast safety.
  31. The correct answer is B (Medial, meaning closer to the midline of the body than the reference structure): Medial describes a position nearer the midline, so the nose is medial to the ear. Lateral is the opposite and means farther from the midline. Proximal refers to nearness to the attachment of a limb, not to the midline of the whole body. Superficial describes depth relative to the body surface and has nothing to do with left-right position.
  32. The correct answer is D (Computed tomography, which reconstructs cross-sectional slices from x-ray data collected around the patient): Computed tomography rotates the tube and detectors around the patient and reconstructs thin axial slices by computer. Fluoroscopy uses a fixed tube and detector to show motion and does not rotate to build slices. Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry scans along the body to measure bone density, not to create cross-sectional anatomy. Digital radiography records one flat projection with no rotation or reconstruction.
  33. The correct answer is C (Intracapsular fracture, where the blood supply to the femoral head may be disrupted): Intracapsular fractures involve the femoral neck within the capsule, where the retinacular vessels can tear and cut off blood to the femoral head, so avascular necrosis is a major risk. Extracapsular fractures lie outside the capsule and the head keeps better blood flow. A greater trochanteric avulsion is a small muscle-pull injury that does not threaten the head. A pubic ramus fracture is part of the pelvic ring and does not involve the hip joint capsule.
  34. The correct answer is B (Lumbar spine, with the largest vertebral bodies carrying most of the body weight): The lumbar vertebrae carry the weight of the upper body, so their bodies are the largest and thickest of the mobile spine. The cervical vertebrae are the smallest because they only support the head. Thoracic vertebrae have costal facets for rib articulation and are intermediate in size. The sacrum is a fused bone rather than separate vertebral bodies and transmits load into the pelvis.
  35. The correct answer is D (Vasovagal reaction, a nerve-mediated fall in blood pressure and heart rate with fainting): A vasovagal reaction is triggered by anxiety or needle stress and causes pallor, sweating, hypotension, and a slow pulse, which matches this patient. A tachycardic crisis would show a fast pulse, the opposite of what was recorded. A hypertensive emergency involves high blood pressure, but this patient's pressure is falling. Laryngeal edema produces airway noise and breathing difficulty, which the patient does not have.
  36. The correct answer is A (The large intestine, extending from the rectum through the colon to the cecum): A barium enema fills the colon retrograde through the rectum, so it shows the large intestine from the rectum to the cecum. The esophagus and stomach are swallowed-barium territory and belong to an upper gastrointestinal series. The duodenum and jejunum are studied with a small bowel follow-through after oral barium. The gallbladder and bile ducts are not part of the bowel and are imaged with ultrasound or cholangiography.
  37. The correct answer is B (The duodenum, the C-shaped first segment of the small intestine beyond the pylorus): The duodenum begins at the pylorus and is the first part of the small intestine, so chyme passes into it first. The jejunum is the middle segment and receives chyme only after the duodenum. The ileum is the last segment and ends at the ileocecal junction. The cecum belongs to the large intestine and lies far downstream from the stomach.
  38. The correct answer is D (Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry, used to measure bone mineral density at the spine and hip): Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry measures bone mineral content with very low dose and is the standard screening tool for osteoporosis. CT angiography images blood vessels with contrast and does not quantify bone density. A barium swallow evaluates swallowing and the esophagus. A voiding cystourethrogram studies the lower urinary tract and gives no bone mineral information.
  39. The correct answer is A (Iodinated water-soluble contrast, which is absorbed safely if it leaks outside the bowel): Water-soluble iodinated agents are absorbed and excreted if they escape the bowel, so they are the safe choice when perforation is possible. Barium sulfate does not dissolve and can cause serious peritonitis or adhesions when it leaks. Air alone is a negative agent and cannot show a leak as a dense positive opacity. Gadolinium agents work through magnetic properties for MRI and give no useful x-ray contrast in the bowel.
  40. The correct answer is C (Renal excretion, in which the kidneys filter the agent from blood into urine): Iodinated contrast is filtered by the glomeruli and leaves unchanged in urine, which is why kidney function matters before injection. The liver does not metabolize these agents to any important degree. The lungs are not an excretory route because the molecules are not volatile. Sweat carries away only trace amounts, nowhere near the bulk of the dose.
  41. The correct answer is D (Lateral chest projection, which localizes lesions in depth and shows retrocardiac space): The lateral view adds depth information and shows the spaces in front of the heart and behind it that are hidden on the PA view alone. An AP supine view is for patients who cannot stand and does not add a side perspective. A lordotic view is a special angled view for the apices. A decubitus view is a horizontal-beam view with the patient lying on one side to show fluid or free air.
  42. The correct answer is C (The symphysis pubis, together with the anterior superior iliac spines for confirming symmetry): For an AP pelvis the central ray is centered midway between the anterior superior iliac spines and the symphysis pubis, so those landmarks guide centering and symmetry. The xiphoid process is far too high and is used for chest and upper abdomen work. The iliac crest centers the abdomen and lumbar spine, which would cut off the lower pelvis and proximal femurs. The greater trochanters lie at the sides and cannot place the beam on the midline.
  43. The correct answer is B (Sagittal suture, which joins the two parietal bones along the midline of the skull): The sagittal suture runs along the midline between the two parietal bones. The coronal suture runs side to side across the skull and meets the frontal bone, not the midline junction. The lambdoidal suture lies at the back where the occipital bone meets the parietals. The squamosal suture lies low on the side of the skull between the temporal and parietal bones.
  44. The correct answer is A (Contrast enhancement, where the agent raises attenuation in vessels and organs compared with adjacent tissue): Iodine has a high atomic number and attenuates x-rays strongly, so filled vessels and enhancing organs appear brighter than adjacent tissue, which is contrast enhancement. Quantum mottle is noise from too few photons and is controlled by exposure, not by contrast agent. Scatter attenuation describes the handling of scattered radiation, which is not the purpose of the agent. Beam hardening is a beam-filtration effect that can cause artifacts rather than improve visibility.
  45. The correct answer is C (Osteomalacia, a softening of adult bone caused by defective mineralization of the matrix): Osteomalacia results from poor mineralization of osteoid, most often from vitamin D deficiency, and it softens adult bone and produces pseudofractures. Osteopetrosis is the opposite problem, with overly dense bone from failed resorption. Osteoarthritis is a joint cartilage disease and does not soften the bone matrix. Osteomyelitis is an infection and would present with fever and localized destruction rather than generalized softening.
  46. The correct answer is A (Osteomyelitis, an infection of bone and marrow from blood spread or direct contamination): Osteomyelitis is infection of bone, reached through the bloodstream or by direct spread from a wound or surgery, and a diabetic foot ulcer is a classic setting. Osteoporosis is a metabolic loss of bone mass with no infection involved. Osteochondroma is a benign bony growth and not infectious. Osteosarcoma is a malignant tumour and would not be described as an infection.
  47. The correct answer is D (It confirms positioning, finds retained material, and provides a baseline before contrast): A preliminary image checks that the patient and anatomy are positioned correctly, shows any retained material that could mimic or hide findings, and gives a baseline for comparison with post-contrast images. It cannot replace post-contrast imaging because enhancement is what shows the pathology. Contrast volume depends on patient weight, renal function, and protocol rather than on scout density. History review stays mandatory because allergy and kidney risk are not visible on any image.
  48. The correct answer is B (Asthenic, a slender build with a long narrow thorax and low-lying abdominal organs): Asthenic habitus is the most slender type, with a long narrow trunk and organs that sit low, so more longitudinal receptor coverage may be needed. Hypersthenic habitus is the stocky opposite, with a short wide trunk and high organs. Sthenic habitus is the average build with organs in typical positions. Hyposthenic habitus is only moderately slender, so it is not the extreme long, narrow pattern described.
  49. The correct answer is A (The vertebral column, which forms the central axis of the trunk and protects the cord): The vertebral column is the central support of the trunk and belongs to the axial skeleton along with the skull, ribs, and sternum. The humerus is a limb bone of the arm and is appendicular. The femur is a limb bone of the thigh and is also appendicular. The scapula is part of the pectoral girdle that connects the limb to the trunk, so it is counted with the appendicular skeleton.
  50. The correct answer is B (Periosteal reaction, new bone laid down by the periosteum in response to irritation): Periosteal reaction is new bone formed by the periosteum when it is irritated by infection, injury, or tumour, and it appears as a line parallel to the cortex. Osteolysis is bone destruction and by definition lacks new bone formation. Chondrocalcinosis is calcium in cartilage inside a joint, not along a shaft. Marrow edema is a fluid change seen on MRI and does not produce a visible layer of new bone on radiographs.
  51. The correct answer is C (Synovial joint, a freely movable joint surrounded by a capsule that contains lubricating synovial fluid): Synovial joints have a capsule, synovial fluid, and articular cartilage, which allows free movement, and the knee is a typical example. Fibrous joints are held by dense connective tissue and allow almost no motion. Cartilaginous joints are joined by cartilage and permit only slight movement. A syndesmosis is a type of fibrous joint with limited motion, such as the tibiofibular junction.
  52. The correct answer is D (Proximal, meaning nearer to the origin or point of attachment of the limb): Proximal describes a point nearer the origin of a limb, so the knee is proximal to the ankle. Distal is the opposite and means farther from the attachment. Superior refers to the head end of the body and is not specific to limb attachment. Posterior refers to the back surface and describes front-to-back position rather than distance along a limb.
  53. The correct answer is B (Viscosity, the thickness of the solution and its resistance to flowing through a catheter): Viscosity is resistance to flow, and a thicker agent needs more force through a narrow catheter, so warming the agent can help. Osmolality concerns dissolved particle concentration and affects reaction risk, not flow. Chemotoxicity concerns direct biological harm from the molecule and does not describe thickness. Hydrophilicity describes affinity for water and relates to how well the molecule dissolves, not to its resistance to flow.
  54. The correct answer is C (Comminuted fracture, a break in which the bone splinters into three or more fragments): A comminuted fracture produces three or more fragments, which matches a tibia broken into several pieces. A transverse fracture is a single straight break and gives two fragments. An oblique fracture is a single diagonal line and also gives two fragments. A spiral fracture winds around the shaft from a twisting force and typically yields two main fragments.
  55. The correct answer is A (The alveoli, thin-walled air sacs surrounded by capillaries where oxygen and carbon dioxide are exchanged): Gas exchange happens across the thin walls of the alveoli, where oxygen enters the capillary blood and carbon dioxide leaves it. The trachea is a conducting tube whose cartilage rings keep it open and which has no exchange surface. The bronchi only carry air to the lungs and have thick walls unsuited to diffusion. The larynx protects the airway and produces voice, so it plays no role in exchanging gases.
  56. The correct answer is D (Dislocation, a displacement in which the articulating bone surfaces completely lose contact with each other): A dislocation is a complete loss of contact between joint surfaces, which matches a humeral head sitting outside the glenoid. A subluxation is only a partial displacement and some contact remains. A ligament sprain injures the soft tissue that holds the joint together and does not by itself describe bones out of alignment. A tendon strain injures the muscle-to-bone attachment and is not a joint displacement.
  57. The correct answer is C (Pneumoperitoneum, free air in the peritoneal cavity that rises beneath the diaphragm when upright): Free air in the peritoneal cavity rises to the highest point in an upright patient and forms a lucent crescent under the diaphragm, which is pneumoperitoneum and often signals perforation. A pneumothorax is air above the diaphragm in the pleural space, with a visceral pleural line and absent lung markings. Ascites is fluid and appears hazy rather than lucent. Ileus shows gas inside dilated bowel loops, not free air under the diaphragm.
  58. The correct answer is A (Coronal plane, a vertical plane that divides the body into front and back portions): The coronal plane is vertical and splits the body into anterior and posterior parts. The sagittal plane is also vertical but runs front to back and divides right from left. The transverse plane is horizontal and divides superior from inferior portions. An oblique plane is any angled cut and is not one of the standard planes that defines front versus back.
  59. The correct answer is B (Joint space widening, an abnormally enlarged gap that can signal ligament disruption): A widened joint space means the bones have separated more than normal, which can point to torn stabilizing ligaments. Joint space narrowing is the opposite change and typically reflects cartilage loss. Osteophytes are bony spurs of degenerative disease and do not widen the gap. Subchondral sclerosis is increased density under the cartilage in degenerating joints and does not describe separation of bones.
  60. The correct answer is D (Red bone marrow, the blood-forming tissue found in the spongy bone of the axial skeleton): Red bone marrow contains hematopoietic stem cells and makes red cells, white cells, and platelets, mainly in the spongy bone of the axial skeleton in adults. Yellow marrow is mostly fat and acts as an energy store. Periosteum is a membrane that supports bone growth and repair and does not make blood cells. Articular cartilage covers joint surfaces and has no blood-forming function.
  61. The correct answer is A (Angiography, the injection of contrast directly into a vessel to show its lumen): Angiography places contrast into an artery or vein and images the vessel lumen under fluoroscopy. Lymphangiography targets lymphatic channels, not blood vessels. Myelography targets the subarachnoid space around the spinal cord. Arthrography targets the joint capsule, so none of these three outline the vascular system.
  62. The correct answer is D (Arthrography, which injects contrast to outline the joint capsule, ligaments, and cartilage): Arthrography places contrast into the joint space so the capsule, labrum, ligaments, and cartilage are outlined, as in this shoulder case. Angiography fills blood vessels rather than a joint. Myelography injects into the spinal canal to show the cord and nerve roots. Sialography fills salivary ducts and has nothing to do with joint structures.
  63. The correct answer is C (Anaphylactoid reaction, an allergic-like response that does not depend on a true immune mechanism): An anaphylactoid reaction looks like anaphylaxis but occurs without prior sensitization and without a true IgE-mediated mechanism, and it is not dose dependent. An overdose effect depends on dose, which contradicts the unpredictable pattern here. A pharmacokinetic interaction requires another drug altering clearance. Cumulative toxicity takes repeated exposures over time, not a response within minutes of a first injection.
  64. The correct answer is B (It regulates the release of gastric contents from the stomach into the duodenum): The pyloric sphincter sits between the stomach and duodenum and meters the release of gastric contents into the small bowel. The lower esophageal sphincter, not the pylorus, controls entry of food into the stomach. Bile flow is controlled by the sphincter of Oddi at the duodenal papilla. The ileocecal valve controls passage from the ileum into the large intestine.
  65. The correct answer is D (Sclerosis, abnormally dense bone that absorbs more x-rays and looks opaque): Sclerosis is increased bone density, which absorbs more x-rays and appears whiter on the image. Lucency is the opposite and means decreased density. Osteopenia is a generalized loss of bone mass and makes bone look thinner and more transparent. Erosion is a focal loss of bone, so it creates a defect rather than an opaque region.
  66. The correct answer is B (Prior contrast reactions, known allergies, kidney function, and metformin use): Prior reactions and allergies predict hypersensitivity, kidney function predicts nephropathy risk, and medications such as metformin may need to be held after contrast. Handedness has no bearing on contrast safety. Employment history does not influence reaction or kidney risk. Food preferences are unrelated to allergy or renal status unless a specific allergy is reported, which is a different question.
  67. The correct answer is A (Posterior, meaning toward the back surface of the body, not the front): Posterior describes the back surface of the body, so a finding behind the femoral condyles is posterior. Anterior is the opposite and refers to the front surface. Superior describes a higher position toward the head and does not address front versus back. Inferior describes a lower position toward the feet and likewise does not address front versus back.
  68. The correct answer is C (Repeat the exposure with a correct right marker, since laterality cannot be added afterward): A radiograph without a marker is not a legally valid record of laterality, and a marker added digitally afterward is not acceptable proof of which side was imaged, so the exposure is repeated with a correct marker. Assuming the side from anatomy is unreliable because forearms look very similar. Adding a marker in postprocessing does not document which limb was actually exposed. Sending the image unmarked passes the identification risk to the radiologist and is not acceptable practice.
  69. The correct answer is B (A radiopaque foreign body in the bowel that needs correlation with the clinical history): A well-defined dense object that matches no expected structure is most consistent with a radiopaque foreign body, and it needs correlation with history and notification of the radiologist. The pyloric sphincter is a muscle ring that does not appear as a dense object on plain films. A correctly placed marker sits at the image edge and is easy to identify, so it does not explain an object within the bowel. Gas patterns are lucent, not radiopaque, and an unexplained dense object always deserves reporting.
  70. The correct answer is A (Anatomy and physiology, the study of how the body is built and how it functions): Anatomy covers structure and physiology covers function, so together they form the base for understanding positioning. Pathophysiology studies abnormal function in disease, which builds on normal knowledge rather than replacing it. Pharmacokinetics studies drug handling by the body. Radiobiology studies how radiation affects living tissue, which is a separate subject taught for protection.
  71. The correct answer is D (Overexposure from excessive mAs or kVp for the thickness of the part being imaged): Too much radiation reaching the detector produces a darker image, so overexposure from excessive mAs or kVp fits an overall dark image with lost bony detail. Underexposure sends less radiation to the detector and gives a lighter, noisy image, the opposite of this finding. Grid cutoff causes a localized or one-sided loss of density rather than generalized darkness. Motion blur softens edges and does not change overall density.
  72. The correct answer is C (Radiobiology, the study of how ionizing radiation affects living cells, tissues, and organisms): Radiobiology studies how ionizing radiation affects cells, tissues, and organisms, and it supplies the reasoning behind ALARA and dose limits. Radiopharmacy deals with preparing radioactive medicines and not with tissue effects. Radiochemistry studies the chemistry of radioactive elements rather than biological harm. Radiogenomics studies genetic variation in radiation response, which is narrower than the general study of harmful effects.
  73. The correct answer is A (Report the prior hives and wheezing to the radiologist before any contrast is prepared or injected.): A previous reaction with wheezing is a recognized risk factor for a repeat and possibly worse reaction, so the radiologist must choose premedication, a different agent, or another imaging approach. Giving the full dose assumes allergy fades on a fixed schedule, which cannot be relied on. Lowering the dose has no proven protective effect and hides the history from the person who decides. Cancelling alone denies the patient a possibly needed study without physician input.
  74. The correct answer is D (Stop the injection, call for emergency help, and monitor vital signs while treatment is prepared.): Stridor with lip swelling signals airway involvement from a severe contrast reaction, which can progress within minutes to complete obstruction. The injection must stop at once and the emergency response must be called while vital signs are watched. Slowing the injection still delivers more contrast to a patient already reacting. Waiting and reassuring wastes the short window for treatment, and giving more contrast only worsens the reaction.
  75. The correct answer is B (Adapt the examination to a supine or recumbent position that the patient can tolerate safely.): Patient safety and comfort come before rigid adherence to a routine, and a recumbent cross-table lateral still demonstrates the lumbar spine diagnostically. Forcing an upright stance risks a fall or worsened injury. Cancelling the series ignores the available alternatives and delays diagnosis. Substituting a pelvis view leaves the requested lumbar images missing and the clinical question unanswered.
  76. The correct answer is C (Show the scout to the radiologist and decide together whether to reschedule or modify the study.): Retained stool can hide polyps and other mucosal lesions or mimic them, so an inadequate preparation limits what a barium enema can show. The radiologist is the right person to decide between rescheduling and modifying the study after viewing the scout. Proceeding blindly wastes radiation and barium on a nondiagnostic examination. Cancelling without consultation delays care and removes the physician from a decision that is theirs to make.
  77. The correct answer is D (Confirm pregnancy status if possible, collimate and shield, and discuss risk versus benefit with the radiologist.): Under ALARA, imaging in possible pregnancy requires confirming status where feasible and weighing fetal risk against the clinical benefit of the study. Collimation, shielding and technique adjustment reduce dose while the radiologist helps decide whether to proceed. Refusing outright can deny necessary trauma imaging. Ignoring pregnancy because of the order, or stalling without communication, fails the patient's safety and the team's shared responsibility.
  78. The correct answer is C (Inform the radiologist so contrast dosing and metformin guidance can be settled before the injection.): Metformin combined with contrast-related kidney injury carries a small but recognized risk of lactic acidosis, so reduced renal function needs a physician decision before contrast. The radiologist can set the dose, advise holding metformin, or choose another approach. Proceeding without review ignores a documented risk factor. Telling a patient to stop all diabetes drugs is outside the technologist's scope, and unilateral postponement delays care.
  79. The correct answer is B (Keep the patient supine and immobilized and use a horizontal beam cross-table lateral projection.): Until the spine is cleared, a possible unstable injury demands that the patient stay supine and immobilized, and a cross-table horizontal beam lateral images the cervical spine without any movement. Removing the collar or asking for flexion and extension risks spinal cord damage. Waiting for clearance defeats the purpose, because the imaging is part of how clearance is achieved.
  80. The correct answer is A (Review the aspiration risk with the radiologist, who may modify the agent or the technique used.): Dysphagia and recent aspiration pneumonia mean the patient could inhale contrast, so the radiologist must weigh the risk and may modify the agent or the technique. Proceeding unchanged ignores a documented danger. Cancelling without input delays a possibly needed diagnosis. Substituting tap water on one's own produces a nondiagnostic study and exceeds the technologist's authority over the examination protocol.
  81. The correct answer is C (Work with the bedside nurse to reposition lines and leads safely without disrupting care.): Critically ill patients depend on lines, tubes and monitors that must not be pulled or disconnected without clinical awareness, so the nurse who knows them should help move them. Moving them alone risks dislodgement. Refusing the examination withholds an important study. Exposing without adjustment may hide the very tubes and lung findings the radiograph is meant to show.
  82. The correct answer is B (Pause the study, reassure the patient, and assess for real breathing trouble before going on.): A report of breathing difficulty must be assessed, because it may be simple anxiety or something serious such as aspiration or a vasovagal response. Pausing, reassuring and checking the patient allows that distinction. Continuing ignores a possible emergency. Abandoning the study without assessment misses the chance to help, and technologists cannot give sedatives without an order.
  83. The correct answer is D (Place the receptor carefully around the dressing and drains without pressing on the surgical site.): Dressings and drains belong to active surgical management, so imaging staff must not remove or move them without direction. Careful receptor placement protects the wound and drains while still producing a useful image. Removing the dressing risks infection and wound disruption. Moving drains risks displacement, and cancelling ignores that drains and dressings rarely prevent a diagnostic study.
  84. The correct answer is A (Explain shellfish allergy does not predict contrast reactions, and document allergies for the radiologist.): Current evidence shows shellfish allergy does not specifically predict iodinated contrast reactions, since the old iodine myth is unfounded, although any history of significant allergy still raises general risk. It should be documented and discussed with the radiologist. Claiming certainty of a reaction is false and may cause needless cancellation. Dismissing the concern or skipping documentation breaks safe practice.
  85. The correct answer is B (Hold and tell the radiologist, since severe prior reaction plus asthma needs premedication or another approach.): A prior severe reaction and asthma are each recognized risk factors for another contrast reaction, and together they raise the risk substantially. The radiologist must decide on premedication, a different agent, or an alternative study. Asthma medication gives no reliable protection. Lowering the dose alone is unproven and unauthorized, and an unenhanced urogram is not diagnostic and cannot honestly be reported as complete.
  86. The correct answer is C (Stop the injection, alert the physician, call for help, and follow emergency protocol.): Early signs of a contrast reaction call for immediate action whether or not the physician is in the room, because reactions can escalate quickly. Stopping the injector, alerting the physician, calling for help and starting monitoring follow standard emergency protocol. Waiting passively or letting the injection finish adds to the reaction. Leaving an unattended, reacting patient is unsafe.
  87. The correct answer is D (Pause, use age-appropriate communication, involve the parent, and consider child life support.): Young children often need time, caregiver involvement and age-appropriate explanation or distraction before an invasive procedure, and child life specialists are trained to help. Forced restraint adds trauma and may harm cooperation for the whole study. Cancelling without trying alternatives delays diagnosis. Pushing ahead through visible distress ignores both the child's welfare and the risk of a failed examination.
  88. The correct answer is A (Contact the referring physician or radiologist to clarify the order before starting the swallow.): A recent stroke with suspected dysphagia carries real aspiration risk and may call for a modified barium swallow done with speech-language pathology. Clarifying the order protects the patient and gets the right study done. Following a possibly inappropriate order mechanically risks harm. Cancelling without asking delays care, and changing the examination without authorization exceeds the technologist's scope.
  89. The correct answer is D (Explain that very little contrast enters milk and guidance does not require stopping breastfeeding.): Only a tiny fraction of iodinated contrast enters breast milk, and the infant absorbs very little of that, so current guidance does not require interrupting breastfeeding. Mothers who remain worried can discuss it with the radiologist. Telling her to stop for weeks or discard milk for a week overstates the risk. Calling contrast universally forbidden is simply incorrect.
  90. The correct answer is A (Stop the injection, assess the swelling, and notify the physician per facility protocol.): Swelling and increasing pain at the site during injection are classic signs of extravasation, where contrast leaks into soft tissue. Stopping immediately limits the volume that escapes, and assessment and physician notification allow proper management. Continuing or speeding up the injection forces more contrast into tissue and risks compartment syndrome or skin injury. Ignoring the patient's pain overlooks the key warning symptom.
  91. The correct answer is B (Keep trying de-escalation and bring in nursing or physician support to agree on a safe plan.): Managing a combative patient with possible head injury involves decisions about sedation or restraint that belong to the clinical team, so the technologist should keep de-escalating and call for help. Applying restraints alone creates safety and legal risk. Scanning through motion produces nondiagnostic images and wastes radiation. Cancelling without help may delay care for a patient who may need urgent imaging.
  92. The correct answer is C (Check the manufacturer's guidance for CT use and follow facility protocol for external devices.): Insulin pumps can be damaged by CT radiation or interfere with imaging, and manufacturers publish guidance on whether the pump must be removed, moved or shielded. Following that guidance and the facility protocol protects both the device and the patient. Removing the pump without guidance interrupts insulin delivery. Scanning without thought risks device malfunction, and cancelling for any device is excessive.
  93. The correct answer is A (Use latex-free supplies throughout the procedure and make sure the care team knows about the allergy.): Latex allergy can cause serious reactions from direct or indirect contact with gloves, catheters, tourniquets and tubing, so every item must be latex-free and everyone involved informed. Latex allergy is not related to contrast choice. It applies in any setting where latex touches the patient, not only in surgery. Changing the contrast agent leaves the real exposure source unaddressed.
  94. The correct answer is C (Stop the instillation and tell the radiologist, since sudden severe pain may mean perforation.): Mild cramping is common, but sudden severe pain during instillation may signal bowel perforation, which is an emergency. Stopping at once limits barium leaking into the peritoneum, and the radiologist must be told immediately. Continuing or speeding up the instillation worsens any leak. Calling severe pain normal without informing the physician delays urgent diagnosis and treatment.
  95. The correct answer is D (Inform the supervising physician first, because anticoagulant use affects the safety of the planned puncture.): Anticoagulants raise the risk of bleeding, including spinal hematoma, after a needle puncture of the spinal canal, so the physician must decide whether to proceed, delay or adjust. Telling the patient to stop a drug is outside the technologist's scope and could cause harm. Proceeding first and documenting afterward removes any chance to prevent the complication.
  96. The correct answer is B (Stop injecting, reassess catheter position and comfort, and discuss the findings with the physician first.): Resistance with discomfort can mean a misplaced catheter, ductal obstruction or another problem, and forcing contrast risks rupturing the duct. Stopping to reassess the catheter and the patient, then consulting the physician, is the safe course. Increasing pressure or continuing at the same pressure could injure the duct. Ignoring reported pain disregards the patient's main warning sign.
  97. The correct answer is C (Review alternative positioning or imaging with the radiologist that respects the cardiac limitation.): A patient with cardiac orthopnea can develop serious respiratory distress when forced flat, so this is a real safety issue. The radiologist can approve raised positioning, modified views or another modality that still answers the clinical question. Forcing the position endangers the patient. Cancelling without discussion ignores feasible alternatives, and ignoring symptoms breaches basic patient care.
  98. The correct answer is B (Hold the examination and confirm pregnancy status by testing, given the possible fetal risk.): Hysterosalpingography exposes the pelvis to radiation and involves instrumenting the uterine cavity, both of which could harm an early pregnancy. When status is uncertain, standard practice requires confirming it with testing before proceeding. Relying on the patient's estimate is unreliable, and waiting for complications means the fetus has already been exposed.
  99. The correct answer is A (Speak up immediately to clarify the injection site against the requisition before the procedure continues.): Every team member shares responsibility for patient safety, and a mismatch between the planned site and the order must be resolved before the needle goes in. Raising it during the time-out or verification step is expected practice. Silence, or documenting afterward, allows a wrong-site procedure to happen. Assuming a clerical error without checking is exactly the kind of assumption that causes harm.
  100. The correct answer is D (Document the reaction, treatment and outcome in the patient record and flag it for future visits.): Complete documentation of the reaction, its severity, the treatment and the outcome protects the patient at future visits and meets legal and facility standards. Flagging the allergy lets later teams plan premedication or alternatives. Omitting the reaction hides a known risk, and verbal handoff alone is unreliable and leaves no record. Recovery does not remove the need to record what happened.
  101. The correct answer is D (An oral corticosteroid with an antihistamine given at set intervals in the hours before injection.): The accepted approach for a prior mild reaction is a staged oral corticosteroid with an antihistamine, begun hours before the scan so the steroid has time to act. A single dose just before injection is too late to be effective. Epinephrine is a treatment for reactions, not routine prevention. Higher-osmolality ionic agents cause more reactions, not fewer.
  102. The correct answer is B (That the catheter is rated for power injection at the flow rate planned for this scan.): Not every PICC is power-rated, and forcing high-flow contrast through a catheter that is not can rupture it and cause extravasation or embolism. The rating and the planned flow rate must match before connection. Catheter age, fasting status and the type of flush do not determine whether the line can tolerate the pressure and flow of a power injector.
  103. The correct answer is A (Stop the infusion at once and notify the radiologist without delay about the pain and resistance.): Sudden pain with resistance can indicate perforation or obstruction, so instillation must stop immediately and the radiologist must be told. Raising the bag height or continuing to fill risks forcing barium into the peritoneal cavity, a serious complication. Repositioning and resuming, or medicating and continuing, both delay recognition of a possible emergency.
  104. The correct answer is C (Proceed using the lowest diagnostic contrast dose as directed by the radiologist.): When a study is urgent and the physicians accept the renal risk, the lowest diagnostic dose is used, because untreated ischemia is a greater immediate danger than contrast-related kidney injury. Dialysis is not a prerequisite. Gadolinium is not a substitute for iodinated agents at CT and carries its own risks. Altering hydration without physician input bypasses the radiologist.
  105. The correct answer is A (That renal function has been checked, since the patient takes metformin.): Metformin together with contrast-related kidney injury can lead to lactic acidosis, so renal function must be confirmed before contrast, with a plan to hold metformin afterward if function is reduced. An extra dose would add risk, not safety. Fasting duration is unrelated to this concern, and a rigid one-week stop is not the standard requirement.
  106. The correct answer is C (Ask the patient to roll the shoulders forward and rest the hands on the hips.): Rotating the shoulders forward with the hands on the hips moves the scapulae laterally out of the lung fields, which is the standard PA chest position. Hands behind the head or arms raised overhead do not reliably clear the scapulae and may rotate the patient. Pressing the forearms against the receptor leaves the scapulae over the lungs and suits other projections.
  107. The correct answer is B (The patient was rotated, with the torso and shoulders turned away from a true frontal position.): Unequal distances from the clavicular heads to the spinous processes are the classic sign of rotation, because a properly positioned patient places them symmetrically about midline. Inspiration affects lung aeration and rib counts, and kilovoltage affects contrast, not lateral symmetry. True thoracic asymmetry is uncommon and should be considered only after rotation has been excluded.
  108. The correct answer is C (A horizontal beam cross-table lateral taken with the patient remaining supine on the backboard.): A cross-table horizontal beam lateral images the cervical spine without moving a patient who may have an unstable injury. An erect lateral requires sitting up, which is unsafe before clearance. A swimmer's lateral as described needs the patient to move an arm and stand. An angled oblique does not provide the true lateral required.
  109. The correct answer is C (Rotation of the forearm away from a true anteroposterior position during the exposure.): On a true anteroposterior forearm, the radius and ulna lie side by side with little overlap, so unexpected crossing and distorted central landmarks indicate the limb was rotated. A long source-to-image distance only reduces magnification and density. Underexposure makes the whole image too light without changing bone relationships, and grid cutoff produces lost density or grid lines, not overlap.
  110. The correct answer is B (The central ray was not perpendicular to the centre of the grid, causing off-level cutoff.): Grid cutoff with density loss toward one edge results from an angled or off-centre central ray or a tilted receptor, so the grid absorbs primary beam on that side. Distance errors and low mAs change overall density rather than producing a one-sided pattern with grid lines. Patient motion causes blur of structures, not a gradient of density across the image.
  111. The correct answer is C (A scaphoid view taken with the wrist positioned in ulnar deviation for the exposure.): Ulnar deviation lengthens the scaphoid and separates it from overlapping carpal bones, so a suspected scaphoid fracture is better demonstrated. A carpal tunnel view shows the carpal arch, not the scaphoid waist. An elbow oblique does not image the wrist, and a PA wrist with flat fingers repeats the routine view that shows the scaphoid foreshortened.
  112. The correct answer is A (Type II, a fracture through the physis with a metaphyseal fragment attached.): Salter-Harris Type II passes through the physis and exits through a triangular metaphyseal fragment, and it is the most common pattern in children. Type I is a pure physeal slip with no fragment. Type III runs through the physis into the epiphysis and joint surface, and Type IV crosses all three zones, including the epiphysis, which is intact here.
  113. The correct answer is C (A greenstick fracture in which one cortex breaks while the other cortex bends.): A greenstick fracture occurs in the pliable bone of children, where one cortex breaks under tension while the opposite cortex bends without complete disruption. Spiral and comminuted fractures involve complete cortical disruption, with a twisting line or multiple fragments. A pathologic fracture occurs through diseased bone, which would show an underlying lesion.
  114. The correct answer is A (A modified cross-table lateral hip with the uninjured leg raised and held out of the beam.): When internal rotation is painful, a cross-table lateral with the uninjured leg raised avoids manipulating the injured hip and still gives a true lateral projection. Frog-leg positioning requires abduction and external rotation of the injured hip, which can displace a fracture. Forced internal rotation is the very movement the patient cannot tolerate, and a steep oblique is not a standard hip view.
  115. The correct answer is C (Osteoporosis, defined by a T-score of negative 2.5 or lower at the site.): A T-score at or below negative 2.5 meets the WHO definition of osteoporosis, comparing the patient's bone mineral density with a young adult reference population. Osteopenia lies between negative 1.0 and negative 2.5, and normal density is negative 1.0 or higher. Osteoarthritis is a joint disease and is not classified by a DEXA T-score.
  116. The correct answer is A (To include the most breast tissue, including the upper outer portion and axillary tail.): Parallel angulation to the pectoral muscle lets the oblique view include the greatest amount of tissue, especially the upper outer quadrant and axillary tail where pathology is common. It does not lower dose meaningfully, and it cannot replace the craniocaudal view, which shows different tissue. Sternal visualization is not the aim of the oblique view.
  117. The correct answer is C (Consider repeating the view because the positioning criteria for adequate posterior tissue were not met.): An adequate mediolateral oblique view shows the pectoral muscle reaching about the level of the nipple, which confirms that enough posterior tissue was included. Falling short suggests tissue was missed, so the view should be repeated with better positioning. The muscle is a recognized landmark, reduced compression does not fix depth, and spot compression does not replace a complete standard view.
  118. The correct answer is D (Using pulsed fluoroscopy at the lowest pulse rate that still maintains diagnostic image quality.): Pulsed fluoroscopy acquires fewer frames per second than continuous mode, so cumulative dose falls while motility can still be assessed.
