A shorter source-to-skin distance, continuous mode and removal of filtration all raise skin dose. Filtration removes low-energy photons that add dose without improving the image, so taking it away is counterproductive.
  119. The correct answer is C (It shows the most recent fluoroscopic frame on screen without any further radiation exposure.): Last image hold freezes the final acquired fluoroscopic frame on the monitor, so findings can be reviewed and discussed without more radiation to the patient or staff. It does not archive the entire sequence or change the frame rate. It also cannot replace a dedicated spot radiograph, which is acquired at higher quality for the permanent record.
  120. The correct answer is B (It keeps image quality consistent while lowering dose in thinner regions of the body.): Automatic tube current modulation changes the mA along the z-axis and around the patient, giving less current through thin regions like the pelvis and more through thick regions, which lowers dose while preserving image quality. It relies on the scout image rather than replacing it. It adjusts current, not kilovoltage, and it is independent of iterative reconstruction.
  121. The correct answer is D (About sixty to seventy seconds after the contrast injection has started.): The portal venous phase best shows hepatic parenchymal enhancement and many focal liver lesions, and it is usually acquired about sixty to seventy seconds after injection begins. The arterial phase comes earlier, around twenty to twenty-five seconds. An acquisition in the first five seconds precedes any enhancement, and ten minutes is a much later delayed phase.
  122. The correct answer is C (Stop the injection, remove the IV, and apply a compress per protocol.): For a mild extravasation, the injection is stopped, the cannula is removed, and the limb is elevated with a warm or cold compress according to departmental protocol while the patient is watched for worsening. Continuing would force more contrast into the tissue. Tight circumferential pressure may increase tissue injury, and epinephrine is not indicated for a local leak without an allergic reaction.
  123. The correct answer is A (A shellfish allergy alone is not a reliable predictor of a reaction to iodinated contrast.): Studies show that shellfish allergy is a reaction to shellfish proteins, not to iodine, so it carries no special risk beyond that of any other allergy. Contrast screening focuses on prior contrast reactions, asthma and multiple allergies. Iodine in contrast is bound in an organic molecule that is unrelated to shellfish allergens. Penicillin allergy is also not a specific predictor, and cancelling the study on this history alone is unjustified.
  124. The correct answer is B (To reduce the risk of aspiration if nausea or vomiting occurs after contrast.): Nausea and vomiting are recognized effects of iodinated contrast, and an empty stomach lowers the chance of aspirating gastric contents. Fasting does not change blood glucose in any useful way for this study. Renal distension depends on hydration and contrast excretion, not fasting. Bowel gas is reduced by separate bowel preparation, and fasting alone cannot clear it from the images.
  125. The correct answer is C (Notify the radiologist immediately and prepare to give an antihistamine as directed.): Hives with wheezing are more than a trivial reaction, so the radiologist must be called immediately to assess and direct treatment. An antihistamine is commonly ordered, with close observation of airway and vital signs. Sending the patient home or continuing the scan ignores a possible progression to anaphylaxis. Waiting passively delays treatment during the period when the reaction can worsen quickly.
  126. The correct answer is D (A water-soluble, low-osmolar iodinated agent that is used in place of the barium.): After aspiration, a water-soluble low-osmolar iodinated agent is preferred because it is absorbed from the lungs and does not persist in the airway. Barium, whatever its concentration, can remain in the bronchi and provoke inflammation. A high-osmolar ionic agent draws fluid into the lungs and can cause pulmonary edema if aspirated. Air alone cannot outline the swallowing mechanism or the esophageal lumen.
  127. The correct answer is C (Bolus tracking with a region of interest placed over the main pulmonary artery.): Bolus tracking monitors enhancement in the pulmonary trunk and triggers the scan once a set threshold is reached, so peak opacification of the pulmonary arteries is captured for each patient. A fixed delay of five minutes would miss the arterial phase entirely. Injecting contrast after scanning produces no enhancement on the images. Portal venous timing is far too late for assessing the pulmonary arteries for emboli.
  128. The correct answer is B (Consult the radiologist to weigh risk and benefit and consider alternative imaging.): A history of severe reactions together with asthma places the patient at high risk, so the radiologist must weigh that risk against the urgency and consider imaging that avoids iodinated contrast. Giving contrast with no modification risks a repeat severe reaction. A single steroid dose just before injection is not an effective prophylaxis and bypasses medical judgment. Barium is an oral agent and cannot opacify the vessels.
  129. The correct answer is D (The elevated risk of nephrogenic systemic fibrosis when clearance is reduced.): Severely reduced renal function slows gadolinium clearance and raises the risk of nephrogenic systemic fibrosis, particularly with some agents, so the radiologist must judge whether contrast is needed and which agent is safest. Gadolinium contains no iodine. A prolonged fast has no bearing on this problem. Barium is not an MRI contrast agent and could not be substituted.
  130. The correct answer is A (Good hydration promotes contrast excretion and improves distension of the ureters for evaluation.): Adequate hydration supports renal clearance of contrast and helps distend the collecting system and ureters with opacified urine, which improves visualization of the urinary tract. Dose is set by scan parameters, not by fluid intake. Hydration does not prevent the urge to void. The delayed phase is still required, because it is the phase that actually demonstrates the excreted contrast.
  131. The correct answer is D (An AP oblique shoulder view, which is also known as the Grashey projection.): The Grashey view rotates the patient so the beam runs parallel to the glenohumeral joint, opening the joint space and separating the humeral head from the glenoid. A standard AP view leaves the two overlapped. An axillary view is taken with the arm abducted, so full adduction defeats it. Imaging the uninjured shoulder shows nothing about the injured joint.
  132. The correct answer is A (Obtain a swimmer's lateral view to demonstrate the cervicothoracic junction.): The seventh cervical vertebra must be visualized in trauma because injuries commonly occur at the cervicothoracic junction. A swimmer's lateral, with one arm raised and the other depressed, separates the shoulders from that region. Accepting an incomplete study risks a missed unstable injury. Repeating the same projection gives the same result, and an open mouth view shows only the upper cervical spine.
  133. The correct answer is B (To opacify the bowel loops and help separate them from an inflamed appendix.): Oral contrast fills the small and large bowel, so normal loops appear bright while an inflamed appendix and any surrounding abscess remain distinct. It does not replace or reduce the intravenous dose, which enhances vessels and inflamed tissue. The lung bases are not a purpose of the protocol. Bowel opacification is the specific reason oral contrast is included for this indication.
  134. The correct answer is D (About five to ten minutes so that any free air can rise beneath the diaphragm.): Free air needs several minutes in an upright position to travel to the highest point under the diaphragm, where small amounts become visible. A thirty second wait is too brief for small collections. Waiting is clearly required, so no specific time is wrong. A full hour is excessive and impractical for an acutely ill patient.
  135. The correct answer is D (Multiple air-fluid levels in dilated small bowel loops at differing heights.): In obstruction, gas and fluid collect proximal to the blockage, producing dilated small bowel loops with several air-fluid levels, often at different heights, on an erect view. A single gastric level is a normal finding. Even gas through the large bowel suggests no obstruction. A gasless abdomen points to other problems, such as fluid-filled loops or a different diagnosis.
  136. The correct answer is B (A tangential, or sunrise, view obtained with the knee flexed and the beam directed axially.): The tangential or sunrise projection flexes the knee and sends the beam along the patellofemoral joint, so the patella is seen in the axial plane. This shows vertical fractures and the femoral groove clearly. A standard AP view leaves the patella overlapped by the femur. The tunnel view targets the intercondylar notch, and a straight leg lateral is not designed to profile the patellar surface.
  137. The correct answer is C (Using a single long image receptor so that the whole spine is captured together.): A single long receptor records the entire spine in one exposure, avoiding the stitching error and distortion that make Cobb angle measurements unreliable. Supine positioning removes the effect of gravity and can understate the curve. The iliac crests should be included to establish the lower limit of the curve. Very high kilovoltage lowers contrast, which does not help measurement.
  138. The correct answer is B (Selecting the lowest pulse rate that still allows adequate visualization of the movement.): Reducing the pulse or frame rate lowers the number of exposures per second, which directly cuts cumulative patient and staff dose while still showing motion adequately. Removing filtration lets more low-energy photons reach the skin, which increases dose. A larger field exposes more tissue unnecessarily. Continuous fluoroscopy delivers more radiation than pulsed mode, so it raises dose.
  139. The correct answer is C (The timing makes it unlikely that the patient is pregnant at the time of the exam.): Hysterosalpingography is done in the first part of the cycle after bleeding stops and before ovulation, so pregnancy is very unlikely and radiation to a developing embryo is avoided. Scheduling is therefore directly affected by menstrual history. The timing shown is appropriate, so rescheduling is not needed. Osmolality is chosen on other grounds, not on cycle day.
  140. The correct answer is A (Retained barium can obscure the sonographic visualization of the abdominal structures.): Barium reflects sound strongly and produces acoustic shadowing, so the pancreas, gallbladder and other deep structures may be hidden behind it. For this reason barium studies are scheduled after ultrasound, or the barium must clear first. Ultrasound uses no ionizing radiation, so dose is unaffected. The equipment itself works normally.
  141. The correct answer is A (To screen for life-threatening injuries while limiting movement of an unstable patient.): The three views quickly screen the cervical spine, chest and pelvis, which are the regions where unrecognized injuries are most immediately dangerous. They are obtained with the patient supine, so spinal precautions are maintained. The series does not replace CT, which is often still required. Billing is not a clinical rationale, and the series adds images rather than reducing them.
  142. The correct answer is C (Obtain two partially flexed AP projections, one for the humerus and one for the forearm.): When the elbow cannot be extended, two AP projections are taken, one with the humerus parallel to the receptor and one with the forearm parallel, so neither part is foreshortened. Forcing the joint can worsen a fracture or dislocation. Cancelling the series leaves the injury unimaged. A prone flexed position is not a standard approach and increases risk.
  143. The correct answer is B (Contrast can obscure the appearance of a calculus within the collecting system.): Most stones are dense and stand out clearly against urine and soft tissue on a non-contrast scan. Contrast within the collecting system becomes equally bright and can hide or mimic a stone. Contrast is not a universal contraindication in flank pain, and it does not change the radiation dose of a single phase. It is not limited to children, and a non-contrast scan is also standard in adults.
  144. The correct answer is D (A neutral low-density oral agent that distends bowel without hiding the wall.): A neutral low-attenuation agent, such as water with a thickening additive, distends the lumen while allowing the enhancing bowel wall to be seen clearly after intravenous injection. A positive agent would be as bright as the enhancing wall and conceal inflammation. Barium has the same masking effect. Without distension, collapsed loops cannot be assessed reliably for wall thickening.
  145. The correct answer is B (It is a common, expected effect of the contrast and usually resolves quickly.): A brief sensation of warmth, sometimes with a metallic taste, is a common physiologic effect of iodinated contrast caused by vasodilation and osmolality, and it fades within a minute or so. It is not an allergic reaction when no other symptoms are present. The sensation has no link to the injection rate needing to change. It occurs commonly with iodinated agents and not mainly with gadolinium.
  146. The correct answer is A (Notify the medical physicist or service engineer and hold the scanner from clinical use.): A water value outside tolerance shows the scanner calibration is wrong, which can alter CT numbers used in diagnosis, so the physicist or service engineer must assess and correct it. The unit is held from clinical use until it passes. Continuing to scan spreads unreliable values across patient studies. Recalibration by the technologist is outside their role, and changing kilovoltage does not correct the calibration.
  147. The correct answer is D (Verify the exact device model's MRI conditional status with the manufacturer or documentation.): MRI safety depends on the exact pacemaker and lead system, and many devices are conditional on field strength, programming and other limits. The technologist therefore confirms the model against the documentation or manufacturer and involves the radiologist and device team. Assuming all devices are safe risks serious harm. Automatic cancellation may deny a possible scan, and substituting a test without informing the patient breaches consent.
  148. The correct answer is B (Not enough posterior breast tissue was drawn forward into the image.): On a mediolateral oblique view the inframammary fold is lifted and opened when the posterior and inferior tissue has been drawn forward, so a closed fold means that tissue was left behind. The remedy is to raise the fold, lift the breast and reposition. Compression force, exposure factors and receptor height affect other qualities of the image but are not what this sign indicates.
  149. The correct answer is B (A thin coating of barium sulfate followed by insufflated air or carbon dioxide.): The double-contrast technique pairs a thin mucosal coating of barium, which is positive contrast, with gas distension, which is negative contrast. The gas stretches the colon wall and the barium outlines small polyps and mucosal detail. A single dense column of barium would hide small lesions. Water-soluble iodinated contrast with gadolinium is not used this way, and oral barium alone does not fill the colon adequately.
  150. The correct answer is A (To check technique and bowel preparation and to screen for radiopaque calculi.): The scout lets the technologist check exposure factors and positioning, and the radiologist check that bowel gas or stool will not hide the kidneys. It also shows radiopaque stones that could later be hidden by contrast. Contrast volume is based on patient weight and protocol, not the scout. Intravenous access is confirmed clinically, and delayed images are still required to show excretion.
  151. The correct answer is B (Wood is often radiolucent, so soft tissue technique or another modality may be needed.): Organic material such as wood has low density and is frequently radiolucent or only faintly seen, so the technologist may need a lower soft tissue technique and additional projections. Ultrasound or CT can be suggested to the radiologist when the radiograph is negative. Wood does not match metal in density. A bone technique with high kilovoltage burns out faint soft tissue contrast, making wood harder to see.
  152. The correct answer is B (Suboptimal inspiration effort by the patient at the moment of exposure.): Poor inspiratory effort commonly elevates the diaphragm and reduces apparent lung volume on a chest radiograph, mimicking or obscuring true pathology, and should be considered a technical cause before assuming a purely clinical explanation. Distance, grid ratio, and marker placement do not directly produce this specific appearance.
  153. The correct answer is C (Using thin reconstructed slices together with a high-spatial-frequency reconstruction algorithm.): High-resolution lung CT depends on thin slices, about one millimetre, and a sharp bone-type reconstruction kernel, which together resolve the fine interstitial detail of fibrosis. Thick slices average small structures together and hide them. The scout is needed to plan the scan range. A field of view limited to the mediastinum would exclude the lung periphery where fibrosis is usually found.
  154. The correct answer is D (The voiding phase, which is imaged while the patient is actively urinating.): Reflux is often shown during voiding, when pressure inside the bladder rises and forces contrast back up the ureters. The scout shows no contrast at all. Imaging only during filling can miss reflux that appears at higher pressures. A next-day image is not part of the examination, and by then the contrast would have been excreted.
  155. The correct answer is D (Flag the soft tissue density for radiologist review before discharge.): An unexpected soft tissue density that does not correspond to normal anatomy warrants prompt radiologist review before the patient is discharged, since it may represent a clinically significant mass requiring further workup. Correct positioning and marker placement do not explain away an abnormal finding, and repeating the exposure with different technique or positioning would not resolve a true soft tissue abnormality.
  156. The correct answer is B (That the obturator foramina and the iliac wings look symmetric on both sides.): Symmetry of the obturator foramina and iliac wings shows the pelvis is not rotated or tilted, and acetabular angles measured on a rotated pelvis are unreliable. Arm position does not affect the pelvic anatomy. Grid use depends on body thickness and has no bearing on symmetry. Breath-holding is irrelevant to a pelvic image, unlike a chest or abdominal image.
  157. The correct answer is A (The leg is internally rotated about fifteen to twenty degrees from the AP position.): Internal rotation of about fifteen to twenty degrees brings the intermalleolar line parallel to the image receptor, which opens the tibiotalar joint space evenly. This is why the mortise view is the best projection for judging the joint space. External rotation of forty-five degrees gives an oblique view. A neutral AP does not align the malleoli, and plantar flexion changes the foot rather than the joint alignment.
  158. The correct answer is D (Bolus tracking with the region of interest placed within the descending aorta.): Bolus tracking measures enhancement in the descending aorta and triggers the scan when a preset threshold is reached, so acquisition matches each patient's circulation time. Cardiac output varies widely, so a fixed delay will often miss the peak. Starting the injection after scanning begins leaves the early images without enhancement. Venous phase timing is too late to show the arterial lumen and the intimal flap.
  159. The correct answer is C (Use latex-free tourniquets, gloves and injection supplies for the study.): Latex allergy is a reaction to natural rubber products, not to the contrast agent, so latex is removed from everything that touches the patient, including gloves, tourniquets and some syringe parts. Cancelling the study is unnecessary. Standard equipment may contain latex and could trigger a reaction. Barium is an oral agent and cannot opacify vessels.
  160. The correct answer is C (It increases entrance skin dose, because the system needs a higher dose rate for the field.): In magnification mode the system samples a smaller part of the input phosphor and raises the dose rate to keep the image bright and sharp, so entrance skin dose increases. A smaller field of view does not mean less radiation at the skin. Dose is clearly affected, so no change is wrong. Patient dose rises first, and staff exposure from scatter rises with it.
  161. The correct answer is B (Notify the radiologist and continue to monitor the patient's condition closely.): Mild nausea and headache can be a minor adverse reaction to gadolinium, so the technologist informs the radiologist and keeps watching for progression to hives or breathing problems. Sending the patient home without assessment is unsafe. Medication is given only on the direction of the radiologist or physician. Assuming the reaction is unrelated ignores a possible early sign.
  162. The correct answer is A (A cross-table lateral projection using a horizontal beam through the hip.): A cross-table lateral keeps the patient supine and the injured leg still, while the horizontal beam passes through the femoral neck to give a true lateral view. Forced rotation of the leg can displace a fracture. Imaging the opposite hip shows nothing about the injury, and standing to bear weight is unsafe with a suspected fracture.
  163. The correct answer is C (Respiratory motion occurring while the scan data were being acquired.): Breathing motion during acquisition is the common cause of blurring and streaking at the lung bases, where movement of the diaphragm is greatest. Better breath-hold instructions or a faster scan reduce it. Window settings change the display and cannot add streaks. Beam hardening arises beside dense bone or metal, and a detector calibration fault would cause ring artifacts, which are not described here.
  164. The correct answer is C (A skeletal survey consisting of radiographs of several regions of the body.): A skeletal survey combines views of the skull, spine, ribs, pelvis and long bones to find the punched-out lytic lesions typical of myeloma throughout the skeleton. One chest film or one bone shows only a small portion of the body. A barium study examines the bowel and gives no information about the bone marrow disease.
  165. The correct answer is A (The neck region of the classic Scottie dog outline seen on this view.): The pars interarticularis forms the neck of the Scottie dog, and a defect there, called spondylolysis, looks like a collar on the dog. The transverse process forms the nose and the pedicle forms the eye. The superior articular process forms the ear and the inferior articular process forms the front leg.
  166. The correct answer is A (Repeat the exposure with a marker matching the limb that was imaged.): A side marker that does not match the limb actually imaged is a patient safety and medico-legal concern, so the correct response is to recognize the error and repeat the exposure with the accurate marker in place rather than accept it or correct it after the fact. Digitally altering a marker after acquisition is not an acceptable substitute for correct marker use at the time of exposure.
  167. The correct answer is D (Lower kilovoltage is used in order to increase bone contrast on the image.): A rib series uses a lower kilovoltage than a chest radiograph so that bone contrast increases and cortical fracture lines become easier to see, since the aim is the bones rather than the lung. Higher kilovoltage flattens contrast and can hide a subtle fracture. Doubling the distance is not standard and increases the exposure needed. Position depends on the site of injury and not on recumbency alone.
  168. The correct answer is A (A pleural effusion at the affected lung base.): A homogeneous density with a meniscus-shaped upper border and blunting of the costophrenic angle is the classic radiographic appearance of a pleural effusion layering within the pleural space. A rotation artifact, grid cutoff, and motion blur each produce distinct, different appearances and would not create this specific meniscus sign.
  169. The correct answer is A (Expiration makes a small pneumothorax appear relatively larger as the lung volume decreases.): On expiration the lung shrinks and becomes denser while the air in the pleural space stays the same, so the pneumothorax occupies a larger proportion of the hemithorax and contrasts better with the lung. Dose is not reduced by the breathing phase. Vascular markings are better seen on full inspiration. Expiration is not a mandatory requirement, and some centres prefer upright inspiratory images alone.
  170. The correct answer is A (A delayed scan several minutes after injection through an upper extremity vein.): Indirect CT venography relies on contrast that has circulated through the arteries, capillaries and back into the venous system, so scanning is delayed by roughly two to three minutes or longer. Arterial phase timing would show arteries but leave the leg veins unopacified. Direct injection into a foot vein describes a different, direct technique. Scanning with no delay would image the chest before contrast reached the legs.
  171. The correct answer is D (Spot compression views, with or without magnification, of the area of concern.): Spot compression spreads and thins the tissue under a small paddle, so overlapping normal tissue separates, while magnification gives finer detail. This helps decide whether an asymmetry is a true mass. Repeating the screening views adds nothing. A skeletal survey and a barium study do not examine breast tissue and have no role at this stage of workup.
  172. The correct answer is D (A narrow window optimized for soft tissue contrast within the brain.): A narrow window spreads a small range of CT numbers across the grey scale, which enhances the slight density difference between grey and white matter, so early ischemia such as loss of grey-white differentiation is seen. A wide bone window flattens brain contrast. Lung and liver windows are set for tissues with very different densities and are unsuitable for the brain.
  173. The correct answer is C (That the force falls within the manufacturer's specified and regulatory acceptable range.): Compression testing confirms the unit delivers force within the specified range, because too little force reduces image quality and increases dose, while too much causes pain and possible harm. Maximum force is not used on every patient, as it is adjusted to tolerance. Testing is repeated on a regular schedule, and compression clearly affects both sharpness and dose.
  174. The correct answer is A (A pleural effusion layering along the affected lung base.): A curved meniscus-shaped density that blunts the costophrenic angle and obscures the diaphragmatic contour is characteristic of a pleural effusion, which layers dependently within the pleural space due to gravity. Overexposure, retained clothing artifact, and underexposure each produce a different, generalized appearance rather than this focal meniscus pattern.
  175. The correct answer is D (Follow-up scans should ideally be performed on the same equipment so that results are comparable.): Different manufacturers calibrate their systems differently, so the same scanner and facility give the most reliable comparison of bone mineral density change over time. Moving to another facility adds variability and can create false changes. The T-score is the usual measure in older adults, and the Z-score is reserved for younger patients, premenopausal women and children.
  176. The correct answer is D (A radiopaque foreign body embedded within the adjacent lateral soft tissue.): A small, sharply marginated, densely radiopaque object overlying soft tissue that does not correspond to any normal bony structure most likely represents a radiopaque foreign body embedded in the soft tissue rather than a skeletal finding. An accessory ossicle would have a smooth corticated margin and typical location, and a grid artifact would not appear as a focal sharply defined object.
  177. The correct answer is A (Beam hardening and photon starvation caused by the metal clip.): Metal absorbs low-energy photons and starves the detectors of signal along some projection paths, so the reconstruction creates the typical dark and bright streaks around the clip. Patient motion causes blurring or double contours instead. Window and level settings change only the display of the image. Poor bowel opacification would affect the bowel but would not produce streaks radiating from a clip.
  178. The correct answer is D (Residual stool can mimic or hide polyps and other mucosal abnormalities within the colon.): A clean colon lets the barium coat the mucosa evenly, whereas residual stool can look like a polyp or mass and can hide a real lesion. Preparation has no effect on fluoroscopy dose. It is needed for adults who have the examination and is not limited to young patients. A clean bowel helps rather than prevents mucosal coating, which is the goal of the preparation.
  179. The correct answer is A (The neck is hyperextended so the vertex of the skull rests on the receptor.): Hyperextending the neck brings the vertex onto the receptor so the beam can pass upward through the chin toward the skull base, profiling the base and zygomatic arches. A tucked chin points the face toward the receptor and produces a different view. A true lateral head shows the sagittal profile instead. A neutral AP head with a small angle cannot reach the skull base from below.
  180. The correct answer is B (It allows real-time fluoroscopic guidance of the needle into the interlaminar space.): Lying prone lets the beam pass through the spine from behind so the radiologist can watch the needle and aim for the interlaminar space with fewer passes. Contrast volume depends on the study protocol rather than on patient position. Local anesthetic is still given for comfort whatever the position. Dose is managed with pulsed fluoroscopy and collimation, not by lying prone.
  181. The correct answer is D (Rotation of the pelvis away from a true AP position during exposure.): When the pelvis is rotated, one iliac wing is foreshortened while the other is broadened, so the wings and obturator foramina look unequal. High exposure darkens the whole image without changing symmetry. Wide collimation adds scatter and exposes extra tissue but does not change the shape of the bones. A tilted grid causes a loss of density across the field, not asymmetry of anatomy.
  182. The correct answer is A (A true lateral view of the hand showing the metacarpal profile.): A true lateral hand view shows the fifth metacarpal in profile, so the volar angulation of the fractured neck can be measured, which decides whether reduction is needed. A repeated PA view shows the fracture line but not angulation in the sagittal plane. A wrist or elbow view leaves the injured metacarpal out of the field.
  183. The correct answer is C (A miscalibrated or failing detector element in the array.): A detector element that reads consistently wrong creates the same error in every projection, which reconstructs as a ring around the rotation axis, and the scanner needs recalibration or service. Motion produces blurring or doubling rather than a fixed ring. Metal creates bright and dark streaks. Beam hardening produces cupping or dark bands between dense structures.
  184. The correct answer is B (Proceed with the usual protocol and record the medication on the form.): A single anti-inflammatory dose in a patient with normal renal function is not a contraindication to iodinated contrast, so the exam goes ahead and the medication is documented. Cancelling is unnecessary and delays care. A twenty-four hour wait has no basis for this history. Barium cannot replace intravenous contrast for an abdominal CT study.
  185. The correct answer is A (Computed tomography of the ankle to assess the fracture fragments.): Computed tomography gives cross-sectional detail of the fragment size, articular involvement and displacement, which guides surgical planning for a posterior malleolar fracture. Repeating the same radiograph adds no new information. A barium study evaluates the gastrointestinal tract and not bone. A bone density scan measures mineral content and does not characterize an acute fracture.
  186. The correct answer is C (The arm is abducted so the beam can pass through the axilla.): Abducting the arm opens the axillary space so the central ray can pass through it and show the humeral head in relation to the glenoid. A fully adducted arm closes that space and gives only an AP type view. Prone positioning is not used for this projection. Head rotation does not change how the beam crosses the joint.
  187. The correct answer is C (To distend the colon and show the mucosal surface for polyps.): Insufflation with room air or carbon dioxide opens the folds of the colon wall so polyps and masses can be seen on the two- and three-dimensional images. It does not change the dose, which depends on the protocol. Bowel preparation and tagging are still needed to avoid residue mimicking lesions. Liver contrast depends on intravenous contrast, not on colonic gas.
  188. The correct answer is D (Angle the central ray caudad to align with the L5-S1 disc space.): Because the lumbosacral joint slopes relative to the rest of the lumbar spine, a caudal central ray angle matched to the patient's pelvic tilt lines the beam up with the disc space and opens it. A fixed cephalic angle would work against that slope. Prone positioning does not give a lateral view. A longer distance changes magnification and sharpness, not alignment.
  189. The correct answer is D (A pancreatic parenchymal phase acquired about forty seconds after injection.): The pancreatic phase, at roughly forty seconds, catches peak enhancement of normal gland tissue, so necrotic areas show as dark, non-enhancing regions. A ten minute delayed phase evaluates the collecting systems. An unenhanced scan cannot show perfusion defects. A pulmonary arterial phase is timed for the chest vessels and is too early for the pancreas.
  190. The correct answer is A (Full inspiration, which lowers the diaphragm and shows the upper ribs.): Full inspiration pulls the diaphragm down and expands the lungs, so the ribs above the diaphragm are seen clear of abdominal organ shadows. Expiration raises the diaphragm and crowds the lower chest, which suits ribs below the diaphragm. Shallow breathing is used for the sternum to blur lung markings. Motion always blurs fine rib detail, so an instruction is needed.
  191. The correct answer is B (Arrange orbital radiographs or CT to screen for a ferromagnetic foreign body.): An undocumented metallic fragment in the orbit could move or heat in the magnetic field and injure the eye, so radiographs or CT must exclude a ferromagnetic object before MRI. The time elapsed does not remove the risk. Lack of symptoms does not prove the fragment is inert. A lower field strength still carries attraction and heating risk.
  192. The correct answer is C (They give a permanent, higher-quality image of a key moment for the record.): A spot image is a discrete, higher-resolution exposure that preserves a clinically important moment, such as peak filling or a wall abnormality, in the permanent record, whereas the live display is transient and lower in quality. Spot images do not alter patient preparation, which still matters for mucosal detail. They also do not change frame rate or barium volume.
  193. The correct answer is B (A fifteen to thirty degree cephalad angle of the central ray.): A cephalad angle of fifteen to thirty degrees throws the clavicle upward off the ribs and scapula, so displacement and overlap of the fragments are easier to judge. A caudad angle projects the bone downward onto the chest structures. A ninety degree axillary beam is not a clavicle projection. A true lateral view is blocked by shoulder tissue.
  194. The correct answer is B (Shallow breathing during the exposure to blur the ribs and lung markings.): Shallow breathing during a long, low-output exposure blurs the moving lung markings and ribs while the sternum, which moves little, stays sharp. A breath-hold keeps those overlying structures still and sharp, so they would hide the sternum. Held full expiration or forced inhalation produces the same problem and adds strain for an injured patient.
  195. The correct answer is D (Choose a suitable injection site and rate to avoid extravasation near vital structures.): The neck holds the airway, major vessels and nerves close together, so a secure site and a suitable rate lower the chance and consequence of extravasation. Doubling the rate raises the risk of vein rupture. A central line is not mandatory when peripheral access is adequate. Monitoring is still needed for reactions, whatever the body region.
  196. The correct answer is B (Spondylolisthesis, a forward slippage of one vertebral body on the one below.): Spondylolisthesis is the slippage of one vertebral body on the adjacent one, best shown on a lateral view as a step in the posterior vertebral lines. Spondylosis describes degenerative osteophytes without slippage. Scoliosis is a lateral curvature seen on frontal images. Spina bifida is a congenital defect of the posterior elements and does not involve a forward slip.
  197. The correct answer is B (The joint is a normally sterile space that a contaminated needle could infect.): A joint cavity is normally sterile, so a contaminated needle or contrast can introduce bacteria and cause septic arthritis, a serious complication, in patients of every age. Allergy history affects contrast choice and premedication, not sterility needs. Sterile technique has no effect on radiation dose, which is managed by fluoroscopy technique and shielding.
  198. The correct answer is D (Consult the radiologist about deferring contrast or using another modality.): Given a documented severe prior reaction and a non-urgent clinical indication, the risk of repeating the same or a similar agent likely outweighs the benefit, so the radiologist should be consulted to consider deferring contrast or using an alternate modality such as MRI or ultrasound. Premedication protocols reduce but do not eliminate risk after a severe reaction, and the passage of time does not remove this history's significance.
  199. The correct answer is B (Use immobilization techniques and the fastest acquisition possible while seeking urgent physician guidance.): In a time-critical stroke evaluation, the technologist should use safe immobilization and the fastest available acquisition technique to minimize motion artifact while urgently involving the physician team, since delaying imaging in suspected acute stroke can worsen patient outcomes. Waiting indefinitely for sedation or using a slow routine protocol both risk unacceptable delay or non-diagnostic images.
  200. The correct answer is D (Discuss the missing renal function result with the radiologist to weigh urgency against risk before proceeding.): In an urgent trauma setting with a specific concern for active bleeding, the potential benefit of timely contrast-enhanced imaging often outweighs the theoretical risk of contrast-induced nephropathy, but this decision should still involve the radiologist weighing the missing renal function against clinical urgency rather than being made unilaterally. Refusing to scan or silently omitting ordered contrast could delay diagnosis of a life-threatening injury.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (A mild to moderate anaphylactoid reaction needing physician notification.): Facial flushing, hives, and wheezing occurring within minutes of intravenous contrast administration are classic signs of an acute anaphylactoid reaction. These reactions are idiosyncratic and not dose dependent, so the technologist must stop the injection, stay with the patient, and alert the radiologist immediately. A vasovagal reaction instead presents with hypotension, bradycardia, and pallor rather than hives and wheezing. Prompt recognition allows early intervention before the reaction progresses to a more severe cardiovascular or respiratory event.
  2. The correct answer is A (Consult the ordering physician about metformin and the renal result.): Metformin combined with reduced renal function raises the risk of lactic acidosis if iodinated contrast further impairs kidney function. Current protocols require the technologist to flag impaired renal function and metformin use so the ordering physician can decide whether to hold the medication and confirm the study is appropriate. Proceeding without this review could place the patient at unnecessary risk. Gadolinium is not an appropriate substitute for a CT study and carries its own renal precautions.
  3. The correct answer is D (Water-soluble iodinated contrast such as diatrizoate meglumine, given orally.): When perforation is suspected, barium must be avoided because leakage into the peritoneal cavity causes severe chemical peritonitis and can be difficult to remove surgically. Water-soluble iodinated contrast is absorbed by the peritoneum if extravasation occurs, making it the safer choice. Barium in any dilution still carries this risk regardless of concentration. Air alone would not adequately demonstrate the perforation site or surrounding anatomy.
  4. The correct answer is B (Twenty-five to thirty-five seconds after the start of the injection.): The late arterial phase, at about twenty-five to thirty-five seconds, shows peak hepatic artery enhancement and makes hypervascular tumours such as hepatocellular carcinoma stand out. Five to ten seconds is too early for contrast to fill the hepatic artery. Sixty to ninety seconds is the portal venous phase, and several minutes is the delayed phase used to see washout.
  5. The correct answer is A (Stop the injection immediately, elevate the extremity, and apply a cold or warm compress per protocol.): Burning pain and localized swelling during a power injection indicate contrast extravasation into the surrounding soft tissue. The technologist must stop the injection right away, assess the extent of the extravasation, elevate the affected limb, and follow departmental protocol for compress application, which typically involves cold compresses to reduce swelling. The radiologist and nursing staff should also be notified for further assessment. Continuing the injection or repositioning the catheter risks worsening tissue damage.
  6. The correct answer is C (Existing diabetic nephropathy with an elevated baseline serum creatinine value.): Diabetic kidney disease with a raised creatinine means the kidneys have little reserve to clear the contrast load, which is among the strongest predictors of contrast-induced nephropathy. A healthy forty-five year old carries low risk. A vasovagal faint is a nervous system response unrelated to kidney injury. Controlled hypertension with normal kidney function adds only a small baseline risk.
  7. The correct answer is B (Nephrogenic systemic fibrosis, which affects the skin and internal organs.): Gadolinium is cleared by the kidneys, so poor renal function prolongs tissue exposure and raises the risk of nephrogenic systemic fibrosis, a fibrosing disorder of skin, joints and organs. Acute tubular necrosis is linked to iodinated agents. Thyrotoxicosis comes from iodine, which gadolinium does not contain. Anaphylactoid reactions are rare and do not depend on kidney function.
  8. The correct answer is D (Use last-image-hold and pulsed fluoroscopy whenever the radiologist pauses to discuss findings.): Last-image-hold lets the radiologist review a frozen image, and pulsed fluoroscopy cuts the beam-on time, so patient and staff dose both fall without losing diagnostic information. Standing near the intensifier raises staff exposure because scatter is greatest there. Continuous fluoroscopy increases beam time unnecessarily. Removing lead protection leaves the staff exposed to scatter for the rest of the study.
  9. The correct answer is A (Mediolateral oblique view, where the muscle should reach nipple level.): On the mediolateral oblique view, the pectoral muscle should be visualized as a convex anterior border extending down to or below the level of the nipple to confirm that adequate posterior breast tissue has been included. This is a key quality criterion used to assess positioning accuracy. The craniocaudal view may show a small portion of pectoral muscle but does not have the same nipple-level requirement. Proper demonstration of the muscle helps ensure the entire breast has been imaged.
  10. The correct answer is B (Osteoporosis, showing a marked reduction in bone mineral density.): A T-score of negative 2.5 or lower meets the World Health Organization definition of osteoporosis, so negative 2.7 qualifies. Scores between negative 1.0 and negative 2.5 are osteopenia, which is milder. Normal is above negative 1.0. Osteomalacia is a metabolic mineralization disorder that is not classified by T-score and is not treated surgically as a first step.
  11. The correct answer is D (Patient rotation away from a true PA position during the exposure.): Unequal distances from the medial clavicular ends to the spinous processes show the thorax was rotated, so the chest was not a true PA and the mediastinum and lung fields may be distorted. Deep inspiration changes lung length, not clavicle symmetry. Underexposure changes density. A wrong distance changes magnification evenly and does not make the clavicles asymmetric.
  12. The correct answer is B (Centering the central ray at the level of the iliac crest to include the symphysis pubis.): The kidney, ureter, and bladder radiograph must demonstrate the entire urinary system from the kidneys down through the bladder, which requires centering at the level of the iliac crest so the inferior border of the image includes the symphysis pubis. This ensures the full length of the ureters and the bladder are captured for evaluation of calculi anywhere along the urinary tract. A cephalad angle or overly restricted field size would risk cutting off important anatomy. Prone positioning is not the standard approach for this examination.
  13. The correct answer is C (Ulnar deviation PA projection to elongate the scaphoid.): Ulnar deviation of the wrist during a PA projection elongates the scaphoid bone, reducing the normal foreshortening that occurs in neutral position and improving visualization of a fracture line that might otherwise be obscured by overlapping carpal bones. This specialized view is a standard part of a scaphoid series when clinical suspicion remains high despite normal initial radiographs. A standard neutral PA or lateral projection does not provide this elongation effect. An AP forearm projection is not targeted at the carpal region and would not adequately demonstrate the scaphoid.
  14. The correct answer is A (An occult fracture with an associated joint effusion or hemarthrosis.): A visible posterior fat pad is abnormal, and with a raised sail-shaped anterior fat pad it indicates fluid in the joint, which after trauma strongly suggests an occult fracture such as a supracondylar or radial head injury. A normal variant would not show the posterior pad. Degenerative change is not expected in a child. Calcification would look dense rather than as displaced fat.
  15. The correct answer is B (Give corticosteroid and antihistamine premedication before the injection.): A documented history of a prior moderate contrast reaction warrants a premedication protocol, typically involving corticosteroids given at intervals before the examination along with an antihistamine, to reduce the likelihood and severity of a repeat reaction. This allows the clinically necessary study to proceed more safely rather than being cancelled outright. Barium sulfate is not an appropriate intravenous substitute since it is not designed for vascular administration. Proceeding without any precaution would place the patient at unnecessary and avoidable risk.
  16. The correct answer is D (Use a faster injection rate with bolus tracking at the pulmonary artery.): CT pulmonary angiography requires rapid, dense opacification of the pulmonary arteries, so a faster injection rate combined with bolus tracking placed over the pulmonary artery or main trunk ensures scanning begins precisely when peak enhancement is reached. A slower rate with a larger volume is more typical of routine parenchymal imaging rather than a dedicated angiographic study. Eliminating the saline flush would reduce the compact bolus effect needed for optimal arterial opacification. Delaying the scan after injection would miss the narrow window of peak pulmonary arterial enhancement.
  17. The correct answer is C (Rapid sequence videofluoroscopy that records the swallowing mechanism in real time.): The pharyngeal phase of swallowing occurs extremely rapidly, often within a fraction of a second, making rapid sequence fluoroscopy or videofluoroscopy necessary to capture the dynamic motion of the bolus and assess for aspiration or penetration into the airway. Static overhead radiographs taken at intervals would not be fast enough to document this brief phase. A single delayed abdomen radiograph provides no information about the swallowing mechanism itself. Ultrasound is not the standard modality for evaluating pharyngeal swallowing function in this context.
  18. The correct answer is B (The patient has fasted from food and liquids for the required period beforehand.): An upper gastrointestinal series requires the stomach to be empty so the barium contrast can adequately coat the mucosal lining without being diluted or obscured by residual food or fluid, so confirming the patient has fasted for the appropriate period beforehand is essential. Food in the stomach would interfere with mucosal detail and could mimic or mask pathology. A recent barium enema on the same day could leave residual contrast overlying the upper abdomen and should be avoided. Insulin timing is a separate clinical consideration not directly tied to imaging quality for this study.
  19. The correct answer is A (Pleural effusion accumulating in the dependent portion of the thoracic cavity.): A homogeneous area of increased density obscuring the costophrenic angle at the lung base is the classic appearance of a pleural effusion, which occurs as fluid accumulates in the dependent pleural space and blunts the normally sharp angle. A pneumothorax instead produces a lucent, air-filled region with a visible visceral pleural line rather than increased density. This appearance is not consistent with a normal diaphragmatic contour, which should show a sharp costophrenic angle. The correctly placed R marker confirms proper side identification is not the source of this finding.
  20. The correct answer is C (CT urography provides cross-sectional detail of the kidneys, ureters, and surrounding structures.): CT urography offers cross-sectional imaging that demonstrates the kidneys, ureters, bladder, and adjacent soft tissue structures with far greater anatomic detail than the overlapping planar images produced by intravenous pyelography. This allows better characterization of masses, stones, and other abnormalities beyond the collecting system itself. CT urography still requires intravenous contrast and delayed phase imaging to opacify the excretory system, so those statements are inaccurate. Contrast dose can actually be comparable or vary depending on protocol rather than always being lower.
  21. The correct answer is A (Contrast is instilled through a cervical cannula under fluoroscopic monitoring of tubal filling.): The radiologist places a cannula in the cervix and instills water-soluble iodinated contrast, watching the uterine cavity and tubes fill under fluoroscopy to judge patency and spill into the peritoneum. Intravenous or oral contrast does not directly fill the uterine cavity. Injecting ovarian vessels is not part of this study and would not show tubal patency.
  22. The correct answer is C (Nonionic, water-soluble contrast that is approved for intrathecal use.): Myelography needs a nonionic, water-soluble agent approved for intrathecal use, because it has lower neurotoxicity than ionic media. High-osmolar ionic contrast can trigger seizures and severe neurologic injury if it enters the cerebrospinal fluid. Barium is insoluble and is not given into the canal. Gadolinium at MRI doses is not approved for intrathecal use.
  23. The correct answer is D (To alert the operator to cumulative fluoroscopy time so radiation dose can be tracked and managed.): The fluoroscopic timer sounds an audible signal, typically every five minutes of cumulative beam-on time, to remind the operator of how much fluoroscopy has been used so far during the procedure. This allows the radiologist to consciously assess whether continued fluoroscopy is necessary and to manage overall patient radiation dose during lengthy interventional cases. It does not automatically stop the procedure, calculate contrast volume, or adjust technical exposure factors, all of which are separate functions from the required timer feature.
  24. The correct answer is B (An open mouth odontoid projection that demonstrates C1 and C2.): The open mouth odontoid view looks through the open mouth at C1 and C2, showing the dens and the symmetry of the lateral masses, which allows detection of rotary subluxation or a dens fracture. A lateral view centred low does not isolate the upper cervical joints. The swimmer's lateral is for the lower cervical and upper thoracic region. An AP axial view shows the lower cervical bodies.
  25. The correct answer is C (Transthoracic lateral or scapular Y projection performed without moving the affected arm.): In a trauma setting where movement of the injured shoulder must be minimized, the transthoracic lateral or scapular Y projection provides a perpendicular view to the standard AP that helps assess the position of the humeral head relative to the glenoid without requiring rotation or abduction of the affected arm. Both internal and external rotation projections require arm movement that may be painful or contraindicated in a suspected dislocation. The axillary projection also requires abduction, which is often not feasible immediately after acute trauma.
  26. The correct answer is A (A weight-based dose calculated according to the patient's body weight in kilograms.): Pediatric contrast dosing is calculated based on the patient's body weight in kilograms rather than using a fixed adult volume, since children vary widely in size and a standardized adult dose could result in significant overdosing or underdosing. This weight-based approach helps achieve adequate diagnostic enhancement while minimizing unnecessary contrast exposure. Doubling the adult volume would be dangerous and is not standard practice. Pediatric patients can safely receive contrast when clinically indicated and appropriately dosed.
  27. The correct answer is D (Stay with the patient, call for help, and prepare to assist with oxygen and emergency medications.): Wheezing and difficulty breathing following contrast injection indicate a moderate to severe reaction involving bronchospasm, requiring the technologist to remain with the patient, activate the emergency response system, and be ready to assist the responding team with supplemental oxygen and appropriate medications such as bronchodilators or epinephrine as directed. Continuing the scan or discharging the patient would delay necessary emergency treatment and place the patient at serious risk. Simply waiting without intervention fails to provide the immediate supportive care this situation demands.
  28. The correct answer is C (Notify the radiologist so renal function can be weighed against the clinical need for contrast.): A significantly reduced eGFR requires the technologist to notify the radiologist so the clinical necessity of the contrast-enhanced study can be weighed against the risk of further renal impairment, with consideration given to hydration protocols, reduced contrast dose, or an alternative non-contrast examination. Proceeding without this review or cancelling the study outright bypasses the physician's clinical judgment. Increasing contrast volume in a patient with reduced renal clearance would increase risk rather than compensate for it.
  29. The correct answer is B (Patient or part motion occurring while the exposure was being made.): Motion during the exposure smears fine bone and soft tissue detail across the entire image, which gives generalized blurring even when density is acceptable. A correct, error-free image would look sharp. Overexposure makes the image too dark rather than blurred. Grid cutoff reduces density toward one or both edges and does not blur detail evenly.
  30. The correct answer is D (Direct cannulation of the duct orifice and injection of contrast into it.): Sialography needs a small cannula placed in the opening of the duct, usually Stensen or Wharton, and a small volume of water-soluble contrast is instilled to outline strictures or stones. Intravenous or oral contrast does not fill the salivary ducts selectively. Injecting into surrounding tissue would not outline the duct and could cause pain and tissue damage.
  31. The correct answer is D (A superficial vein on the dorsum of the foot with the leg non-weight bearing.): Contrast injected into a dorsal foot vein flows up through the deep veins of the calf and thigh, which are filmed while the leg is tilted and not bearing weight. A superior vena cava catheter would not fill the leg veins. Arterial injection shows arteries first. Intrathecal injection belongs to myelography and has no role in venous imaging.
  32. The correct answer is A (Serial radiographs or fluoroscopic spot images taken at timed intervals as barium moves.): A follow-through uses serial images at timed intervals, often every fifteen to thirty minutes, to track transit and show narrowing, obstruction or mucosal change. One immediate or one delayed image cannot show how the barium moves. Continuous fluoroscopy for hours would give needless radiation dose without adding diagnostic value over timed images.
  33. The correct answer is B (Frog leg lateral projection requiring abduction and external rotation of the hip.): The frog leg lateral projection requires significant abduction and external rotation of the hip, which can cause severe pain and risk displacing an unstable or comminuted fracture fragment in a trauma patient. For this reason, a cross-table lateral projection is preferred in suspected hip fracture cases because it does not require moving the affected limb. The AP pelvis and standard AP hip projections can typically still be obtained with minimal movement, making them acceptable initial views. Avoiding unnecessary limb manipulation is essential for patient safety and comfort in acute trauma.
  34. The correct answer is C (A carpal tunnel tangential projection with the wrist hyperextended and the beam angled through the canal.): The tangential carpal canal view has the wrist in maximum hyperextension with the central ray angled along the canal, which shows the pisiform, hamate hook and trapezium in profile. A flat PA wrist and a lateral wrist superimpose the carpal bones. An oblique wrist is a general carpal view and does not profile the canal margins.
  35. The correct answer is A (A mortise projection with fifteen to twenty degrees of internal leg rotation.): The mortise projection requires internally rotating the lower leg approximately fifteen to twenty degrees, which aligns the intermalleolar line parallel to the image receptor and opens the ankle mortise joint space, allowing clear visualization of the space between the talus and both the medial and lateral malleoli without overlap. A standard AP ankle projection in neutral position results in some superimposition of the distal fibula over the talus and does not demonstrate the joint space as clearly. The lateral projection provides a different anatomic perspective and does not show the mortise joint symmetry.
  36. The correct answer is D (A swimmer's lateral projection with one arm raised and the other depressed.): The swimmer's lateral separates the shoulders by raising one arm and lowering the other, so the lower cervical and upper thoracic vertebrae are seen without overlap. Repeating the same lateral leaves the shoulders in place. The open mouth view and the AP axial view show other regions and do not clear the cervicothoracic junction.
  37. The correct answer is C (Angle the central ray a few degrees caudad, adjusting for pelvic width and tilt.): The lumbosacral joint slopes relative to the rest of the lumbar spine, so a small caudad central ray angle, adjusted for pelvic width and tilt, aligns the beam with the joint and opens the L5-S1 space. The same angle as the standard lateral would repeat the problem. Centring at T12 misses the target level. An oblique position shows the posterior elements, not the disc space.
  38. The correct answer is B (A soft tissue mass displacing the normal prevertebral tissue planes.): A discrete, well-defined density in front of the vertebral bodies that disturbs the normal prevertebral planes is consistent with a soft tissue mass, and cross-sectional imaging should follow. A normal thyroid shadow is not a localized mass. Rotation and underexposure alter the whole image rather than producing a circumscribed finding in one region.
  39. The correct answer is D (Repeat the exposure with the marker on the correct anatomic side.): A misplaced anatomical side marker represents a patient safety and legal documentation error regardless of whether the underlying anatomy appears normal, because an incorrectly marked image can lead to wrong-side identification in future clinical decisions. The correct action is to repeat the exposure with the marker properly placed to accurately reflect the true anatomic side imaged. Digitally flipping the image would falsify the anatomic orientation and is never an acceptable correction method. Adding a text annotation does not resolve the fundamental error of an incorrectly placed physical marker and does not meet documentation standards.
  40. The correct answer is A (Vicarious excretion through the liver, gallbladder, and bowel.): When renal function is impaired and the kidneys cannot efficiently clear iodinated contrast, a process called vicarious excretion allows a portion of the contrast to be eliminated through the hepatobiliary system, passing through the liver into the gallbladder and ultimately the bowel. This alternate pathway becomes proportionally more significant as renal clearance decreases. Iodinated contrast is not metabolized by skeletal muscle, eliminated through the respiratory system, or permanently stored in adipose tissue, making none of these accurate descriptions of contrast elimination.
  41. The correct answer is D (Nonionic agents have lower osmolality and do not split into charged particles.): Nonionic contrast media do not dissociate into charged particles in solution, resulting in lower osmolality compared to older ionic agents, which is associated with a reduced incidence and severity of adverse reactions. This makes nonionic contrast generally preferred, particularly for patients with a history of sensitivity or other risk factors. Nonionic contrast still contains iodine and therefore still carries some risk of reaction, though generally lower than ionic agents. Nonionic contrast is commonly administered intravenously and is not restricted to oral use.
  42. The correct answer is A (Implant-displaced views using the Eklund technique to push the implant posteriorly.): The Eklund technique involves manually displacing the implant posteriorly against the chest wall while pulling the breast tissue anteriorly, allowing compression and imaging of the natural breast tissue that would otherwise be obscured by the radiopaque implant on standard views. This additional technique is performed alongside, not instead of, the standard craniocaudal and mediolateral oblique projections. A single uncompressed lateral view would not provide adequate compression for diagnostic quality. Ultrasound may supplement mammography in implant patients but does not replace the mammographic views entirely.
  43. The correct answer is B (A reference population of the same age, sex, and ethnicity as the patient.): The Z-score compares a patient's bone mineral density to an age-matched, sex-matched, and ethnicity-matched reference population, which is particularly useful in younger patients where comparison to peak young adult bone mass, as used for the T-score, may not be clinically appropriate. This helps identify bone loss that is abnormal for the patient's specific demographic rather than simply reflecting expected age-related changes. The T-score, by contrast, compares the patient to peak bone mass in a healthy young adult reference group. Neither score is based on the patient's own prior results or a combined all-age average.
  44. The correct answer is C (Better spatial resolution and less geometric distortion across the whole field.): Flat panel detectors offer better spatial resolution and remove the pincushion and vignetting distortion typical of image intensifiers, and they often allow lower dose. Greater peripheral distortion describes the older system. Higher dose is not required. Flat panel systems still use ionizing radiation, so exposure is reduced and not eliminated.
  45. The correct answer is D (A recent history of coughing or choking episodes while eating or drinking.): A recent history of coughing or choking while eating or drinking is a significant clinical indicator of underlying swallowing dysfunction and substantially raises concern for aspiration risk during a barium swallow examination, prompting closer monitoring and potentially modified technique or contrast selection. Well-controlled hypertension, a prior normal chest radiograph, or a normal body mass index are not directly related indicators of swallowing safety. Recognizing these risk factors before the study allows the technologist and radiologist to take appropriate precautions during contrast administration.
  46. The correct answer is A (Giving intravenous normal saline hydration before and after the contrast injection.): Adequate intravenous hydration with normal saline before and after contrast administration is the most commonly recommended preventive measure for reducing the risk of contrast-induced nephropathy in patients with underlying renal impairment, as it helps maintain renal blood flow and promotes contrast clearance. Restricting fluids would have the opposite effect and could worsen renal perfusion. Nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory medications can actually be nephrotoxic and are avoided rather than administered in this context. Prolonged fasting from food and fluids is not part of standard contrast nephropathy prevention protocols.
  47. The correct answer is C (Retained iodine can block radioactive iodine uptake and falsely lower the study results.): Iodinated contrast can saturate the thyroid gland's iodine pool for several weeks, which can falsely lower a later radioactive iodine uptake result by competing with the radiotracer. Telling nuclear medicine about the contrast lets them delay the study or account for the interference. Contrast does not usually trigger thyroid disease in patients with normal thyroid function, and the studies are spaced apart rather than combined on one day. The claim that later studies are unaffected is wrong because the iodine load is the problem.
  48. The correct answer is B (Patient movement occurring during the exposure time of the radiograph.): Diffuse blurring of both bone cortex and soft tissue outlines without a regional density change is the typical look of motion during the exposure. A sharp image would show no blurring. Grid cutoff lowers density toward the edges and does not blur detail evenly. Tight collimation shrinks the field but leaves the structures inside it sharp.
  49. The correct answer is D (Contrast is instilled up the ureter through a catheter placed cystoscopically.): In retrograde pyelography the urologist passes a catheter through the bladder into the ureter and instills contrast upward into the collecting system under fluoroscopy, which works independent of renal function. Intravenous urography relies on kidney excretion. Oral contrast would reach the bowel. Direct injection into renal tissue is not used for this procedure.
  50. The correct answer is A (Contrast is injected directly into the joint space under fluoroscopic guidance.): Arthrography needs a needle placed in the joint under fluoroscopic or ultrasound guidance so contrast outlines the capsule, ligaments and cartilage, often followed by MRI. Intravenous contrast does not selectively fill a joint. Oral and inhaled routes cannot distend the joint space, which is required to outline the capsule and tendon surfaces.
  51. The correct answer is B (Administer a corticosteroid premedication regimen before the injection as ordered.): Premedication with corticosteroids and antihistamines is standard for patients with a documented history of a mild to moderate prior contrast reaction before using the same class of iodinated agent again. This reduces but does not eliminate the risk of a repeat reaction. Switching to a non-iodinated agent for a urogram is not clinically practical, and rushing the injection does not address the underlying allergic mechanism.
  52. The correct answer is B (The patient's torso was rotated slightly toward one side during the exposure.): Rotation of the torso causes asymmetric distance from the spine to each clavicle even when the patient believes they are standing straight. This is the most common cause of unequal clavicle-to-spine distances on an otherwise well-centred PA chest image. Angulation and SID errors typically affect magnification and rib count rather than clavicle symmetry.
  53. The correct answer is B (Apply gentle caudal traction on both wrists while the patient remains supine.): Gentle caudal traction on the wrists, unless injury forbids it, pulls the shoulders down so C7 and T1 can be seen while the neck stays immobilized. Changing distance and tube angle does not move the shoulders. Collar removal before clearance risks spinal cord injury. Sitting upright and flexing the neck also puts an uncleared spine at risk.
  54. The correct answer is A (Residual barium coating the mucosa can obscure lesions on the enema images.): Retained barium from a prior upper GI study coats the bowel mucosa and can obscure polyps, masses, or other mucosal detail on a subsequent barium enema. Confirming clearance, sometimes with a preliminary scout image, is essential before proceeding. Barium does not chemically react with the new contrast agent and rarely causes acute obstruction from a single prior dose.
  55. The correct answer is A (Confirming that a sterile catheter is placed and the bladder is ready to fill.): A voiding cystourethrogram depends on a sterile catheter being placed so the bladder can be filled retrograde with contrast, then imaged during voiding. Fasting and bowel cleansing are not standard because the study targets the urinary tract. Withholding fluids for twelve hours is unnecessary and could dehydrate a young child.
  56. The correct answer is D (Free intraperitoneal air from a perforated abdominal viscus.): Gas on both sides of the bowel wall indicates air that has escaped into the peritoneal cavity, which is a sign of a perforated viscus and a surgical emergency needing immediate notification. A hiatal hernia lies in the chest. Barium studies leave contrast, not free gas. Normal bowel gas stays inside the lumen and does not outline the wall externally.
  57. The correct answer is C (Consult the radiologist about using a reduced contrast dose or an alternative study.): A low estimated glomerular filtration rate places a patient at increased risk for contrast-induced nephropathy, so the radiologist should be consulted about dose reduction, hydration protocols, or an alternative non-contrast study. Proceeding with a full standard dose without this consultation disregards established renal safety practice. Doubling the rate or switching to oral administration would not address the underlying risk.
  58. The correct answer is A (Insufficient posterior tissue was included on the craniocaudal projection.): The posterior nipple line compares how much posterior tissue is on the craniocaudal view with the oblique view, and a markedly shorter line means posterior tissue was left out, so the view should be repeated. Compression differences affect thickness and sharpness, not tissue coverage. A misplaced sensor changes density. Density change alters appearance but not the measured line length.
  59. The correct answer is C (A forty-five degree posterior oblique projection of the lumbar spine.): The forty-five degree posterior oblique profiles the pars interarticularis, which forms the neck of the Scotty dog, so a pars defect appears as a collar. A true lateral shows vertebral alignment and disc spaces, and an AP axial shows the vertebral bodies and L5-S1. A cross-table lateral is used mainly in trauma and does not profile the pars.
  60. The correct answer is B (Residual barium in the bowel can artificially elevate the measured bone density values.): Residual barium remaining in the bowel from a recent contrast study can overlie the lumbar spine and artificially elevate the measured bone mineral density on a DEXA scan, so the examination should ideally be rescheduled until the barium has cleared. Barium does not alter vertebral mineral composition, and there is no fixed six-month restriction between the two studies. The radiation dose from barium studies is entirely separate from the DEXA scan's own exposure.
  61. The correct answer is C (The needle tip has moved out of position outside the joint capsule.): Rising resistance with growing pain suggests the needle tip has slipped outside the capsule so contrast is being forced into soft tissue, and the needle needs repositioning before more is injected. A large joint would accept contrast easily. Distension causes fullness but not sudden resistance. Osmolality changes do not produce resistance and pain together.
  62. The correct answer is B (Using bolus tracking with a threshold measured in the main pulmonary artery.): Bolus tracking measures attenuation in the main pulmonary artery and triggers the scan once a threshold is reached, which fits the timing to each patient's circulation. A single slow push or starting the scan with the injection would miss peak opacification. A fixed delay ignores differences in cardiac output between patients and may scan too early or too late.
  63. The correct answer is A (Thirty-five to forty-five degrees into a posterior oblique, affected side against the receptor.): The Grashey view is a posterior oblique with the affected side rotated about thirty-five to forty-five degrees toward the receptor, which places the scapula flat on the receptor and the beam parallel to the joint, opening the glenohumeral space. A small anterior rotation leaves the joint overlapped. A true AP leaves the humeral head superimposed on the glenoid. Ninety degrees gives a lateral view.
  64. The correct answer is A (Saline infusion sonohysterography with ultrasound in place of iodinated contrast.): Saline infusion with ultrasound, including contrast-enhanced saline techniques, can show tubal patency without exposing an iodine-sensitive patient to iodinated media. A more concentrated iodinated agent would raise the reaction risk. Reactions during hysterosalpingography are documented and should not be dismissed. Barium is not suitable for studying the uterus and tubes.
  65. The correct answer is D (The patient's head was rotated slightly instead of being held in a true lateral position.): Head rotation separates the mandibular rami because the rami lie at different distances from the receptor, so any turn of the head spreads them apart even if other landmarks look acceptable. A tilted interpupillary line produces vertical separation of paired structures, which is a different pattern. A shifted central ray entry point changes projection geometry slightly, not rami superimposition. A longer source-to-image distance mainly lowers magnification and does not separate paired structures.
  66. The correct answer is D (Current low-osmolar contrast rarely provokes a hypertensive crisis, so special premedication is generally not required.): Older high-osmolar contrast was linked to catecholamine surges in pheochromocytoma, but current low-osmolar nonionic contrast rarely provokes a hypertensive crisis, so premedication is generally not required and the study can proceed. The tumour does enhance, imaging with ionizing radiation is not contraindicated, and adrenal tissue does not retain contrast for years.
  67. The correct answer is A (The arm nearest the receptor is raised overhead while the opposite shoulder is pulled down.): The swimmer's lateral raises the arm closest to the image receptor overhead while the opposite shoulder is depressed, which separates the shoulder shadows and opens the view of the cervicothoracic junction. Leaving both arms at the sides does not clear the region. A forty-five degree body rotation does not make a true lateral. Pushing both shoulders forward leaves the superimposed shoulders in place.
  68. The correct answer is B (It lets barium pool in the antrum and duodenal bulb while air fills the fundus.): In the right anterior oblique position the antrum and duodenal bulb lie dependent, so barium pools there while air rises into the fundus, which gives a double-contrast view of the gastric body and antral region. The gallbladder does not fill with gastric barium, the stomach is not moved out of the beam, and this position does not replace later projections.
  69. The correct answer is C (Some drugs that lower the seizure threshold raise the risk of contrast-related seizures.): Intrathecal contrast can reach the brain and irritate neural tissue, so drugs that lower the seizure threshold raise the chance of a contrast-related seizure, and the radiologist may adjust or hold them. Seizure drugs do not change the way a needle enters tissue. Medication history matters in many procedures, not only cardiac ones. Contrast mixes with cerebrospinal fluid regardless of these drugs.
  70.
