The correct answer is A (Place the wrist flat on the image receptor and remove any rotation of the forearm.): Uneven superimposition of the distal radius and ulna means the forearm or wrist is rotated, so the correction is to lay the wrist flat against the receptor with no rotation. Extra flexion does not remove rotation and changes joint relationships. Angling the central ray toward the ulna distorts the joint spaces instead of curing rotation. A greater distance only affects magnification and cannot reposition the bones.
  71. The correct answer is D (The patient is obliqued about thirty-five degrees with the leg nearest the table flexed.): A thirty-five to forty degree oblique with the near leg flexed separates the urethra from the overlying thigh and pelvic bones and stretches it out, which avoids foreshortening. A perfectly supine position superimposes the urethra on the symphysis and bones. A full ninety degree turn throws the urethra into a lateral that overlaps the soft tissue. Lateral with a pelvic sponge does not open the bulbar segment.
  72. The correct answer is B (Consult the radiologist about whether a macrocyclic agent or a non-contrast study is safer.): Severe renal impairment raises the risk of nephrogenic systemic fibrosis, so the radiologist should decide whether a more stable macrocyclic agent or a study without contrast is safer. Doubling the dose increases exposure when clearance is already poor. Linear agents are the ones most linked to this disease, so giving the ordered dose is unsafe. Withholding the history from the radiologist ignores the risk the screening was meant to catch.
  73. The correct answer is C (To evaluate how far the condyle travels and detect restricted joint mobility.): Comparing open and closed views shows how far each condyle glides forward out of the fossa, so reduced or excessive motion can be recognized. The pair of images is not meant to confirm orthodontic healing, which is judged with other views and clinical findings. It does not measure dose, which comes from exposure records. It also cannot predict anesthesia needs, which depend on clinical assessment.
  74. The correct answer is D (The patient currently takes an opioid that slows normal bowel motility.): Opioid medications reduce gastrointestinal motility and can markedly delay barium transit, so current use should be documented to prevent misreading slow transit as pathology. A large drink of water is more likely to speed transit than to slow it. Room temperature has no meaningful effect on bowel motility. A small kVp change alters image contrast and has no influence on how fast barium moves.
  75. The correct answer is D (Contrast enhancement can obscure early ischemic change and mask the appearance of acute hemorrhage.): The first scan must separate hemorrhage from ischemia quickly, and contrast can hide early ischemic change and confuse the appearance of acute blood, so a non-contrast study comes first. Contrast is not toxic to brain tissue. Detectors register enhancement well. Contrast angiography or perfusion is often added later in the stroke workup, so protocols do not prohibit it outright.
  76. The correct answer is C (A shallow breathing technique to blur overlying rib and lung markings.): continuous shallow breathing maintained throughout a long exposure time blurs the overlying rib and lung markings that would otherwise obscure the thoracic vertebral bodies and disc spaces on a lateral projection, while the less mobile spine stays relatively sharp, producing a breathing technique image with clearer disc space visualization. A held deep inspiration would sharply demonstrate the ribs and lungs instead of blurring them, which is the opposite of what is needed here. Rapid panting is used for other purposes and is not appropriate here.
  77. The correct answer is C (Discard the vial and obtain a properly stored replacement before the injection begins.): Contrast that has crystallized or been stored outside its labelled range may have changed in concentration or sterility, so the vial must be discarded and replaced. Microwaving heats the agent unevenly and cannot restore its approved state. Dilution changes the concentration and the osmolality. Straining through gauze does not guarantee sterility or correct concentration and risks injecting particles.
  78. The correct answer is B (A water-soluble, non-ionic contrast agent formulated for oral use.): A water-soluble, non-ionic oral contrast agent is preferred after aspiration because, unlike barium sulfate, it is less likely to cause a significant inflammatory reaction if it enters the airway or lung tissue. Continuing with barium, even at a different consistency, is not appropriate after aspiration has occurred. Ionic high-osmolar agents intended for intravenous urography are not suitable for oral or aspiration-risk use.
  79. The correct answer is B (The chin touches the receptor with the orbitomeatal line at about thirty-seven degrees.): In the Waters method the chin touches the image receptor and the orbitomeatal line forms roughly a thirty-seven degree angle with it, which projects the petrous ridges below the maxillary sinuses. The vertex-down position is not used for this view. The forehead-and-nose position is a PA skull and leaves the petrous ridges over the sinuses. Resting on the occipital protuberance gives an AP-type view instead.
  80. The correct answer is D (It distends the bowel without hiding subtle enhancement of the mucosa on contrast images.): A neutral agent distends the small bowel while leaving the wall visible, so subtle mucosal enhancement from intravenous contrast can be seen. A dense positive agent would mask this enhancement. Enterography still needs intravenous contrast to show wall enhancement, so the agent does not replace it. The agent does not coat the wall for hours, and it does not travel more slowly than positive agents.
  81. The correct answer is D (The pelvis was rotated instead of being aligned in a true anteroposterior position.): Unequal obturator foramina point to rotation of the pelvis away from a true AP, which also changes how the trochanters are profiled. Internal rotation of the legs by fifteen to twenty degrees is actually the standard way to place the femoral necks parallel to the receptor, so it is not an error. A high central ray shifts coverage rather than symmetry. A shorter distance increases magnification only.
  82. The correct answer is C (Asthma is an independent risk factor that raises the likelihood of a reaction.): Asthma is recognized as an independent risk factor for an adverse reaction to iodinated contrast, so the radiologist weighs premedication or an alternative strategy. The relationship does exist, so denying it is wrong. Asthma raises risk but does not automatically cancel an examination that is clinically needed. The risk does not depend mainly on prior contrast exposure.
  83. The correct answer is C (Stop the contrast infusion immediately and report the symptoms to the radiologist.): Sudden severe pain with a possibly displaced balloon may signal rectal injury or perforation, so the infusion is stopped at once and the radiologist is told. Removing the catheter in secret delays assessment. Increasing the rate or continuing to fill the colon could force barium through a tear and cause peritonitis. Moving the patient prone does not address the danger.
  84. The correct answer is D (It produces a tight, well-timed bolus needed to opacify the pulmonary arterial tree.): A higher injection rate produces a tight, concentrated contrast bolus that is necessary to achieve adequate opacification of the pulmonary arterial tree within the narrow timing window required for CT pulmonary angiography. This does not by itself reduce the total contrast volume needed based on patient weight. Bolus tracking or timing calculations remain necessary regardless of injection rate, and contrast will still reach the systemic circulation after passing through the lungs.
  85. The correct answer is B (It demonstrates true joint space width under physiologic loading conditions.): Weight-bearing AP projections of the knees demonstrate true joint space width under normal physiologic loading, which better reflects functional cartilage thinning than a supine, non-weight-bearing image. This technique does not inherently reduce radiation dose or eliminate rotation artifact, and it does not permit a significantly lower kVp setting. Its primary value is functional rather than technical.
  86. The correct answer is D (A water-soluble iodinated agent injected directly into the cannulated ductal system.): ERCP uses a water-soluble iodinated agent injected directly into the cannulated bile and pancreatic ducts under fluoroscopy, which outlines the ducts and is safely absorbed if it leaks. Air alone would not opacify fluid-filled ducts. Gadolinium is an MRI agent and is not used for fluoroscopic ductal imaging. Barium given orally would obscure the endoscopic view and does not enter the ducts.
  87. This finding is not a normal variant and should prompt further evaluation. It reflects joint fluid rather than positioning flexion angle or exposure factor issues.
  88. The correct answer is A (To let contrast opacify the renal collecting system, the ureters, and the bladder.): The delayed excretory phase is acquired once contrast has been filtered by the kidneys and has entered the collecting system, so the calyces, ureters, and bladder are opacified for evaluation. It does not quantify unabsorbed contrast. It does not confirm that the blood is clear of contrast, and an added acquisition cannot lower the dose already given earlier.
  89. Fifteen degrees caudad, a perpendicular ray and a ninety-degree lateral entry would not demonstrate the axial calcaneus.
  90. The correct answer is C (To promote natural drainage of the contrast out of the ductal system after imaging.): A sour stimulus increases salivary flow, which flushes contrast out of the duct system and shows how well the gland drains. Numbing gel is a separate step and is not provided by candy. Taste does not prevent contrast reactions. Dilating the duct orifice would be needed before cannulation, not after the images are taken, so it cannot be the purpose here.
  91. The correct answer is A (Air-fluid levels inside dilated loops of bowel that indicate an obstruction.): A horizontal beam with the patient upright or decubitus lets gas rise and fluid settle, which creates air-fluid levels inside dilated bowel loops that point to obstruction. Diaphragm position is assessed on chest imaging, not for bowel obstruction.
  92. The correct answer is C (Explain that shellfish allergy alone is not a reliable predictor of contrast reaction.): Current evidence shows that shellfish allergy is caused by proteins in shellfish rather than iodine, so it does not reliably predict a reaction to contrast, although any history of allergy is still reported to the radiologist. It does not guarantee a severe reaction or require cancellation. Shellfish iodine is not the same as contrast media. Many other people can react, not only shellfish-allergic patients.
  93. The correct answer is A (Flag the image for radiologist review given the new lateral soft tissue finding.): A new, well-defined soft tissue density on a technically adequate humerus radiograph that was absent on a prior comparison study represents a significant interval change requiring prompt radiologist review rather than dismissal as a technical artifact. Repeating the exposure with different technique or altering the marker would not be appropriate responses to a genuine interval finding. Discarding the image would remove clinically important information from the patient's record.
  94. The correct answer is A (To confirm the bile ducts are open and free of retained stones after surgery.): A T-tube cholangiogram outlines the biliary tree through the drainage tube so the radiologist can confirm patency and look for retained common duct stones before the tube is removed. It does not quantify bile output, which is measured by drainage volumes. The gallbladder has already been removed in this scenario. Pancreatic function is not assessed by biliary contrast injection.
  95. The correct answer is B (A slower, steadier rate reduces cardiac motion artifact and improves coronary image quality.): The coronary arteries move rapidly with each beat, and a lower steadier heart rate gives a longer low-motion window, so motion artifact falls and the vessels are sharper. Heart rate clearly affects image quality, so downplaying its effect is wrong. Dose is influenced more by scan mode than by rate alone. A fast heart rate does not harm the detectors.
  96. The correct answer is B (The patient achieved an adequate deep inspiration for the exposure.): Visualization of ten posterior ribs above the diaphragm with sharp costophrenic angles and clear lung markings on a PA chest radiograph indicates the patient achieved an adequate deep inspiration for the exposure. This appearance is inconsistent with poor breath holding, an expiratory exposure, or an abnormally elevated diaphragm from pathology. It reflects proper technique and patient cooperation.
  97. The correct answer is B (To reveal subtle strictures or narrowing that liquid barium alone might miss.): A solid bolus is held up where the esophageal lumen is narrowed, so it can reveal mild strictures or rings that flow easily past liquid barium. The bolus does not shorten fluoroscopy and dose is not the reason for its use. Thicker material does not prevent aspiration and in fact carries more risk. It does not replace stomach imaging, which is a separate part of the examination.
  98. The correct answer is A (About forty to forty-five degrees of knee flexion with the beam angled axially.): The Merchant method places the knees at about forty to forty-five degrees of flexion with the beam angled caudad so the patellofemoral joint and sulcus are shown in profile. Full extension relaxes the patella and loses the sulcus view. A deep flexion such as one hundred thirty-five degrees is used in other tangential methods. Ninety degrees standing is not the Merchant position.
  99.   100. Increasing exposure factors would not clarify whether the density represents a true foreign body. Repositioning the marker is not warranted since the marker placement itself was correct.
  101. The correct answer is A (The image is overexposed, because excess receptor exposure has reduced contrast resolution overall.): Excess radiation reaching the detector makes the image too dark, so contrast resolution is reduced and fine cortical and trabecular detail is lost. Grid cutoff would show a lighter band at one edge rather than a uniformly dark knee. Motion would blur edges instead of darkening the whole image. Rotation would change the alignment of the joint without altering overall brightness.
  102. The correct answer is C (Tell the radiologist about the prior reaction so the plan and any premedication can be reviewed.): A previous reaction to iodinated contrast is a strong predictor of another, so the radiologist must be informed and decides on premedication or a different agent. Proceeding as if nothing happened ignores the main risk factor. Changing the rate or the agent without consultation is outside the technologist's role and may not reduce the risk, because reactions differ in type and severity.
  103. The correct answer is D (Confirm the result with the ordering physician and radiologist, because impaired renal function raises nephropathy risk.): A raised creatinine suggests reduced kidney function, which increases the chance of contrast-associated kidney injury, so the result is confirmed with the physician and radiologist who decide how to proceed. Creatinine clearly bears on contrast safety. Lowering only the rate does not change the total contrast load. Barium sulfate is not an intravenous contrast agent and cannot be substituted for CT angiographic enhancement.
  104. The correct answer is D (Hold the barium and notify the radiologist because perforation is suspected.): Barium leaking into the peritoneal cavity can cause severe peritonitis and adhesions, so when perforation is suspected the barium is withheld and the radiologist is told at once. A smaller volume still risks contamination. A physician's order does not override new findings. Changing agents without informing the radiologist is outside the technologist's role and would delay proper assessment.
  105. The correct answer is A (The wrist is rotated about its long axis, distorting bone relationships.): Rotation of the wrist about its long axis changes how the radius, ulna, and carpal bones overlap, so the expected relationships are distorted. Underexposure would make the image light and grainy overall rather than change bone relationships. Motion would blur the cortical edges.   106. The correct answer is D (Confirm with the radiologist whether metformin should be held, given lactic acidosis risk with renal impairment.): Metformin is cleared by the kidneys, and contrast-related kidney injury could let it accumulate and cause lactic acidosis, so the radiologist decides whether it should be held after contrast. Saying no step is needed ignores the renal risk. Doubling the volume only adds contrast load. Taking more metformin before the scan increases the very accumulation that creates the danger.
  107. The correct answer is A (Confer with the radiologist about necessity and use shielding and technique changes if it proceeds.): Under ALARA the examination is justified with the radiologist and referring physician before it proceeds, and if it does, shielding and technique adjustments keep the fetal dose as low as reasonably achievable. Performing the series unchanged ignores the pregnancy, cancelling alone bypasses the physicians who decide justification, and adding projections raises fetal dose without need.
  108. The correct answer is A (The image is underexposed, with too little receptor exposure causing excessive quantum noise.): Too little radiation reaching the detector gives a light, grainy image because too few photons form the picture, which is quantum noise and may hide fine bone detail. Overexposure would produce a darker image rather than a grainy one. A foreign body would appear as a discrete dense object. Grid cutoff would show a band of decreased density at one edge instead of overall noise.
  109. The correct answer is B (Stop the barium, keep the patient upright, and alert the radiologist.): Coughing while swallowing may mean barium is entering the airway, so the contrast is stopped, the patient is kept upright, and the radiologist is told so aspiration can be assessed. Continuing more slowly still risks aspiration. Lying the patient flat makes aspiration more likely. Thicker barium is harder to swallow and can cause greater harm if it reaches the lungs.
  110. The correct answer is A (Use pulsed fluoroscopy, last-image-hold, and the lowest frame rate that keeps diagnostic quality.): Pulsed fluoroscopy, last-image-hold, and the lowest acceptable frame rate all reduce the number of exposures and the dose, which matters especially in children. Continuous fluoroscopy delivers far more exposure for the same time. Source-to-skin distance should be set before the study starts, not midway. Automatic exposure control alone does not lower dose without correct pulse and frame rate choices.
  111. The correct answer is C (A soft tissue mass at the distal lower leg, apart from both bones.): The finding toward the distal end is a soft tissue mass that does not involve the bone. A fracture would show a lucent line in the cortex, motion would blur all margins, and a foreign body would be a dense linear object.
  112. The correct answer is A (Patient movement has blurred the femoral condyles and patellar outline.): Movement during the exposure smears the bone edges, so the femoral condyles and patellar outline look blurred. Uneven superimposition of the condyles would indicate rotation, which would not blur the edges. Excess radiation would darken the entire image rather than soften the margins. A dense object would appear as a distinct opaque shape and would not blur the bones themselves.
  113. The correct answer is C (Grid cutoff, shown by a lighter band across the chest from tube-grid misalignment.): Grid cutoff occurs when the tube and grid are misaligned, so primary rays are absorbed and a lighter band of reduced density appears across the image. Motion would double the diaphragm edges and blur vessels rather than lighten a band. Rotation would show up as clavicle asymmetry. Underexposure would make the entire image grainy and light rather than leave a localized band.

Billing is not the main aim of critique. Entering the right examination code is a data-entry task, not an image-quality review. Checking a physician's spelling on the requisition is clerical and has no effect on whether the image is diagnostic.

  116.   117. The correct answer is A (Grid cutoff, seen as lighter streaks across the humerus from an off-centre tube alignment.): Grid cutoff happens when the beam is misaligned with the grid, so some primary photons are absorbed and lighter streaks or bands of reduced density appear across the image. Motion blur would soften the cortical edges instead of lightening areas. Overexposure would darken the whole image. Poor collimation adds scatter fog, which lowers contrast but does not produce lighter bands.
  118. The correct answer is A (The image is underexposed, with too little receptor exposure causing excessive quantum noise.): Too few photons reaching the detector make the image light and grainy with quantum noise, which can hide the fine bone detail needed for the knee. Grid cutoff would give uneven density across the field rather than overall graininess.
  119. The correct answer is B (Insufficient milliampere-seconds, so too few photons reach the image receptor.): Quantum mottle is the grainy look produced when too few x-ray photons reach the receptor, and the usual technical cause is too little mAs. Raising the mAs adds photons and smooths the image within ALARA and the exposure indicator range. Patient motion causes blur, not graininess, so it does not explain the mottle. A matched grid ratio and correct collimation do not starve the receptor of photons, so they are not the source of the grain.
  120. The correct answer is B (A fracture line crossing the femoral cortex in the upper right field.): The finding is a break in the cortex, which is a fracture line. Swelling with intact bone, a general density problem, and a metallic foreign body would each look different from a cortical break.
  121. The correct answer is A (No side marker is visible, so the exposure must be repeated with a correct marker.): An image without a visible anatomical side marker cannot be reliably identified as right or left, and laterality cannot be added digitally, so the exposure must be repeated with a correct marker. A marker that overlaps only the edge without covering anatomy is acceptable. Subtle rotation that does not affect interpretation does not require action. A mildly off exposure indicator does not prevent diagnosis.
  122. The correct answer is C (An off-level grid and tube alignment causing grid cutoff across the image.): A symmetric gradient of density from one edge to the other is the typical appearance of grid cutoff from off-level tube or grid alignment, because the grid absorbs primary rays unevenly. Motion produces blurred edges rather than a density gradient. Excessive exposure darkens the whole image evenly. Correct centring of the central ray is proper technique and would give even density.
  123. The correct answer is B (The entire structure of clinical interest must be included, with the joints or margins required.): Anatomical coverage means the whole structure of clinical interest, with the required joints or margins, is included on the image, otherwise important findings could be missed. The exposure indicator reflects receptor exposure, not coverage. An identification band in the field is not a coverage criterion and may cause an artifact. Recording the examination time on the requisition is clerical and unrelated to coverage.
  124. The correct answer is C (A fixed, repeatable linear or geometric pattern that appears in the same place across unrelated exposures.): A defect that repeats in the same location across unrelated exposures follows the receptor rather than the patient, which points to a dirty, damaged, or faulty detector. A blur that disappears on the repeat suggests motion. A density change matching clothing or jewelry follows the patient. A normal exposure indicator with no visible defect gives no evidence of any receptor artifact.
  125. The correct answer is C (Document the observation and promptly tell the interpreting physician per critical-findings protocol.): Technologists do not diagnose, but they are responsible for documenting an unexpected significant finding and passing it quickly to the interpreting physician under the department protocol. A private log leaves the chart and the physician uninformed. Telling the patient a suspected diagnosis exceeds the technologist's role and may cause harm. Repeating the study with no communication adds dose and assumes an error without evidence.
  126. The correct answer is A (Unplanned angulation of the central ray or of the part relative to the receptor.): Foreshortening happens when the part or the central ray is angled relative to the receptor, so the part is projected shorter than its true length. Underexposure changes brightness and noise, not shape. Focal spot size affects sharpness of detail rather than length. A perpendicular central ray with the part parallel to the receptor is the correct setup that avoids foreshortening, so it cannot be the cause.
  127. The correct answer is D (The patient's thorax was rotated relative to the image receptor during the exposure.): On a well-positioned PA chest the medial clavicular ends sit equally far from the spinous processes, so unequal distances show the thorax was rotated. Rotation shifts the heart and mediastinum and can mimic disease, so the positioning should be corrected and the image repeated. Straight positioning would produce symmetry, the opposite of what is described. Depth of inspiration is judged by counting posterior ribs, and source-to-image distance changes magnification, so neither explains side to side clavicle asymmetry.
  128. The correct answer is C (It estimates the radiation reaching the image receptor and helps flag overexposure or underexposure.): The exposure indicator is a vendor specific value reflecting the amount of radiation that reached the detector, which lets the technologist spot overexposure or underexposure. Together with the deviation index it supports the ALARA habit of preventing gradual upward drift in technique. It says nothing about the side marker, which must be visible on the image itself. Identification is checked against the requisition by the technologist, and no indicator can remove motion blur.
  129. The correct answer is D (Excessive exposure factors that overexposed the receptor and reduced the visible contrast between structures.): Excessive exposure factors overexpose the receptor, which darkens the image, flattens contrast and hides fine trabecular detail. Because positioning and grid ratio were already appropriate, the exposure choice is the most likely cause. A short source-to-image distance would enlarge and blur the part rather than flatten contrast, and a low grid ratio would permit more scatter only if the part were thick. Tight collimation reduces scatter and improves contrast, so it cannot explain this appearance.
  130. The correct answer is C (A break in the bone cortex in the lower right forearm, consistent with a fracture.): The finding in the lower right region is a break in the bone cortex, which is the radiographic sign of a fracture and fits the clinical history of a fall. Soft tissue swelling alone would leave the cortex intact, so it does not match a cortical interruption. Motion blur would soften or double edges across the whole bone rather than create a localized break. Grid cutoff produces a band of lost density with straight edges, not a defined cortical defect.
  131. The correct answer is D (A change in part thickness or calibration altered the exposure at the detector.): Noise rises when too few photons reach the detector, and a change in part thickness, swelling, a cast, or detector calibration can alter the real exposure even when the displayed indicator looks similar. This makes a quantitative difference in exposure the most plausible explanation. The indicator is a useful, if imperfect, guide and should not be dismissed. The same room points to the same detector, and noise is directly tied to receptor exposure.
  132. The correct answer is A (A medial soft tissue mass bulging beside the humerus without involving the bone itself.): The medial finding is a mass in the soft tissue with intact bone. A fracture would show a cortical break, grid cutoff would lighten a band without a defined shape, and rotation would alter bony outlines rather than add a bulge.
  133.   200. Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Confirm patient identification and markers, then positioning, then exposure, then evidence of pathology): Critique starts with identification and markers because an image of the wrong patient or without laterality cannot be used however good it looks. Positioning comes next because it decides whether the right anatomy is shown, and exposure is judged on a correctly positioned part. Pathology comes last since the technologist judges technical adequacy, not diagnosis. The other sequences put technical detail ahead of the checks that can make an image unusable.
  2. The correct answer is C (Correlate with the history and notify the radiologist of a likely retained foreign body.): A small dense object over the soft tissue after a workshop accident, with a wound that raised suspicion of an embedded object, fits a retained foreign body. The technologist checks the history and tells the radiologist, who decides on the next imaging or surgical step. Treating it as a marker artifact and deleting it would falsify the record and could hide an embedded fragment. A lower kilovoltage would not remove a real dense object, and ignoring it because it is not over the shaft misses the very reason a wound was noted.
  3. The correct answer is C (Whether a hair braid, part, or external object could be mimicking a fracture line.): A thin lucent line that was not on the prior examination could be a real fracture or something external lying in the beam, so the technologist first considers whether a hair braid, a part line or another object could be mimicking a fracture. External objects over the skull often produce linear shadows that cross bone in a way no break would. A drop in bone density over a short interval would not create a single line. A low kilovoltage would affect the whole image, and a shorter source-to-image distance changes magnification, so neither explains a new linear lucency.
  4. The correct answer is A (A nutrient canal has smooth corticated margins, while an acute fracture edge is sharp and uncorticated): A nutrient canal is a normal channel for blood vessels, so its edges are smooth and corticated and it runs obliquely through the cortex. An acute fracture edge is sharp and has no cortical rim. The canal is not limited to lateral projections or the epiphysis, since it is found in the shaft. Anatomy does not move between exposures, so that claim is false.
  5. The correct answer is D (Document the reason for the repeat, explain to the patient, and reposition for a new exposure): Motion blur that affects diagnostic quality is a technical reject, so policy calls for a logged reason, an explanation to the patient and a repositioned repeat. Logging also lets the department track repeat rates. Sending the image unrecorded hides the error. Window and level changes cannot restore sharpness lost to movement, and archiving it accepts a quality failure the policy exists to correct.
  6. The correct answer is D (Flag the altered density for radiologist review rather than repeating or diagnosing it.): An area of altered bone density near the acetabulum that was not on the requisition is a finding the technologist should point out, since interpreting it belongs to the radiologist. A correctly marked, adequately exposed pelvis does not need to be repeated, and the finding is not a technical fault. Raising mAs adds dose without a technical reason, and calling the change motion mistakes a real bone finding for blur. Cropping the area out of the image removes the exact information that could matter in an older patient with chronic hip pain.
  7. The correct answer is B (Static electricity discharge from low humidity, prevented by careful plate handling and humidity control): Fine, branching, tree-like dark lines on a dry day are the signature of static discharge, which happens when low humidity lets charge build up on the plate. The remedy is careful handling and humidity control. A residual latent image would ghost earlier anatomy rather than draw branching lines. Grid cutoff gives graded density loss, and quantum mottle is a speckled grain from too few photons.
  8. The correct answer is D (Repeat the exposure with the correct physical marker placed within the collimated field): A missing physical marker means laterality was not recorded at exposure, and policy forbids adding it afterward, so the exposure is repeated with a correct marker in the field. A digital marker would record laterality that was never verified. A verbal note leaves the image itself unidentified. The chest is not midline in the sense that removes laterality, because the heart and lungs differ from side to side.
  9. The correct answer is A (Reposition and repeat the exposure using immobilization aids to reduce blur from movement.): Blurred cortical margins in the mid-shaft with sharper joints show that the part moved during the exposure, and a possible fracture cannot be assessed through blurred cortex. Repeating with immobilization aids such as a sandbag, a radiolucent splint or a parent-assisted hold removes the cause, and a shorter exposure time also helps in a child. Accepting the image because the wrist and elbow look adequate ignores that the suspected injury site is the blurred region. A longer source-to-image distance does not remove motion blur, and a grid affects scatter and contrast, not movement unsharpness.
  10. The correct answer is B (Immediately notify the radiologist and referring team of a possible pneumothorax for urgent review.): A line near the lung apex with no lung markings beyond it after a central line placement is the appearance of a pneumothorax, a complication that can worsen quickly and needs immediate communication to the radiologist and the referring team. The correct answer is B (The image may show increased quantum mottle even though overall brightness looks adequate): An exposure index well below target means less radiation reached the receptor than intended, and digital processing can rescale brightness so the image looks fine while quantum mottle hides in it. That is why the index is checked along with visual appearance. Calling it unrelated ignores that it is the objective signal measure. A grid ratio problem would give cutoff, and a failed chamber is not proven by the index alone.
  74. The correct answer is D (Whether the lucency corresponds to a normal open growth plate for the patient's age.): A smooth, symmetric lucent line at the distal radius in a child corresponds to a normal open growth plate, so before calling a fracture the technologist considers whether the line matches the expected growth plate for the patient's age. This avoids a false report and an unnecessary repeat. Whether the lucency shifted between exposures is not a good first test, since a growth plate does not move. Exposure index and grid ratio relate to technique and do not decide whether a line is anatomical.
  75. The correct answer is A (It ensures accurate identification of air-fluid levels and organ position across views.): Markers must match the patient's real right and left on each image because supine and erect abdominal views are compared to judge air-fluid levels and organ position, and a wrong side could misplace a finding. The marker has no effect on dose, so it does not reduce exposure. It does not remove the need to document position changes, and automatic exposure control does not read markers to choose a technique.
  76. The correct answer is A (The central ray was angled rather than perpendicular to the part and the receptor): A narrowed joint space with foreshortened adjacent bone, despite a correctly placed receptor, points to an angled central ray. When the beam is not perpendicular, the joint is no longer shown open and the bone is shortened by projection. A longer distance changes magnification slightly without tilting the geometry. Kilovoltage affects contrast and penetration, and grid ratio affects scatter cleanup.
  77. The correct answer is B (Correlate with the injury history and notify the radiologist of a retained foreign body.): A small, sharp dense fragment in soft tissue that matches no bone after an industrial accident fits a retained foreign body, and the technologist links it to the history and informs the radiologist. Calling it a phlebolith assumes a benign cause without considering the mechanism of injury. A lower kilovoltage does not make a real dense object disappear. Cropping the fragment from the displayed image would remove clinically relevant evidence from the record.
  78. The correct answer is C (Whether the thoracic vertebrae are faintly visible through the cardiac silhouette without excessive noise.): With no exposure index, a useful visual clue on a chest image is faint visibility of the thoracic vertebrae through the cardiac shadow without excessive noise, which shows adequate penetration and receptor dose. Body habitus compared to a chart does not show how the image came out. Collimation borders and marker legibility are checks of other qualities of the image and tell nothing about exposure.
  79. The correct answer is C (Repeat the exposure with the correct physical marker, since this error cannot be fixed after acquisition): A wrong-side marker is a laterality error that cannot be corrected after acquisition, so the exposure is repeated with a correct physical marker. Relabelling digitally would record something unverified. Chest anatomy is not symmetric in ways that matter, since the heart and lung lobes differ, and a wrong marker could cause a wrong-side finding. A note leaves the incorrect marker on the permanent image.
  80. The correct answer is B (Compare with prior positioning notes and, if uncertain, repeat with attention to skin fold elimination.): A linear line that could be a skin fold or a true pleural line cannot be settled by guessing, so the technologist checks the positioning notes and, if still uncertain, repeats with attention to smoothing skin folds. That gives the radiologist a clear answer rather than an ambiguous image. Declaring the line artifact without checking could hide a pneumothorax, and declaring it pathological without checking could cause needless alarm. Changing the window and level until the line fades alters the display and not the underlying doubt.
  81. The correct answer is C (Correlate with the history and notify the radiologist of a probable glass foreign body.): A small, well-defined dense fragment near the carpus after a broken glass laceration fits a retained glass foreign body, since glass is often radiopaque. The technologist relates it to the history and notifies the radiologist. Calling it an accessory carpal bone assumes a normal variant without regard to the injury. A grid improves scatter control and will not remove a real fragment, and deleting it with editing software would falsify the image.
  82. The correct answer is D (Flag the interval change for radiologist review rather than assuming a technical artifact.): New increased density in matching position and technique is a real interval change until proven otherwise, so the technologist flags it for radiologist review. Blaming processing differences assumes an artifact when technique was equal. Repeating with different factors adds dose and changes the comparison. Ignoring the density because the prior image lacked it misses the very point of a follow-up study.
  83. The correct answer is C (Lower kilovoltage generally produces higher contrast with fewer distinguishable grey shades in the image): At lower kilovoltage, attenuation depends more on tissue differences, so contrast is higher and fewer grey shades are distinguishable, which matches this comparison. The reverse statement about lower contrast and more shades describes high kilovoltage. Kilovoltage clearly affects contrast in digital images. Dose is not reduced simply by lowering kilovoltage, since the other factors change as well.
  84.   85. The correct answer is A (To document how long the patient remained in a given position before exposure.): A time marker on a series such as decubitus abdominal studies documents how long the patient stayed in the position before the exposure, because fluid and gas need time to move. It has no role in identifying the side. It does not record exposure factors, which are recorded in other fields, and it never takes the place of an anatomical side marker, which stays necessary.
  86. The correct answer is D (Scatter radiation reaching the receptor without grid attenuation, reducing image contrast.): A grey haze with reduced contrast on a thick body part imaged without a grid is explained by scatter radiation reaching the receptor with no grid to remove it. Thicker tissue produces more scatter, which fogs the image even when the exposure index is acceptable. Underexposure would reduce signal and cause mottle, not haze. Grid cutoff cannot occur when no grid is used, and static artifact shows as branching lines, not a general haze.
  87. The correct answer is B (Investigate the equipment in that room for a mechanical or calibration problem): An artifact that appears in one room across technologists and patients points to something in that room's equipment, so the response is to investigate for a mechanical or calibration problem. Repeating every examination adds dose without finding the cause. Treating the pattern as coincidence lets the fault continue, and assigning experienced staff does not repair equipment.
  88. The depression separates it from soft tissue summation, which has no cortical step. Grid cutoff gives a broad density falloff, and a nutrient canal does not depress the articular margin.
  89.   90. The index can look acceptable because it averages the whole image. A control failure would affect the whole image, scatter from a low ratio would be general, and high kilovoltage would not limit mottle to one region.
  91. The correct answer is C (The deviation index, since it directly compares the actual exposure with the target for that technique): The deviation index compares the actual exposure with the target for that technique, so it speaks directly to adequacy, and a markedly negative value with visible noise indicates underexposure that may justify a repeat. The exposure index alone does not show how far the exposure fell from what was intended. Trusting only visual impression discards objective data, and console milliampere-seconds does not show what reached the receptor.
  92. Normal dorsal tilt is a gentle, smooth angle of the articular surface and not a cortical disruption. That is why a second view is requested to test whether a suspected mass is real. Real pathology usually remains on more than one projection. Summation artifacts change with angle, and a second view can clarify the first.

  105. This is part of professional practice standards and keeps the radiologist from reading a flawed study. Releasing any first-attempt image to avoid a repeat puts dose avoidance ahead of quality, and routinely taking two exposures adds unjustified dose.
